The Examiner Saturday, November 7, 1903.

FASHIONS OF TO-DAY.

There is no doubt that skirts are no longet made plain and perfectly tight fitting, for all the newest models for early winter wear show more or less elaboration in the cut and fashion, ing of the skirt. One might, indeed, almost go so far as to say that there exists at present in the realms of dress no such thing as a plain skirt. To many of us this hardly can be con- sidered as news to be welcomed when we ruefully view last season's tailor-made which we quite reasonably expected to act as a secondary costume throughout the best part of winter. Let us not, however, abandon hope and condemn the dress to banishment from our wardrobes or even resign ourselves to the still more unwelcome fate of wearing the dress as it is for there are many ways in which a clever fingered girl can alone, or with

;.,.. ~........ , • i . i : ; ' i / /7 / , • 4 . / I\ s' II

===== 0002601_19031107_art0015.txt | included=true | included =====
A RENOVATED SKIRT.

the aid of a dressmakei, so alter and improve matters as to render the costume almost un- recognisable. A very simple plan is to open the seams as far as the knees and insert fan shaped pleatings of silk or even velvet, the kiltings being, of course, introduced from underneath and becoming visible when walk- ing, while a large silk or velvet covered button gives a finish to the commencement of the Twist or loops of cord with tassels are sometimes used instead of the buttons. An example of this kind of skirt is given in my first sketch. Should the skirt have been originally of the trailing order I ghould counsel my r9itders to curtail it before making any alterations and to cut it sufficiently short to clear the ground thoroughly all round. To achieve this the skirt should measure the same from the waist to the hem all the way round. Neatness and care are essential when insert- ing the pleats, but the lask 3-.) an easy one for even the home worker, and the result is an altogether smart and • fashionable • skirt in the place of one that stamped itself &mode. Another method of altering a skirt, but one that calls for a greater amount of skill on the part of the worker, is to cut the skirt right down the centre of the front and insert a panel front which can be either made of the same material, if that is obtainable, or of silk or

d Ntssup,4lloo , %My V • „ 0 I • i e , s 1 4

===== 0002601_19031107_art0016.txt | included=true | included =====
A COMFORTABLE COATJ

embroidery or even velvet, though the latter, to my mind, is apt to look too patchy and matronlike. The panel must be cut narrower at the top as it should widen towards the lower edge. The edges of the skirt are made to overlap the panel and can be finished in a variety of ways, such as stitched bandings, cord, braid, or pipings. The extra fulness must be brought back to the hips where it is tucked or gauged or else brought to the centre of the back and formed into a box pleat. The coat depicted is of a most comfortable description, being of fawn box cloth lined with silk and boasting a collar of fur which can be worn up in the manner of a storm collar or lying.flat upon the shoulders. Sac backs for coats are perhaps not so general this season as last, for there is a marked tendency towards tight fitting garments of all kinds, but the looser style of .coat has such undoubted ad- vantages that it ca v safely be selected as a welcome item of one's outfit. It allows of the wearing of dressy blouses or bodices under- neath which would be ruined by the tight clinging coat. Some of the new coats display fur collars of a rather novel cut. These are very broad, fastening close round the throat and closed up in front, the points coming Nkrell down to the centre of the figure and extending

A PRETTY HAT. across the sho ulde,rs, finally dipping efols - a tippet at the back. Another style is cu a rounded cape all in one piece falling away over the shoulders and sloping away from the front up to the 'throat where it is finished by a bow or chou.

The hat illustrated is of a style much in vogue at present. The hat itself is of felt trimmed with a large bird laid flat on the crown with wings outstretched. The colour of the hat is grey wi.th a white bird, while 'clusters of parma violets conceal the bandeau at the back. Bright red velvet geraniums with their leaves might be substituted for the violets, or even shaded autumn leaves would look exceedingly chic. There are some very smart toques which for country, golfing, or cycling wear must meet with universal appro- bation. These somewhat resemble a Scotch bonnet and as it were to emphasise the resemblance are expressed in plaid or tartan velvet. For afternoon hats of picturesque aspect the ostrich feather is the trimming spar excellence. The best specimens are wide and bushy and are only just Slightly curled at the tips and measure quite twenty inches. These are not only worn in black, white, or the natural colour, but also in bright tints and are sometimes shaded from the palest to the deepest tone. One has nowadays to dress the coiffure to suit the hat, and the most popular is undoubtedly on the top of the head with the front hair pulled well forward and brushed close to the head at the back,

Two courses had been finished, and still the bash- ful, youth, had, not said a word, Suddenly the young lady, spoke. "I'll tell you what let's do," she said; "let's talk." The young man's com- plexion uttered several bright things in reply, but he spoke not a word.

She was a pretty young thing fresh from a fashionable "finishing. school." She was bidding the "happiest man on earth "a fond adieu in the porch of the old country house. "Now, dearest, just before .1 go," he said, commencing all over again, "what kind of a ring shall it be?" "Oh, Jack !" she answered, rapturously, "do get me a solitaire with one diamond in it." JOINED TOGETHER. - Teacher : "Thomas, will you tell me what a conjunction is, and compose a sentence containing a conjunction?" Thomas (after long and solemn reflection): "A conjunction is a word connecting anything, such as, The horse is hitched to the fence by his halter.'. Halter is a conjunction, because it connects the horse and the fence !"

HER HOME Mina BREAD. Young Man: "Doctor, you have been attending me for a week, and I am worse than I was at the start." Physician: "I will be frank with you, sir. Being unable to discoAr what was the matter with you, and being unwilling to risk interfering with the curative power of nature, I have given you no medicine at all. In fact, my treatment has not commenced yet." "But you have given me pills right along. "They were only a sham. They were made or bread." f‘ Where did you get the bread?" "Your young and charming wile made it." "No wonder I'm worse." A SPADE NOT A SHOVEL.—Sir Frank Lockwood used to tell a story which happened in his own -ex- perience, as showing the dulness of certain men who are on the magisterial bench. A man had stolen a spade, and was tried before a well-meaning 'and conscientious magistrate. His worship care- fully looked up Archibald's "Criminal Law," to find a precedent (Ai which he could convict and punish the man. "I can't find anything under the word ' spade,' " he said at length, "although I see that a man was convicted and severely pun- ished for stealing a shovel." Then, looking at the culprit severely over his spectacles, he added, "You have had a very nartow escape, but you may go !" SEEING AND KNoWING.—An eminent judge, who was trying a right-of-way case, had before him a witness—an old farmer—who was telling the jury that he had " knowen the path for 60 yeer, and my feyther tould I as he heerd my grandfeyther say--" "Stop !" said the judge; "we can't have any hearsay evidence here." "Not," ex- claimed Farmer Giles. "Then how dost knaw who thy feyther was 'cept by hearsay." Judge : In courts of law we can only be guided with what you have seen with your own eyes, and nothing more or less. " On, that be blowed fora tale," re- plied the farmer. ".1 ha' a bile on the back of my neck, and I never sied un, but I be prepared to swear that he's there, I do " This second triumph on the part of the witness let in a torrent of hear- say evidence about the footpath which obtained weight with the jury, albeit the judge told them it was not testimony of any value, and the farmer's party won.

SODA WATER BOTTLE IN HIS STOMACH.

A remarkable operation has just been performed in a hospital at Ruabon. A sailor of the Morayshire re- cently presented himself at the institution and calmly informed the medical authorities that he had a soda water bottle in his stomach. Of course, the statement was considered pretty tall'; but the sailor, persisting in his assertiou, induced the doctors to " investigate." The operation was duly performed, and great was the astonishment when the soda water bottle was discovered in his intestines. The sailor was reported to be doing very well when this information was dispatched.

===== 0002601_19031107_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON MUNICIPAL ELECTIONS.

; SURPRISING RESULTS.

Mr. Magion's Heavy Majority.

The Warriagton Municipal elections on Monday yielded some surprising results which probably few townspeople would have ventured to prophesy. In striking contrast to the fights last year, when the Non-Traders achieved memorable triumphs, only one of the official nominees of the Non-Trading Com- mittee (Mr. T. H. Ashcroft) was returned at the head of the poll. The most remarkable change of feeling seems to have 6wept over St. John's Ward, where Mr. john Maginn, who only a few months ago was defeated by Mr. Henry Thornton by ' a substantial majority, came out victorious with 041 votes more than his opponent, Mr. John Ashley. There was a keen fight in Fairfield Ward, but Mr. T. J. Hulse retains his seat .by a margin of 38. Great interest centred in the 'three-cornered contest in Orford Ward where Mr. Fred Stott made a plucky effort in the interests of the Independent Labour Party. Mr. Arthur Peacock, the retiring representative, worked hard and had the satisfaction of winning a well-fought battle by 82. Mr. W. J. Shari', who came forward as the official Non-Trading candidate, was at the bottom of the poll with only 108 votes. It would be difficult to imagine a more unfavourable day for electioneering than Monday proved to be, rain falling almost incessantly from the opening until the close of the poll. The candidates were, of course, actively engaged in bringing voters to the booths, and conveyances were placed at their disposal. The pre- siding officers and poll clerks were as follows ORFORD WARD.—Polling booth, Silver-street school. Silver-street • presiding officers, Messrs. S. Wallhead and T. S. Steel ; poll clerks, Messrs. J. W. Howarth and W. Gleave.

ST. JOHN'S WARD.—Polling booth, Old Club-room, John-street; presiding officers, Messrs. E. Bradshaw, B. Shearer and W. S. Howard; poll clerks, Messrs. J. W. Bullen, H. P. Brierley, and T. G. Grounds. FSIRFIELD WARD.—Polling booth, Fairfield Infants' school, off Manchester-road ; presiding officers, Messrs. J. Honghton, E. England, :and E. A. Connor; poll clerks, Messrs. E. Hague, E. A. Sanders and J. Smith. HOWLEY WARD.—Polling booth, National School, Church-street; presiding officers, Messrs. A. Fox and T.H. Spinks ; poll clerks, Messrs. E. J. Smith and J. Lee, jun. ST. AUSTIN'S WARD.—Polling booths, Cairo-street infants' school and Thewlis-street infants' school ; pre- siding officers, Messrs. F. Timperley W. H. J. Jenkins, and F. G. Xaylor •' poll clerks, Messrs. T. P. Bradshaw T. J. Hopkins and W. T. Crank. LATCHFORD WARD.—Polling booths, St. James's school, Old-road, and Christ Church school, Wash-lane; presiding offices, St. James's school, Messrs. . A fair quantity of election literature was submitted for the perusal of the electors. The much debated inquiry question was, of course, kept to the fore.posters announcing Mr. H. Roberts's offer of £250 towards the expenses of a shorthand report, being conspicuous on the hoardings throughout the town. • There was also an address to the ratepayers by Mr. Roberts,in the course of which it was stated"to tell you that the' inquiry 'will cost from £5,000 to £B,OOO if you have the proceedings properly reported and printed is an outrageous exaggeration. The Comntissioners have already sat for four days, and the bill sent in for reporting and printing the proceedings has not been more than £6 per day. If the Non-traders will find £250 (thus providing with £2501 have offered, a sum of £500) the total cost of reporting and printing the pro- ceedings of an inquiry lasting nearly three months (which is surely ample time for the purpose) will be furnished. If the Non-traders really desire the inquiry to beheld, let them admit the press to it, and the pro- ceedings will then be reported without costing the town a penny." As a counter-blast the Non-traders issued a handbill pointing out that on September 24th there was only a short sitting of the Commissioners, and the following was the bill :—Attending and taking notes, £1 is.; transcribing 152 folios at Bd. per folio, £5 Is. 4d.; printing 152 folios at is. per folio, £7 12s. ; total, £l3 14s. 4d. This sitting, continued the ,circular, did not extend over half the time arranged, therefore the ratepayers can form an idea as to whether we have "outrageously exaggerated" (as stated by Mr. Henry Roberts) by say- ing that the inquiry would cost from £5,000 to £3,000. The inquiry will last 12 months. It will cost from £2O to £25 for a full day's sitting, and therefore to offer £250 towards the cost is bluffing of the most barefaced character. The way to cheapen the inquiry is to elect Non-traders. 0 In Orford Ward Mr. Peacock circulated an illustrated hand bill giving particulars of a scheme for the provi- sion of a recreation ground between Orford-lane and Winwick-road ; Nahilst Mr. Stott called attention to the remarks of Councillor Dodds at a Parr Hall meeting in which he favoured Mr. Stott's candidature. Coun- cillor Dodds came to the support of Mr. Ashley in St. John's Ward with a circular stating that although he (Councillor Dodds) was a Liberal he desired without any hesitation whatever to solicit the votes and sym- pathy of the electors on behalf of Mr. Ashley who, although a Conservative, was thoroughly strong upon the trading question and.rnight be relied upon to loyally stand by and support the purity party in the Council. Mr. Ashcroft in St. Austin's Ward, issued a similar appeal signed by Councillor Dodds. DECLARATION OF THE POLL.

A great crowd assembled on the lawn in front of the Town Hall to hear the declaration of the poll. The figures were announced by the returning officers of the respective wards at intervals between nine o'clock and half-past nine. The names of the successful candidates were received with warm cheers, but Mr. Maginn's success aroused the most enthusiasm. Owing to the loud and prolonged acclaims of the crowd when it became known that he was at the head of the poll, some little time elapsed before the number of votes recorded could be annonnced. WOMEN'S CHAT.

It was thought that the King would this year give a ball at Sandringham on the occasion of His Majesty's birthday. There has not been a ball at Sandringham since the death of the Duke of Clarence, but formerly always two dances were given at the Hall during the winter, one for "the county" and the other for the servants on the Royal and neighbouring estates. Amongst the county families near to Sandringham are Lord and Lady Farquhar at Castle Rising, Lord and. Lady Leicester at Holkham, Lord and Lady Cholmond- ley at Houghton Lord and Lady Sheffield at Gunton, and Lord and Lady Walsingkam at Merton. The mem- bers of tile county" have generally received invitations to the play "Monsieur Beaucaire." which M. Lewis Waller has been commanded to present at Sandringham on‘his Majesty's birthday. The Lady Mayoress.—Sir James Ritchie, the new Lord Mayor of London, is a widower, and the position of Lady Mayoress will belong to his eldest daughter. The position carries certain privileges, though not the right which is said to belong by ancient custom to the Lady Mayoress of York, to retain the prefix of Lady before her surname for the remainder of her life. As the Lord Mayors invariably have, or retire from office with a title, it does not much matter, but there is an ancient rhyme referring to York which says The Mayor is a Lord for a year and a day, But his wife is a Lady for ever and aye. Mr. Ritchie has seven daughters, all very accomplished in music and art, and only one of whom is married. The duties of hostess at the Mansion House are no light matter, when in a single pair of hands,and Miss Ritchie will no doubt be glad of the help .of her sisters. . .

The Mansion Huse.—The faniily life at the Mansion House is ordinarily of a very quiet kind, but the splendour of some of the Aldermanic banquets at the official residence of the Chief Magistrate of the city of London, would suggest the resources of a Royal Palace. On these occasions, a large staff of servants is brought in, the regular staff consisting of a chef, and two cooks, with about 20 or 24 servants. The kitchen arrangements are of the most ample kind, and at one of the ranges which is 16 feet long, a baron of beef, weighing from 150 to 180 lbs, can easily be roasted. There are several steam steel tables and closets for keeping warm plates and dishes, and it is no uncommon thing for as many as five or six thousand plates to be in use, on the occasion of a great banquet. The Dinner Hour and the Theatres.—The fashionable dinner hour has been for years steadily getting later and later, and its interference with going to the play is once more the subject of discussion in theatrical circles. But people who usually sit down to dinner at times varying from eight to nine o'clock, do not always want to go to the theatre, and when they do, they can easily arrange for a light meal beforehand—and supper later on. Mr. Pinero would like to see Society return _

to the "high tea" system, but he is scarcely likely to persuade theatre goers to return to the repast of "ham and tannin" which had its rise and fall during the

Victorian reign. "High tea" is still a great inaitu- tion with certain classes, and it is popular in America, as so many Americans are just as ready to drink tea or coffee with their mid-day or eveßing meals, as at break- fast, but it is not likely to be seriously entertained by fashionable hostesses, as a desirable social reform in

this country. Smart Note Paper.—Fashionable stationery just now takes the form of linen surface paper, and some of it is guilt edged. It is made in various art shades and the owner's initials or monogram is usually displayed in medallion form at the top in the centre, or left hand side of the sheet. There are however so many fashions and fancies in stationery now, that only the most fasti- dious, trouble to follow them through their varying changes. For ordinary people it is just as well to adopt a certain style and quality of paper that suits best, and to stick to it. Any other system only fills the escretoire with a bewildering assortment of writing paper and envelopes, of all shapes, sizes,colours and dis- criptions. Frauds in Furs.—The London Chamber of Commerce, in drawing attention to the fact, that furriers have been known Ito mark, advertise and sell furs under a, wrong name, will prevent some of nsat least, from pay- ing for what we do not get. Not infrequently the white rabbit skin has been known to pose as ermine, goat skin as bear ; otter as seal, and hare and mink as sable, while very often American sable is sold as the real Russian article. When buying expensive furs, it is always a wise plan to insist upon a full description of the articles purchased, appearing on the bill, which thus affords one some hold upon the shopkeeper. But a safer plan still is to go to some thoroughly reliable establishment, which has never been known to practice such fraud upon unsuspecting customers. A new Material.—Velvet is .greatly to the fore just

now, not the old variety, which gave to a woman a dignified dowager appearance, but a, new and quite different description, known as "velours chiffon.' In texture this is as rich and lustrous as the most beauti- ful Lyons or Genoa velvet, and so fine and silky is it, that it can be drawn through a ring. Accordion pleated it has the, most exquisite effect, while it possesses the still further attraction of being absolutely uncrush- able. . _

Lace Toilettes.—Entire lace gowns are little seen, an itemnot likely to trouble many among us. Unless built of the best lace, and by an experienced modiste, such a toilette is common looking, and altogether undesirable. The entire lace blouse too has disappeared, probably because we have had too much of it, in very inferior kinds. For evening wear, black is this season, again much worn. Black net trithmed with velvet spots and sequins, and worn over a soft black satin slip, is indeed almost a livery just now. The sequin is by no means extinct. In heavy bosses, padded and raised, or in irregular showers, it is adorning all airy fairy fabrics but never richer materials, such as satins, silks, velvets, or brocades.

A Useful Hint.—A Parisian chemist has discovered a new use for the telephone with which he is able to de- tect the addition of water to wine and spirits. This method is scarcely likely to come into universal use, but there is a way of ascertaining deterioration in an

SPEECHES AT THE LIBERAL CLUB Councillor Peacock's Success.

A large number of Councillor Peacock's workers assembled in the committee room of the club, after the declaration of the poll. Councillor WILKINSON read the result of the poll in Orford Ward, amid great cheering. In commenting upon the success of Councillor Peacock, he said they had all seen that Councillor Peacock had been a hard worker whilst a member of fhe Council. (Hear, hear.) He had always done his level best for the workingman. (Hear, hear.) Whilst a member cf the School Attend- ance Committee, Councillor Peacock had always been willing to listen to any grievances which had been brought forward. (Hear, hear). Three cheers were then raised for Councillor Peacock. The audience also gave three hearty cheers for Mrs. Peacock. Councillor WILKINSON said Mrs. Peacock had worked ' very hard all day on behalf of her husband. It showed that he must be a good husband. (Laughter). - Councillor PEACOCK, who was received with loud cheers,said no one was more pleased with the result than he was. It was said before the election that he would show the white feather—(laughter)—but he never had any intention of doing anything of the kind. (Hear, hear.) Whilst a member of the Council he had never been afraid of speaking his mind and telling Non- traders what he thought about them. (Hear, hear.) 'On several occasions when they had made incorrect statements he had got up and contradicted them. He was a man of straightforwardness and the truth would always go the furthest. (Hear, hear.) As long as he was the representative of Orford Ward he would strive to serve the best interests of the ratepayers. With regard to the recreation ground which he had sug- gested for the ward, that was no new scheme. He had had the matter in hand for three years but it was very difficult to get it carried out. It would be his endeavour to push the matter forward and he felt sure that ere long a recreation ground would be provided in the vvard.l (Hear, hear). Any grievances of working men, whether relating to the Union or anything else, would receive consideration. He was a working man himself and knew their diffidulties—(Hear, hear)—and it would be his endeavour to uphold their rights. (Applause). s Orford ward would always receive his first considera- tion. He was glad to have been able to do something towards its improvement in the past. Many things yet remained to be done and they would have immediate attention. During the election several matters had been pointed out to him as needing atten- tion and he would report them to the committees responsible when he returned to the Council. He did not expect to satisfy everybody nor did he hope to achieve all that was expected of him. But he would endeavour to do his best. (Hear, hear.) He would conclude by thanking all who had worked so admirably for him that day to secure his return. (Hear, hear.) In past elections he had been indebted to many willing workers, but he thought they had excelled them- selves on this occasion. The weather had been against them, but despite all difficulties they had triumphed. He wished especially to thank the lady workers who had assisted his wife in bringing many to the poll. (Cheers). Mr. JAMES CRITCHLEY addressed his sdpporters in anether room. Although he had been defeated, he said he was personally satisfied at the results generally. The poll had been very satisfactory, and from a rate- payer's point of view showed that the non-traders were ' not the party they assumed to be. He was as strong a supporter of the principles of Non-trading as any candi- date that had come forward. But while he had not agreed with the methods of the non-traders, he had not desired to join them for the reason that he was a tradesman in the town. On those lines only had he fought that contest, still he had been beaten and he could only thank his supporters. Whatever candidate they might have in the future he hoped he would have the same support with a much better result. (Applause) Mr. JAMES Woens, of Church street, considered that under the circumstances the candidate had done re- markably well, seeing that Mr. Ashcroft, his opponent was a member of the Board of Guardians for the ward, and being a candidate for municipal honours he had the full strength of his insurance collectors working for him. Mr. Critchley had been at a discount in not hav- inga good staff of workers. Therefore, he considered their candidate had done very well indeed. If Mr. Critchley came forward again he hoped he would be more successful. (Hear, hear).

MR. STOTT AND THE NON-TRADING OPPOSITION. -- Determined to Fight Again. After the declaration of the poll Mr. F. Stott ad- dressed a large gathering from the steps of the Market Hall. He said he was afraid many of his supporters were disappointed at the result of the election. He however, was well satisfied. It was their first experi— ence in election work, the weather had been of a most wretched description, they had no conveyances' and a candidate who was to a great extent a stranger to the electors, while on the other hand their opponent, Mr. Peacock, was well known. He had represented the ward for six years, he had a large number of influential workers, a surfeit of conveyances, and was experienced in election tactics. Therefore there was every reason to be proud of the fact that 357 electors had walked to the poll and registered their votes for a candidate who had been condemned as a "dangerous man." It was true that many of his views were not popular, but he would not hesitate to express them, believing them to be just and right. He was condemned and opposed by the Non-Traders because he was a Socialist. He (the speaker) declared that he was, and had been for a number of years, a convinced Socialist, and he was proud to acknowledge it. (Applause.) He chal- lenged the Non-Traders to publicly debate the question of municipal socialism, believing that instead of the reforms he advocated being dangerous, they would be beneficial to the working classes. The Non-Trading Association had from time to time declared that they would oppose labour candidates, but on the first occasion that a labour candidate had been nominated he had been opposed by that Association. He claimed to be a genuine representative of labour, his candidature having been endorsed by the Trade and Labour Council, the Labour Representation Committee, and many ttade union branches. If he (the speaker) was not a repres- sentative labour candidate, where was the man in War- rington who was? (Applause.) His candid opinion was that the majority of the Non-Trading Association would prefer the return of Councillor Peacock, who had been one of their bitterest opponents, to himself. The Non-Trading Association was being controlled by the Property Owners' Association or a section of it, which explained to a large extent why his candidature had been opposed. It was well known that he was in favour of the Corporation taking steps to remove the insanitary dwellings in the town, and building on the land municipal houses to be let at the lowest pos- sible rent. 'Therefore it could not be expected that the property owners would be in support of his candidature. In his opinion, however, the action of the Non-Trading Association in regard to his candidature in Orford Ward was to a great extent the cause of the non-success of the official Non-Trading candidates. Numerous instances had been brought to his notice where men had refused to any longer give their support to Non-Trading candi- dates owing to their opposing him, and that feeling appeared to be very general, especially among the trade unionists. He thanked all those who had assisted in his candidature, and appealed to them to continue their support for he was determined to contest the forth- coming by-election in the ward. He would contest the ward entirely as the nominee of the Independent Labour Party. If he went to the Town Council he would go only in a straight and honest manner, without using methods for the purpose of getting votes which were alike degrading to a candidate and the electors. He would not say that' Councillor Peacock was not a good Councillor, but it could not be said that he was as interested in the working classes as he (,Fr. Stott) was. He asked them to give him their earnest support. If they did that,there was no doubt he would be returned at the next attempt by such a majority that would show they were determined to have direct labour representa- ayes on the Warrington Town Council. (Applause.)

. Addressing the crowd from the Town Hall steps, Councillor Maginn thanked the returning officers and other officials, and said he had found them to be most courteous, obliging, and straightforward. not only on that occasion, but when he was defeated a short time ago. He had heard it stated that the officials at the Town Hall were not courteous, but, speaking from his own knowledge, he did not think it could be possible for any body of men to be more courteous than they had beento him during his campaigns. (Applause.) He had put the officials to a great deal of trouble in answering his questions. He had now to thank his bravo body of workingmen who formed the committee, who, although defeated on one occasion, went on fight- ingagainst great odds, and had succeeded ht last in giving him a record majority. ::NTS OF THE WEEK.

A GRANDE DAME AND A PERSONALITY.

By the death of Lady Spencer England lioses one of her most conspicuous grande dames, and by •iirtue, Snot only of her position, but also of her dignified and striking person- ality, as well as her lofty character, it was .always as a leader that she appeared in -society. She was one of three handsome sisters, daugh- ters of the late Mr. Frederick Seymour and .lady Mary Gor- don. When,after her marriage to Earl Spencer, .she had to take .an important position in Eng- lish social life, .she became at ,once the leading hostess of the Idber al Party, and her recep- tions at Spencer House drew all

the greatest THE LATE LADY SPENCER. people in Lon- o n together. She was a great favourite with the late

-Queen, and indeed with all the Royal Family, :and during her last illness Queen Alexandra and Princess Christian were among the most constant inquirers. During the two periods when Lord Spencer was Lcvfil Lieutenant of Ireland she gave him the most valuable help .on the social side of his duties. The Irish Viceregal Court was probably never more brilliant or more dignified than during Lord -Spencer's term of office, and the energy lvith which' he and Lady Spencer threw them- -selves .into everything that might prove of benefit to • the country, and the gracious and -dignified manner in which they performed the difficult duties of their office, will long be re- anembered in Ireland. Those who have had the privilege of visiting her at Althorp can snever think of Lady Spencer apart from her country home. Far more than many houses which. have been especially designed by the owners, it seemed to form a perfect setting for her fine and dignified character.

THE TOLSTOIAN CANDIDATE.

Never has there been such a remarkable Parliamentary candidate as Mr. Stewart Gray, who has come forward for the Tonbridge Division of Kent in opposition to Mr. Griffith— Boscawen, the sitting Member, and Mr.i Hedges, the adopted Liberal candidate. At his first public appearance before the, electors at the Tunbridge Wells. Town Hail he ap- peared with a sack thrown over his shoulders in place of an overcoat, and, dispensing with the formality of inviting some person to act as president; placed a Union Jack in the chair —symbolical of Mr. Gray's political platform, Which is • " The Land for the People," and until they get it he counsels the adoption of obstructive tactics on the Irish Parliamentary pattern. MR. STEWART GRAY AND HIS HABITATION.

look upon him as an English Tolstoi. " He certainly lives in Tolstoian fashion. He de- pends for his daily bread on work done for farmers in the district, and his address is Horseshoe Bothy," Tonbridge—a brick hut on Castle Hill, between Tonbridge and Pem- bury, which was condemned some years ago as uninhaoitable by the boLough council: There for two years Mr. Gray has lived the life of an agricultural labourer. The hut con- tains nothing but a washstand, a couch, a bench, a croquet box (which serves as a cup- board), and an old tea-chest. He comes from a family of "bonnet lairds "—men who farm their own land —in Midlothian, and after• being educated for the law became A Writer to the Signet. He has made some £4,000 by cutting up a large estate into smaller holdings. Brewery shares once occupied his attention, and the failure of a Haddington brewery com- pany, in which • he was largely interested, he claims to be due to the under-selling of beer by Pattisons, of whisky-selling notoriety. He now clainis to be a follower of St. Francis of Assisi. He was at one. time an owner of race- horses and secretary to the Moderator of the General. Assembly of the Free Church. of Scotland.

OUR ROYAL FRIEND OF GREECE.

r The King of the Hellenes, as brother of Queen Alexandra, has always been very popu- lar in this country, and everyone will cor- dially welcome him as an honorary admiral in our navy. In the last "Gazette " it is announced that King Edward has been pleased to confer this honour upon his brother-in- law, with whom he has always been upon the most affection- ate terms. King George ofGreece makes only the fifth officer hold- ing honorary rank in the British Navy. The late Queen made the Kaiser an Admiral as far back as 1889, and King Ed- ward similarly honoured Prince Henry of Prus- sia in 1901 and

RIS MAJESTY TIM GREECE.

King Carlos of

Portugal this year. The only other honorary officer in the Navy is Prince Christian of Denmark, who was appointed a lieutenant in February, 1901. In the Army there are two foreign Field-Mar- shals—the German Emperor, appointed at thc time of the Queen'sluneral, and the Emperor of Austria, who received his baton Only this last week from Lord Methuen, who went to Vienna on a special mission for the purpose.• King George's appointment is particularly apropos, as, when a boy, he was a sailor, only abandoning the Navy when the Crown of Greece was offered to him forty years age. He has been in serious•family trouble during the last few days, as the Queen of Greece has been operated on in St. Petersburg and had. a tumour removed. Her illness was kept as quiet as possible to allay anxiety, and she preferred to return to her old home for the *operation. The Queen of Greece was t Russian Grand Duchess.

THE MECHANICAL BRICKLAYER.

We live in an age of labour-saving appli= ances, and it seems as though in the future everything will be done by the aid of tlachigerY. Almost every day one hears of ionfie fresh invention or some new method of hung away with the mere 4bousan being and N'atitati3ag mactim - Tkt 13teg 44,411,

these is a very clever contrivance for laying bricks at six times the rate a man can work. This patent bricklaying machine both lays and cements the bricks, which are mechanically covered with mortar run out of a hopper. All it requires is to be fed and to have the handle turned I Two vertical

• ----77 q , n ____.--ma=ca?-;AP . . ' "1-• . ' . 0...„-- ~,-.._ -.: ___ . ~.. v. J --I--L—L---L-1--a. --I-

HOW THE NEW BRICK-LAYING MACHINE

WORKS.

poles are fixed at the end of the wall which is being built, in addition to the usual scaffold poles, and intermediate posts are placed where required. Fixed to these is a girder, which is raised as the wall grows higher. On the top of this girder the machine itself is placed. A pinion is geared into a chain which runs along the girder, and this is tnrned by means of a winch-hatdle. By this process of turning the machine is run along the girder, pressing the bricks into their places as it goes by means of a lever and revolving rollers. In this way the work is got over at a great speed, and carried out with absolute accuracy.

THE NEW WOMEN'S CLUB.

Men nowadays have long ceased to have the monopoly of club life, and women's clubs have sprung up on all sides. Hitherto, howL ever, they have been rather a luxury to their members than a source of use and economy; but the latest is to combine the two latter quali- ties with the The

former

projected club,

7which is to be

• - it 4 3 .t

===== 0002601_19031107_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
called the Ly-

ceum, will be situatedbetween

Charing

and Fleet Street, though its actual

site has not yet been chosen: but

necessary

capital has been guaranteed by

certain

It is hoped by Miss Consltince Smedley, who is

the originator

and mainspring *MISS CONSTANCE SMEDLEY, of the whole THE FOUNDER OF THE CLUB idea, that it will be ready to be

opened by next April. The object of the club is to provide a meeting-place and a home for women of. the learned professions, where they can not only enjoy themselves, but also find a headquarters for work. The club will be affiliated to similar clubs in the other capitals in due course, and the mem- bers will be helped in their work in every way by introductions. Another source of help will be, amongst other things, a com- plete journalistic register, which will contain full particulars of all papers and editors, and the work required by them. There are to be a hundred bedrooms on the premises for residents, and meals will be served at all prices to suit all pockets. Ciie great feature will be American chafing-dish suppers for the belated journalist or theatre-goer. The scheme is a splendid one, and ought to be certain of success, as it will fill a long-felt want amongst working women.

===== 0002601_19031107_art0035.txt | included=true | included =====
THE LATEST DOMESTIC PET.

The Crystal Palace Cat Show has been re- sponsible for bringing under public notice a new sort of cat .that is not really a cat at all, but a lemur. The animal is soft and Ifluffy, aind makes an ideal pet for children owing to the fact that, being a vegetarian, it is not furnished with claws, and has merely harmless teeth which it never uses spitefully. The Madagascar cat is its formal title, -and there is every likelihood that this pretty little creature will become very popu- lar as a pet. An interesting cat story was told the other day by Lady Decies. She

has a cat which always rushes to the dining. room directly the gong sounds. If by chance it fails to arrive before the door is closed it seizes the rug outside, which is kept down by a' weight, and executes a very creditable double knock by lifting the edge with its teeth and alloying the weighted end to drop on the ground.

THE HISTORIC ARIADNE.

Considerable interest was evinced in the Ariadne yacht case, which came before Mr. Justice Walton and a special jury in the King's Bench Division last week. The. action arose out of the refusal of an underwriter at Lloyd's, Burr by name, to pay Mr. T. C. 4 Kerry an insur- -• ance policy on his yacht, •the Ariadne, which was lost off New Zealand in March, 1901. The policy was for twenty thou- sand guineas, of which Mr. Barr himself only held a frac- tional part—in all, two hundred and fifty, guin- eas ; and. his refusal to pay was based on 2 charge of mis r e presentation, as he alleged MR. T. C. KERRY, THE that the yacht PLAINTIFF. was purchased for two thous- and guineas, and the other valuation was grossly excessive. In the alternative the de- fence asserted that the yacht had been wil- fully stranded by Mumford, the captain, at the instigation of the owner, who was alleged to have given him four hundred pounds to do so. Mr. Lawson Walton, K.C.. who ap- peared for the plaintiff, made a very strong speech, asserting that the yacht had been wrecked by the strange currents, and that Mumford's confession was a bogus one, and the agreement purporting to emanate from Mr. Kerry promising him four hundred pounds was a forgery. Mumford was convicted at Christchurch, in New Zealand, and the charge at the time dismissed against Mr. Kerry. Evidence of a very unusual aud interesting nature, which would have afforded a splendid plot for an author, was taken at great length, and then the case ended by the withdrawal of all allegations against Mr. Kerry, judgment being given for him for an agreed sum. Thus ended a very sensational case: _ _ -

The Examiner, Saturday, November 7. 1903. THE COUNTY CHAMPIONSHIP.

THE NORTHERN UNION.

HULL KINGSTON ROVERS v. WARRINGTON. There must have been no fewer that 10,000 spectators round the Craven street enclosuroi when Warrington kickedoff in this match. It was a match to draw a crowd, for both teams occupy an interesting position, and the promise of fast and exciting play was fulfilled to the letter. Teams : _

Hull Kingston Rovers—Sinclair, back; West, Gordon Sleep, and Madley, three-quarter backs ; Barry and Guy half backs: Fletcher, Starks, Kemp, Pickering, Townsley, Goulding, Gorman, and Taylor, forwards. Warrington—Hallam, back; Fish. Isherwood, Dicken- son and Harris, three-quarter backs; Bate and Davies half backs ; Boardman, Morrison, Swift, Harmer, Lunt, Jolley, Edmundson, and Naylor, forwards. Referee, Mr. F. Kennedy, Broughton. Touch judges, Messrs. H. Sewell, Leeds. and H. Tomes, York. Dickenson started,and aftera reply to Barry the game settled in mid-field. Bothsides were penalised in turn, and then an exchange of kicks went in favour of the home team. The home forwards started off badly in the pack, and the visitors were constantly getting possession. Sinclair and Gordon were each resionsible for some exceedingly smart work, the former at length putting the ball in touch near the visitors' line. Here the wirepullers were penalised,and Stark made a bid for goal, but was unsuccessful. A succession of exciting incidents followed. First Madley got clear of every- body but Hlllam, and was only just stopped by him on the line, a throw escape for the visitors; and then Sin- clair was near dropping a goal. The primrose and blue were let off with a touch-down, and the drop-out saw Isherwood and Fish indulge in some combined work, which carried play the entire length of the field, and compelled Madley to seek refuge in a minor point. Another penalty fell to the Rovers, and Gordon placed a goal. Half-time: Rovers 2 points, Warrington nil. Starks re-started, and one of the visiting forwards making his mark, the ball was sent into touch at half- way. Sinclair was early called upon, and was not found wanting. The home forwards at this stage were very deficient in the packs. A big rush by the home van- guard improved their position considerably, and Sleep was seen making tracks for the Warrington crease. Had he parted to West at the right moment, the latter would have got in unopposed, but he delayed, and Isherwood knocked the ball out of his hand.

Starks was badly obstructed, and a penalty ought to have been awarded the Rovers in front of their oppon- ents' sticks, but the infringement went unnoticed. The home team lost ground by off-side tactics, but quickly regained it by vigorous forward play. One of the visi- tors was injured in a tackle by Gorman, and the cir- cumstances were .deemed grave enough to merit a rebuke. Warrington got the upper hand in succeeding play, and Sinclair only just touched down in time. Hallam proved a tower of strength for the visi- tors, and on one occasion put in a remarkably brilliant effort. Neither side showed an inclination to open out in the most approved fashion, the play for the most part consisting of scrimmaging and kicking. The visit- ing forwards continued to get possession in scrum after •scrum, and when Fish got hold the downfall of the home citadel seemed imminent. Sinclair was, however all there when wanted, and touched down in the nick of time. Starks made a fine run, and put the wirepullers on their defence, but Hallam replied with a beautiful kick, which Guy misjudged, and allowed to roll in touch in the home 25. The visitors (thanks to the admirable kicking of their backs) were more than holding their own at this juncture. Rovers kicked as strongly, but failed to find touch, and it was due to this that they lost ground. Thanks to the fine jkicking of Dickenson, the visitors invaded the home lierritory, and looked like pulling the match out of the fire. Gordon. how- ever, raised the seige with a grand dribble. it was touch and go right up to the finish. Final :—Rovers, 1 goal (2 points) ; Warrington nil.

HUNSLET v. BROUGHTON RANGERS

This match was played on the Hunslet ground, and was won by the Rangers, after an exciting game by a margin of four points. From the kick-off it was noticed that the visiting forwards, contrary to expectations, were holding their own with the home lot, and this enabled the brothers James to repeatedly get the ball from the scrimmages. Brough- ton Rangers : Two goals and one try (seven points) ; Hun.slet, one try (three points).

LANCASHIRE COMBINATION.

WARRINGTON " A "V. WERNETIL

About 2,000 spectators witnessed this match at Wilderspool, on Saturday. In the three-quarter line Kenyon made a promising debut. The game opened fast, and several fine bursts by the home forwards con- stantly harassed the Werneth custodian. Smart clearances were affected, and neat passing was only partially responded to, with the result that Knight had a spell of defensive work. Mahoney took a pass from Jordon, and a hot attack was made on the railway goal. After Short had been pulled up near the corner, the Warrington backs again got cleverly going, and Kenyon scored a fine try.Werneth retaliated,and one of theirthree- quarters effected a fine run, which was improved upon. Warrington now attacked strongly, and from a penalty Knight equalized, half time arriving with five points for each team. On resuming Mahoney took a pass from Jordan and evading; the opposing backs, dodged over. Near the close the visitors were penal- ized, and Knight kicked a penalty goal,Warrington thus winning by ten points to five.

A FARMER'S WIFE ATTACKED.

Not far from Monikie, a quiet little village near Dundee. lies Denhead Farm, tenanted by a farmer named Davidson. Mrs. Davidson was, until quite recently, frequently attacked by acute biliousness, in- digestion, and headache. She has now been completely cured by Charles Forge's bile beans, and in gratitude has given the facts of her case for publication. Says she :—"For twenty years peace and rest were denied me in consequence of acute biliousness and in- digestion. Night after night I had to sit up in bed racked with pain. My head .often felt like splitting, and the pains across my back, shoulders, and stomach, were almost unbearable. I held cold, damp cloths to my head to ease the pain, and I tried to bring up the bile from my stomach by making myself sick, but it was all to no purpose. Day and night I suffered alike. "I would be going about doing my work. milking the cows, and doing other farm work, when suddenly a mist wsuld rise before my eyes and hlmost blind me. I was also subject to dizziness, and often when I crossed the farmyard I would turn dizzy and so faint with pain that I have had to walk about al- most doubled in two. A doctor in Alyth told me that the biliousness and indigestion were of too long standing to be cured. I tried patent medicines, but they were no good. Then a few monthsago one of my sons heard of Chas. Forde's bile beans. I tried a box, and after a few doses I got a night's undisturbed sleep—the first for twenty years. As I took the beans I got better and better, and I am glad to say I am now quite cured. I have no pain whatever, and am in better ' condition than I have been for twenty years. Life has again become pleasurable. I owe my present health solely to bile beans, and I cannot speak too higly of them. My husband and family will bear witness to what they have done for me." Charles Forde's bile beans, which effected the above. mentioned remarkable cure, are an absolute specific for indigestion, biliousness, liver and kidney disorders, pains in the side, loins, and back, constipation, piles, dizziness, sleeplessness, nervousness, antemia, and all female ailments. Also for colds, chills, miuralgia, lumbago rheumatism, and as a preventive of influenza. Of all Chemists at one-and-three-halfpence or two- and-nine per box. Avoid substitutes and imitations, which are harmful.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Baptist Church, Colborne Street.

On Sunday evening last at the above Church the Rev. A. Wells, a Medical Missionary from China, gave a very interesting address on his work among the Chinese. During the 26 years he had laboured inChina, God had been very good and many had been won for Christ. The speaker related many instances of faithfulness on the part of the native Christians during the terrible raid by the Boxers in 1900,when numbers endured death rather than deny their God.

Wycliffe P.S.A. Society.

There was an average attendance at the meeting of this society on Sunday. Mr. Simcock presided and spoke upon the prevailing habit of using bad language. Lecture: on " Old. Warrington."

On Monday eveningin the Wycliffe Memorial Build,- ings a lecture on "'Old Warrington" was given by Mr. James Kenworthy under the auspices of the. Young People's Society. The lecturer explained that no Roman remains had been found in Warrington but that it was proved beyond a doubt that there were at one time Roman settlements at Wilderspoal and: Stockton Heath. Many interesting historical events were related, reference being made to the build- ing of a wooden bridge across the Mersey to enable Icing Henry VII. to cross on his way to visit his son- in-law, the Earl,of Derby, the expense of the bridge being borne by the ',Earl and also the making of the roads through Warrington. The construetion of the Bridgewater Canal and the effect on the trade of the district; Warrington as a .shipbuilding centre, men- tion being made of the launching of the famous ship "'Payleur" ; reminiscences • of the old coaching days when Warrington was more important than Liver- pool or Manchester; and the • historical events of Bewsey Hall and the Priory were related by tire lecturer. Mr. Kenworthy dealt with his subject in uch a manner as to. make it most instructive and entertaining.

THE LATE MR. J. SHAW GREEN, J.P.

IMPRESSIVE FUNERAL.

Address by the Rev. R. Wilkins Rees.

Amid many manifestations of respect, the grave closed over the remains of the late Mr. J. Shaw Green on Saturday. Prior to the interment at the Cemetery a funeral service was held in Bewsey-road Wesleyan Chfirch, with which the deceased gentleman had been. identified since its erection. The cortege left the late residence of the deceased, D3.lby Villas, Latch- ford, shortly after one o'clock, and was met at the church gates by . members and officials of the Corporation and local magistrates. The mourners:were John S. Green(brother),Mr. J. Wilfrid Dupre (son-in-law), Mr. J. Green Chappell (nephew), Mr. Cook (brother-in-law), Mr. W. Thornton (nephew), Mr. T. Fairhurst, Mr. William Silcock, Mr. R. Silcock, Mr. A. Silcock, Alderman Sutton, Mr. C. Whitley, Mr. J. Bennett, Mr. A. T. Davies (Liverpool), Dr. Bennett (deceased's medical attendaut), Mr. %V. Knowles, Mr. C. Healey and Mr. Herbert Booth (the last three representing the office staff), the Revs. John Gunnell, G. H. Carnson, R. Wilkins Rees and W. C. J. Neville, ministers of the circuit. The service commenced with psalm xc and after the singing of "Jesu,lover of my soul,"the Rev. it. Wilkins Rees read the usual burial lesson(' Cor- xv c). He then paid a graceful and:touching:tribute to the memory of the deceased gentleman in the following words :—Amon,* the shadows, the fears, the terrors of man the apostle here moves with ever-lightening footsteps • the close of the chapter is not a dirge, it is a joyful wedding march—" Thanks be to God which giveth us the victory through our Lord Jesus Christ." The strong Son of God has won the supreme victory in the shadowy realm of death ; has vanquished the great adversary; has captured captivity. Now we see that death is no destruction. Now we see that the end of death is not the perishing of the seed in the ground. The end of death is the uprising of a new and larger life.. Death no longer conquers. Death no longer wins a victory. Death no longer pricks us with a sting. Death is deprived of its sting. The face of death is towards the sun of life. Death is the advent to a larger life and God shall clothe that life with glory as it pleaseth Him. The dead in Christ shall have the tree of life; they shall neither labour nor be weary. "Thanks be to God which giveth us the victory through our Lord Jesus Christ." He lives whom we call dead. To him was given that perfect presence of God's face which we for want of words call death. Our beloved: they are at rest. We may not stir the heaven of their repose with load-voiced grief or passionate regret or selfish plaint. Our beloved do not wait until some far- off tin*, while their bodies sleep beneath the sod, and the cold winds play and the cold rains beat upon their bed. They are from home as to the body, but they are at home with the -Lord. They depart, but they are ; with Christ, which is far better. "Thanks be tcl aol for His itaspeakable gift." Our friend, to whonl iikiriciw pay our tribute of respect and affection, was . .

A MAN 00 THIS

Here he was born' here he Vera lis childhobd, his youth, and the days of his maniictddl and Within sight and sound of it he quietly put his life aside and re- moved his house to the greater company. It was here he served his fellow men ; it was here he soved his God. He loved this town, and his love expressed itself in much earnest, self-sacrificing toil on behalf of what he deliberately and intelligently considered to be its best interests. Many who are here to-day know full well the arduous work he faithfully performed for long years in connection with your Town Council, and with what efficiency he discharged his duties as member. and as chairman of some of the Council's most important committees.. He served this town with a passionate, an unresting,l an unsparing, a losing service. It is not too much to say that Warrington was engraven in his very heart.

===== 0002601_19031107_art0055.txt | included=true | included =====
HE LOVED THIS CHURCH.

He was one of the trustees of this building and, of late, treasurer of its trust. For years he was leader of the principal Bible class, and, after, superintendent of the Sunday school associated with this church. In the school some of his best work was done. His scholars respected him, loved him. He knew the children—knew them not as a mass, but as individuals, was interested in their welfare, and cared in all things for their truest well-being. The young men and young women held him in high esteem; the teachers confided hr him, honoured him, and when failing health obliged him to retire from the active oversight of the school, they, unwilling to lose his name from their list of officers, retained him as honorary superintendent. Within these walls he worshipped with constant regu- larity—coming again and yet again when almost over- pbwered by weakness. It seems but as yesterday that he sat yonder,andnowhe worships in the upper sanctuary. 'And yet he now joins.with us and we now-join with him. Tt is a broken chorus' but it is a common psalm. Our. departed friend was

A KEEN. LOVER OF NATIME. With what pleasure he would produce his pocket micro- scope and show the -unsuspected beauties of some way- side 'weed, some common flower! "A primrose by the river's brim, a yellow primrose was to him," ah ! but it was something more. .He saw with the eye of the naturalist; he saw also with the eye of the poet—the devout worshipper in nature's limitless temple. I can- not readily forget being with him in the' open country early this year and seeing the delight which lit up his face as he stopped to listen to the song of the first soaring skylark of the spring. He was a man of wide. intelligence, of kindly disposition, of intense selT- respect(a desirable quality so apt to he misunderstood), of courteous manner—may 11 not say that he had more than a touch of an old-world courtesy? and of devout spirit. One who judges righteous judgment, and who knew him well for several years, says without reserve :

===== 0002601_19031107_art0056.txt | included=true | included =====
"HE WAS A CHRISTIAN GENTLEMAN INDEED."

And some there be that have no memorial—who are perished as though they had never been. A good life bath but few days, but a good name endureth• for ever. And how shall' speak of his home life? lie had very strong domestic ffections, was a most affectionate'and considerate husband, and a model father—a fine com- panion of his children. This gift all other gifts above shall not be lost in death; too strong, too great holy love to fail with failing breath,. And each member of his little household can surely say: ff Death hides, but it cannot divide,' Thou art but on Christ's other side. Thon with Christ and Christ with me And so together still are we."

• DEATH HAD A HARD STRUGGLE with him, and again and yet again retired baffled from the contest. For the last few years his weakness was such at times that he might well have said: "Tired with all these, for restful death I cry." But no ! He bravely disputed the ground inch by inch and insisted, as we all should insist, not merely on what time would give him, but on all he could win from time by sheer courage and a fight. The complaint of the com- mander about the soldiers he had to fight was that they did not know when they were beaten. That must have been time's complaint concerning our friend. His was a life that senta challenge to the end, but when the end came —and after all it came soon, but though the span of his life was short he had lived a full life—but when the end came he could say: Welcome, friend ! Among his last words was a confident testimony to the exceeding preciousness of Jesus Christ. He passed away gently. The kiss of Heaven came and he slept. And when God sends His angel to us with a scroll of death let us also look on it as an act of mercy and lay our heads down softlyand go to sleep. "Our knowledge of that life is small, The eye of faith is dim, But 'tis enough that Christ knows all, And we shall be with Him." "Rock of Ages" was then very feelingly sung, and the Rev. John Gunnell offered prayer and pro- nounced the Benediction. The Dead March in "Saul" was played as the coffin was removed from the church: The procession was re-formed and assumed imposing dimensions. Along the line of the route to the Cemetery, tradesmen had put up their shutters and private residents had drawn their blinds as a mark of respect. At the grave side the service, again con- ducted by the Rev R. Wilkins Rees. was, wisely brief but excea4ingly reverential and impressive. A posse of police under the superintendence of Chief Inspector Phillips was drawn up near the grave. Amongst those who attended at the Cemetery were : Mr. F. Taylor (secretary of the Gas Works), Mr. J. H. Mounfield, Mr. T. E. Gibson, Mr. Thomas Tunstall, Mr. Charles White, Mr. Wm. Holbrook. Mr. T. Welsby, Mr. A. Jolley, and Mr. A. Mounfield.

WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS. The Non-Reception of Fever Cases at the Borough Hospital.

A meeting of this Board was held on Wednesday morning. Mr. A. Brooks presided, and there were also present :—Mrs. Clay, Rev. Father Wilson, Rev. T. R. Matthews, Dr. Patterson, Messrs. A. J. The CLERK said it would be seen that the cost had largely increased. It was a subject that was agitating the country.

THE ISOLATION OF FEVER PATIENTS. THE CORPORATION AND THE QUESTION OF COST. The CLERK referred to the question discussed at the interim meeting re the non-reception of fever patients from the Workhouse into the Borough Infectious Diseases Hospital. On the 30th of October he wrote to the Town Clerk as follows: "I under- stand that your view is that although Nurse Potts lived within the municipal borough, hence within the hospital rating area, she could not be admitted when attacked byscarlet fever therein, unless the Guardians in whose service she was first undertook payment of the cost of what Dr. Gornall terms " the sojourn in hospital" and what you, possibly meaning the same thing, term maintenance."" This was, of course, the serious departure from what has so long obtained. My intention was to apply for a mandamus, a simple remedy productive of the judgment of a high court. It now occurs to me that you may have cause for your present action not yet made clear to the Guardians to whom I am furtner to report and you will oblige by a. reply to me thereon."

Sin 6.3 then, continued the clerk, another case of scarlet fever had arisen in the Workhouse. It was the case of a child born in the Borough and in all respects a Warrington child. He (the clerk) telephoned to the Tewn Clerk and received the following reply : Town Clerk's Office, November 2nd, 1903.—Dear Sir, your telephonic message was the first intimation I had of another outbreak of scarlet fever in the workhouse. I have since communicated with Dr. Gornall and he is prepared to admit the case into the hospital. As you and I entertain different opinions as to the liability of the Guardians to pay for the maintenance of cases removed from the Workhouse premises it is of no use discussing in this letter the legal aspects of the case but as stated in my previous letter to you lam prepared to advise the Corpora- tion to accept the ruling of the Local Government Board on the point. In the meantime if you will give me a written undertaking to pay the cost of maintaining the case removed from the Workhouse in the event of the Local Government Board deciding against you this and any other case may be removed

without delay.—Yours faithfully, J. Lion Whittle. The CLERK said he telephoned back to say that he would advise the Guardians to adopt the course sug- gested. From inquiries he had mado he found the matter to be a very small one, as only one case of scarlet fever had been removed for a long number of years. He was quite clear on one point, and that was that the nurse in question was an inhabitant just as much as any other officer of the Board who might live outside the Workhouse. The Workhouse paid rates on an assessment of £B5O, and the whole of the hospital charges of the B6rough were included in the rates, so that the Guardians were paying towards the cost of maintainingkpatients in the fever hospital, whether rich or poor. The Board also paid liberal subscriptions to the District Nursing Association and the Warrington Infirmary, from which the out-lying districts ed the Union received no advantage. It was scarcely worthy of the Borough to raise this question at this time. Mr. MATHER. thought that some equitable arrange- ment could be arrived at.

The CLERK said it was a small thing if decided against the Board.

-Mr. A. J. JOLLEY said with all due respect to what the Clerk had mentioned, he was confident that there was something wrong in the statement that only one fever case had been sent to the infectious diseases hospita) within recent years. During the time of his (Mr.ley's) chairmanship of the House Committee several cases wereremoved and Mr. Pilling bore him ou in that statement. Mr. PILLING: Yes.

Mr. A. J. JOLLEY (continuing) said with reference to the contributions to medical charities which happened to be situated in the urban area, those charities had nothing to do with the Corporation of Warrington. He did not hold a brief for the Corporation but it did not seem to him that the Board's contribution to the Infirmary, which did a great deal of surgical work not merely for Warrington but for the districts covered by the union as a whale, affected the position taken up by the Warrington Corporation with respect to fever cases. Then the Nursing Association was a great help to the relieving officers of the union and kept cases off the books. A principle lay behind the contention of the Corporation as to whether oases of fever from the Workhouse were to he paid for by the ratepayers of Warrington or by tila Nyhalck ninon. Ho thought the Local Qqyq.';iinlealt qaT4 *atild give an Oven its a legAl decision an the point., he CIAIC :,414 he hwi net haaa nhle totrace mor& than one fever case being tenl4v4l. inr6d6ut yetirg. Ji cost of the Berough Infectious diseases hospital to which they had to contribute through the rateable value of the Workhouse premises was incurred not only by poor people but by some of the best families in Warrington and therefore the Corporation was making a charge upon the poor rate for expenses incurred in the main- tenance of fever patients, who were well able to pay the cost themselves. (Hear hear). The subject then dropped.

MR. BTRRELL SICK OF THE C.E.T.S. Mr. Birrell, president of the National Liberal Federa- tion, in a speech in East Bristol on Wednesday night, pointed out that the two ewe lambs of temperance people, viz., the decisions in the Sharpe and Wakefield and Farnham Justices cases were to have their throats cut, next session if Mr. Balfour's intentions took effect. He asked temperance people" Of what staff are you made?" and confessed that he was sick of the Chnrch of England Temperance Societies, who opened their meetings by invoking Divine aid against the demon drink, and the members of which then voted for pub- licans' nominees. This was making temperance work a sham.

At Altrincham on Wednesday, Judge Bowen Rowlands awarded ,L 75 to Alfred and Anil Taylor in a case in which they claimed £lBO from the Manchester Ship Canal Company for compensation for the loss of their son, who lost his life while following his employment as a shunter.

be excused for declining to regard his policy ag the only opportunity and himself as the last of our Lnper ial statesmen. Mr. CHAMBERLAIS again accused Lord ROJEBERY of having giver' up the "bond of commercial unity" wuchwAs ever his ideal. But when Lord ROSEBERY used that phrase ha declared at the same time that our commercial relations with the Colonies were per- fectly satisfactory, and that we must strive for Imperial federation in a political sense. We are all as ready as Hr. CHAMBERLAIN to recognise the brotherhood which binds the Colonies to the mother country. But th It is no reason why We should impoverish our own land by adopting his crude proposals. He charges the Liberal leaders with caring for no unity but, the unity of their party, and he charges Lord GO3CHEN and Sir MICHAEL HICKS-BEACH with being Imperial in theory and Little Englander in practi2e. This is sheer recklessness. Mr. CHAMBERLAIN does not hesitate to browbeat the distinguished public men who will not support him, although they are disposed to keep Mr. BALFOUR in office. The Prime Minister, we imagine, will be made ex- tremely uncomfortable by these onslaughts, which indicate a verj different temper from that of a man who is confident of victory.

Mr. CHAMBERLA BIRMINGHAM AND performance at Bir- THE CABINET. mingham make the policy of the Govern' Mont more shad owy and ineffectual than ever. The situation is admirably summed up by a writer in the " Fortnightly Review " : "There is Mr. CHAMBERLAIN. There is the opposition to Mr. CHAMBERLAIN. The Government can scarcely be said to exist." Only once since his campaign began has Mr. CHAMBERLAIN deigned to remember that Mr. BALFOUR has a little scheme. It consists of a warning to foreign States that if they persist in treating our commerce unfairly we shall really have to be quite cross with them. Ur. BALFOUR reminds us of the curate in that amusing farce, "The Private Secretary." When the curate is annoyed he says to his tormentor, "If Yoll are not careful I shall give you a good hard knock ! " Mr. CHAMBERLAIN is a real fighting man, and if his party want to fight without any discrimination of the objects of attack, and with a supreme disregard for the consequences to the prosperity of the nation, he is the man for the money. " What does it matter," he asks, "if the nation be made a little richer or a little poorer ? " This spirit is not business, anti it is not common sense; but compared with the attitude of Mr. BALFJUR it is certainly heroic. Wealth or poverty—it is all one to us," cries the voice of Birmingham. "It was Protection any way that laid the foundations of the national trade. COBDEN was a schemer who wanted to ruin the landowners in the interest of the manu- facturers. He thought cheap bread would keep wages low. He hated the Colonies. But for him we should still be enjoyiflg. commercial supremacy with a :high tariff. What we have to do now is to set the tariff up again." This is one of Mr. CHAMBERLAIN'S menaces, which grow more strident with every speech. At Glasgow he was anxious to assure us that his dose of Protection was a very mild one, and that we could take it without fear of violent

reaction. At Birmingham there is no attempt, t° conceal the real spirit and scope of the prescrip- tion. Mr. CHAMBERLAIN will tell US, no doubt, that he still believes in the wonder-working' potency of 2s. on corn, and so forth. But he stands revealed as a Protectionist who, when he finds a low tariff is no use, will not scruple to build it as high as the most formidable tariff wall constructed by the foreigner. All this gives fresh point to Mr. MoßLßv's question whether Ministers will take off their masks at a Cabinet Council. It is said that the Roman augurs used to smile at each other when they were safe from public observation. Will Mr. BALFOUR and his colleagues drop their little masquerade in the privacy of Downing- street ? Hitherto they have assumed, with per- fect gravity, that they have a beautiful policy quite distinct from Mr. CHAMBERLAIN'S—a policy moreover, which can be kept conveniently vague for at least another session. If the Opposition should enlarcre od the danger of Protection, Mr. BALFOUR will answer in the debate on the Address: "Dear me! It may be all that you say. But what has it to do with our policy? MY right honourable friend, the Secretary for War, a reformer if ever there was one, has got a scheme to make the War Office reafly useful. Please wait for that, and don't talk to me about COBDEN- who is dead." So a Government which can scarcely be said to exist, except by a stretch of !courteous make-believe, will give itself the airs of reforming zeal, and ask the con" stituencies to suspend their judgment on the fiscal controversy until some important legisla- tion has been added to Vie Statute-book. Is it possible that Mr. BALFOUR'S party will be able to practice his equanimity ? The question raised by MT. CHAMBERLAIN cannot be put upon the shelf, to be taken down at a convenient moment. It has a vital bearing upon the trade of the country which is bound to suffer from uncertainty, and delay. How can men engaged in great industrial enterprises apply all their energies to their busi- ness while they live under the shadow of a fiscal revolution? Many of them are in the House of Commons; and Mr. BALFOUR expects them to give their minds to War Office reform, which, impor- tant as it is, cannot be properly considered under such extraordinary conditions. What will Mr. CHAMBERLAIN be doing meanwhile? Free from official duties, he must either lapse into the com- parative isolation of a private member or spend his time outside the House spreading his pror aganda. Everybody knows which :course :heel° choose. Wherever he goes he will :carry the real issue with him, and leave Mr. BALFOUR to main- tain a pretence which must become too absurd to be tolerated.

at The difficult problem of the Mayoralty has last been solved, and Alderman William Bolton 16 destined to wear the robes and chain during th,e next municipal year. The honour lay netwera him and Councillor William Pierpoint-, but latter, who was approached first, could not see.lll' way to accept office. We are sure the decision he has come to will be generally regrettec.ll for Councillor Pierpoint has deserved well of hi,s, townsmen, and would have made, we believe, excellent Mayor. There is still an Aldermsnle vacancy to be filled,and if anyone is entitled to the, position it is Councillor Pierpoint. The thanks. °I the town are due to the Mayor-elect, for the eh" culty of finding a suitable Chief Magistrate was ,11 serious one,and Alderman Bolton has courageous stepped into the breach. May he have every assistance and prove himself worthy of the confidence reposed in him.

THE EXAMINER.

NOVJAMBHR 7th 1903.

On Wednesday night MR. CHAMBERLAIN Mr. CHAMBERLAIN

AT BIRMINGHAM. indulged freely in history and prophecy. He seems to have a strong suspicion, not yet amounting to a presumption, that the abandon- ment of Protection in this country was a mistake. The world went very well before Sir ROBERT PEEL changed our commercial policy. True there was a period of bad trade, and Mr. CHAMBERLAIN is good enough to ad -nit that the Free Traders had a strong case. He does not go so far as to say that they were entirely wrong, but he will probably say it before this campaign is over. He admits, too, that for twenty-five years after the repeal of the Corn Laws the country enjoyed abounding pros- perity. But, of course, Free Trade had nothing to do with that. Our foreign rivals are prosperous, and Mr. CHAMBERLAIN naturally ascribes this to otection, and nothing else. He failed on Wed- rusday night, as he has always failed, to show any sufficient reason why we should change our policy now. Having quoted history, he launched into the prophetic vein, and threatened us with the loss of the Colonies if we did not respond to their de- sires. He challenged denial of the statement that our trade with the Colonies hat greatly increased. Nobody has the smallest disposition to dispute with him on this score. Our. colonial trade has increased under the system which he declares to be no longer suited to our needs. What is there in this to fill our colonial kinsmen with a sense of uneasiness and even of revolt? Where is there the 'smallest indication that they have issued what Mr. CHAMBERLAIN is pleased ,to regard as an ul- timatum? It is not so long ago that Sir WILFRID LAURIER expressed the strong belief that only under Free Trade could England maintain her commercial greatness. When Canada granted a preference to British goods, the Prime Minister declared that it was a free gift designed to show her grateful sense of the benefits she derived from the Imperial partnership. Sir WILFRED LAURIER carefully repudiated the suggestion that she was making a concession and getting nothing in re- turn. But now Mr. CHAMBERLAIN talks as if the Colonies had with one voice demanded a pre- ferential tariff and given us a plain hint thit our refusal might lead to the disruption of the Empire!

We believe that the Birmingham speech will be read by colonial statesmen with a feeling akin to dismay. There is no doubt whatever that they would like us to grant them a preference if we believed this to he compatible with the well-being of our own people. But they will be disquieted to find that Mr. CHAMBERLAIN'S electioneering impetuosity virtually represents them as putting pistols to our heads. After all, we are entitled to say that we do not consider Mr. CHAMBERLAIN'S plan for imperial unity as feasible. No scheme which makes every commodity dearer in the United Kingdom can be expected by fair-minded men to commend itself to the British people. When they reject Mr. CHAMBERLAIN and his policy at the polls this will not be, a message of defiance to colonial aspirations. We have now an opportun- ity he says, which may never recur; but we may
