===== 0002601_19031107_art0089.txt | included=false | skipped/gap: fewer than 20 non-control characters after cleanup =====
•

===== 0002601_19031107_art0090.txt | included=true | included =====
Municipal election results are usually surprising Even when one attempts to fotesee all possible contingencies the free and independent voter will i contrive to do something extraordinary. He did so last November, and he has repeated the per— formance this week. There is a striking parallel be- tween the two elections. There were just the same number of contests, twelve months ago, and only in one ward did the Non-Traders fail to secure a representative. This year there is only one ward in which a Non-Trading candidate has been returned. Apparently a striking reversal of public opinion. But one has to look deeper in,o these matters before coming to any definite conclusion Upon the opinion of the electorate. There is one obvious fact that there is no gainsaying,—the rate- payers did not support the Non-Trading nominees with a particle of the enthusiasm of a year ago. Why? That is the question, and we make bold to answer it to the best of our belief. First and foremost the candidates of the Non-Trading Association were neither of sufficient standing nor sufficiently well-known in the wards, and secondly they did not wont with the determination necessary to secure election. An over-weening confidence on the part of most members of the Non-Trading Association has unquestionably proved their:undoing. We know, for a certainty, that some of the .candidates did practically no electioneering, nor were they backed up on the day of the poll. They made the serious error of relying on their laurels before they had won them and the electors have signally marked their dis- approval of what must have appeared to them, •studiAd neglect. It should hardly be necessary to point out that no man can win an election if he does not try. Monday's results should teach our Non-Trading friends a wholesome lesson. Like every other party, either of politicians or purists, they will have to roll up their sleeves and do battle manfully at election times.

The opinion has been general during the week, that the Non-Traders have received a death-blow. I We do not believe it. They have met with a rebuff, but he would be a poor fighter who gave up the struggle after the first knock. And the Non- Traders have not shown themselves poor fighters! In our opinion they are a force still to be recaconed With, and the fact must not be overlooked, that, as a direct result of the elections they are stronger in the Town Council than before. It depends entirely upon themselves, and their conduct in public affairs, whether they retain, or forfeit for ever, the confidence ,of the public. We do not doubt that the great bulk of the ratepayers are still, and always will be, -opposed to the trading which has been done in the past between members of the Town Council and the Corporation, and opposed also to such methods of business as have been disclosed in regard to,tho slack contracts, but they plainly ex- pect something more from the man who seeks their support than that he should merely be labelled" Non-Trader."

In another column we publish an important letter from Councillor Dodds, based on the lessons of the elections, and embodying a declaration of policy. The latter was especially necessary, for of late the policy of the Non-Trading Association has been so many-sided as to confuse. Some of the members appear to have got hold of the idea that they are a supervisory board, self-constituted in order to keep down the Corporation expenditure and make their presence felt in various diverse directions. They were even so ill- advised as to opposeilr.F.Stott's candidature not- withstanding his assurance that he was at one with them on the question of municipal trading. Councillor Dodds, who, personally, has never übscured the issilb or departed from his high ideals very rightly and clearly states that "our aim and object are to abolish trading, to see that men of our views on this question are elected whatever -their views on other subjects may be." This is the sum and substance of what the Non-Trading Association set out to accomplish, and they will only succeed by keeping the object steadily before them, and by not allowing *themselves to be influenced by other affairs,in re- gard to the conduct of which they will, very prob- ably, not have the town with them. A new party is neither required nor desired, and on this point also Councillor Dodds is explicit. He alone is the leader of the agitation, and it is only by following his counsels that the Non-Traders can hope to succeed. This fact has, possibly,been convincingly proved to them by the events of Monday. If not, then the only thing for Councillor Dodds to do is to re-form the Association.

Our mild and unassuming contemporary "The Warrington Tises appears to he crushed quite flat this week. It contains less than a couple of columns of local matter,and is, in fact,clothing itself in sackcloth and ashes. The election results are not even published,which is,to say the least,remarkable. Surely they were worth the space? There is much to be learnt from a careful and discriminat— ing study of election returns, and it is unkind of the" Times " to'deny its readers the opportunity, even though the effect might have been saddening. It is to be hoped the official organ of the Non- Trading A.ssociation does not accurately reflect the state of mind of the members, otherwise that august body must like Mahomed's coffin, be 3ust now suspended somewhere 'twist Heaven and -Earth.

For the first time in the correspondence which he has contributed to our columns, Captain G. R. Cresfield this week enters fully into the objects of the National Service League and explains in de- tail the advantages which would accrue from the ompulsory military training he advocates. We are not prepared or disposed to argue that the beneftts he instances might cot be experienced. 'Doubtless we should get an efficient home defence armysoo,ooo strong,and doubtless also the national Physique would be improved to a considerable degree by enforced military training, but the question is essentially one of principle, as well as Policy. The -British nation does, to once again, quote Mr. Crosfield, "make a God of freedom," and We hope it always will. Are the necessities of the hour such that we must sacrifice principle and throw freedom to the winds ? That in our opinion is the point at issue. Captain Crosfield will pro- bably answer in the affirmative. We are .still unconvinced, or rather our conviction that no form of conscription should be enter- tained remains unshaken. It is all very well for Captain Crosfield to plead that 100 days' service for every man is all that would be necessary. What guarantee have we that the 100 days would tot in course of time be extended to one, two, or three years, as the heads of the War Office might deem advisable? Compulsory service, however slight, is conscription neither more nor less, and the freedom-loving Briton will have none of it or We are much" mistaken. Like the corn-tax to. use Mr. Morley's recent simile, compulsory ser 'roe might start as a curly-headed infant, but how ,on g •would it be before it grew to be a hoary- needed sinner ? No'.! The National Service League evill have to come forward with a much stronger ase before compulsory service, however short, can be seriously considered in this country. In the Itieentime we would counsel the direction of effort rwards obtaining "efficiency." There is much to be hoped for from wiser administration—and we toost see that we get it.

===== 0002601_19031107_art0091.txt | included=true | included =====
Robinson as being on our side on the question of compulsory service. She brings arguments to bear which We have purposely ignored in our newspaper war with Capt,tin Crostield, but which are none the less powerful on that account. It has sufficed with us to argue that there s not even a military necessity for compulsory service and we are glad to note that Miss Robinson has put forward so convincingly the humanitarian aspect of the question.

Mr. King, the prospective Liberal candidate for the Knutsford division, is touring the conscituency to some purpose. Rarely have Liberal principles been so forcibly and ably pat before the electors of the division than has been the case during the past twelve months by Mr. King and thpse who have rendered him assistance. Meetings have been held in all parts of the division, and those around Warrington have been visited in turn. The meet- ing at Stockton Heathon Tuesday as well atten- ded, and the topic of the hour was freely discussed Our Free Trade policy found able defenders in Mr. King, Mr. A. H. Crosfield and Mr. C. E. Parker. who preyed that-they had a thorough grasp of this intricate question,and a courage to express them- selves in clear and unmistakeable terms. Mr. King was able to show, by his long lacquaintance with Warrington, that the gloomy picture drawn by Mr. Chamberlain with reference to the wire trade in the town is not borne out by experience Warrington has prospered—and seems likely to continue to prosper—under existing trade con- ditions. Who is bold enough to declare that we should have done better under Protection?

The town's meeting called by the Mayor to protest against Turkish misrule and atrocities in Macedonia was disappointing. The various religi- ous denominations were well represented, but the platform was not as influential as it ought to have been and the attendance of the general public was anything but satisfactory. The inclemency of the weather may have had something to do with this, but it appears to us that there may be other reasons. Can it be that the recent three yeara' war has Made us as a people callously indifferent to the sufferings of humanity? We trust that the sorrows and sufferings which were witnessed on the South African veldt have not so hardened us as to make us unresponsive to the cry of an op- pressed people. The terrible doings of the Turk- ish soldiery against the men, women and children 'of Macedonia should at least have arolsed a deeper feeling of horror and indignation than was indicated by the attendance at the meeting on Thursday. We trust that the appeal which is about to be made for the relief of the long- suffering Macedonians will meet with a more generous and practical response.

The Examiner, Saturday November 7, 1903

• Personal. Miss Cameron, the matron of the Fever Hospital has been appointed matron of the Royal Hospital City-road, London. _ _

PUBLIC BENEFIT, UNDER the CLOCK for BEST

BOOT .3 i1:1 NANCASHIRE.—Advt. Forthcoming Visit of the Grenadier Guards'

honour had been respected. For twenty-five years these people had endured their misery and it seemed almost as if one might say they had been deserted apparently both by God and man. Careless seemed to have been the great avenger—the heavens had been as brass, and there had been none to answer. Freedom and liberty had been on the scaffold and wrong and oppression had been on the throne. Yet, they had faith to believe that that scaffold would sway the future, and "behind the dim unknown, standeth God within the shadow keening watch above his own." What wonder was it that the people should revolt and that the slumberingifires of the Balkans should once again burst into a blaze? This revolution—justified if ever a resolution was justified—the Turks had striven to suppress with a savagery and brutality which had been characteristic of that nation ever since they dumped themselves on Europe. He did not mind whether we dumped them back—(laugh- ter) or whether we converted them there but as far as the Christian nations which they had tortured for such a long time were concerned, it was certainly time we clipped their claws. (Applause.) The state of things in Macedonia was not new. Bulgaria, Servia, Crete, and Bosnia had all hadto pass through this baptism of fire and blood before they gained their freedom. It was for every town in England to impress upon the Government that the state of things of 1878 should not be repeated in regard to Macedonia. (Applause.) He thought at the present time there was a great dallier in England of forgetting that righteousness was greater than prosperity, that freedom was more to be desired than riches, and that honour was a greater thing than gold. They all trusted that at this critical time, when an effort was being made to assuage the open wound in Macedonia, England ,to herself would be true, and true to her very best. (Applause). The resolution was carried unanimously. THE MAYOR ASKED TO OPEN A RELIEF FUND.

Alderman BENNETT moved :—That the Mayor be re- quested to open a fund in aid of thatnow established by the Balkan committee in London to meet the prevailing distress in Maeadonia. It was gratifying, he said, to find that there were some occasions in English life when men of all religions, and political opinions, and all shades of municipal life could unite as one man in the promotion of a definite object. There was a time however, when Englishmen would have faced much more than a foggy night to lift up their voices on behalf of an oppressed nationality. It seemed to him that it. was symptomatic of a very serious falling off in our national vigour that whentgreat causes demanded our sympathy and support we were not able to rise to the height of the old enthusiasm and make our protest with such vigour that even governments were compelled to listen. In regard to this question and the terrible tragedy in Armenia, he had felt ashamed of his country and very bitterly ashamed. Many times he had felt in- clined to agree with Mr. Gladstone's pamphlet in which he spoke of clearing out the Turk- "bag and baggage." (Applause.) If the Powers would not act England ought to act alone and if necessary accelerate the clearance which he felt would be for the good of every- one concerned. The fact that the people were in such a terrible state of oppression was due to England more than to any other nation; and if England took action he did not think Europe would dare to interfere. (Applause). Captain G. R. CEWSFIELD seconded. There were, he observed, many people who said "we have plenty of suffering at home. It is no use dissipating our charity and our energy by going and sending money to these distant lands.' He was quite sure they had heard enough that night to make them feel that there was far greater suffering in Macedonia than in the whole of England put together. The motion was unanimously carried. PROTECTING RELIEF WORKERS.

On the motion of the REV. MORLEY STEVENSON, seconded by the Rev. J. L, Milne the following resolu- tion was adopted :—" That this meeting desires to impress upon the Government the urgent need of pro- viding effective measures of protection to those engaged in rescue and relief. That copies of this resolution be also forwarded with copies of the first."

PROPOSED COLLECTION IN PLACES OF WORSHIP. .

In proposing a vote of thanks to the Mayor for con- vening the meeting and to the Rector for presiding, the Rev. John Yonge suggested that collections in aid of the relief fund should be made in the various places of worship in the town. The Macedonian outrages, he continued, were a matter of great concern to us as a nation, because we were solely responsible for the state of affairs. The Rev. J. F. MANTREWS seconded and the motion was carried. •

In responding the RECTOR cordially thanked Mr. C. E. Parker who initiated the movement and the Rev. John Yonge who had charge of the arrangements.

A military concert vfill be given in the Parr Hall on Sunday afternom, November 15th,- by the full band of his Majesty's Grenadier Guards. Farther particulars appear in our advertising columns.

Fire Brigade Practice. The members of the Fire Brigade had an interest- ing practice on Saturday afternoon at the Training College. One of the . most important features was the testing of the pressure of the water, and it was found that the supply from the adjoining brook was plentiful, and by means of the hose-pipes could be thrown over the top of the building. As the brigade was returning, after a successful practice, the fire escape came in contact with the tram lines in Church- street, and caused the wheel to collapse. five of the men had a narrow escape.

Death of Miss Minshull. Many of our readers will regret to hear of the painfully sudden death of Miss Gertrude Minshull, who was well known in the town through her connection with the Library as an assistant. She was seized with an attack of peritonitis onFriday night,and died Saturday night at the house of her sister,MrsSoble with whom she resided. The funeral took place on Tuesday afternoon at the Cemetery. Mr. Madeley (librarian) and Mr. Howarth (sub-librarian) were pre- sent as representing the Library staff and Mr. Alder- man Bennett on behalf of the Committee.

The Latchford Baptist Literary and Debat- ing Society. Impromptu speeches were the order at Monday evening's . meeting of this Society. " "Is it right to watch a pantomime?" "Con- trast courage with cowardice,' "What will be the result if Chamberlain's policy is adopted?" BIRD SHOW AT WARRINGTON.

Some of the best cage-birds in the country are to be seen at the eighth annual show in connection with the Warrington and- District Ornithological Society which was opened in the Assembly Room at the Conservative Club, Sankey-street, yesterday afternoon and will be continued to-day (Saturday). Excellent arrangements had been made by the officials with Mr. J. D. Leigh as secretary, and the show will rank as one of the most successful in the history of the society. The entries are particularly good in all sections,—totalling between 400 and 500—and in the classes for cage- birds they are double those of last year. The keenest competition is. in the section for Norwich canaries, there being as many as twenty-eight birds in one class. The judging was undertaken by Mr. A. Wellings, of Prescot,(cage birds), and Mr. H. A. Moore, of Lypint (pigeons). The special prize for the best bird 'lithe mgeon classes was awarded to Mr. E. Burrows, a well-known fancier, and Mr. J. D. Leigh carried off the prize for the best cage bird. The show was opened by Mr. W. H. J. Jenkins who alluded to the good work of the society and the excel- lence of the show.—A vote of thanks was accorded to him-on the proposition of Mr. P. Mather (President of the Society), seconded by Mr. J. D. Leigh. Owing to the late hour last night at •which the wards were issued we are unable to publish them..

WARRINGTON AND THE MANCHESTER SHIP CANAL COMPANY.

Another Proposal from the Company.

Notwithstanding the abrupt conclusion of the negotia- tions between the Warrington Corporation and local trader on the one part and the directors and officials of the Manchester Ship Canal Company on the other part, thtl company have again approached the Corporation suggesting that the proposed Manchester Ship Canal Bill in Parliament and the appeal which has been made by the Company against the decision of .Mr. Justice Byrne in the action of Crosfield and others v. The Man- chester Ship Canal Company should be allowed to pro- ceed together. The subject was considered at a meet- ing of the Rivers Committee on Thursday presided over by Mr. Alderman Bolton, and the extreme difficulty of the position was discussed. The general opinion was expressed that no assent should be given to the Com- pany's proposals pending the appeal in the action al- ready referred to. This appeal will be disposed of within a month and the position of the Corporation with reference to the Ship Canal Company's proposals at Warrington will then be re-considered.

THE MACEDONIAN ATROCITIES.

PROTEST MEETING AT WARRINGTON R2lief Fund to be Opened.

There was only a small attendance at the Town's meeting held in the Parr Hall on Thursday evening to protest against the atrocities in Macedonia. The Rector (the Rev. Canon Willis) presided and was supported on the platform by the Revs. John Yonge, Morley Steven- son, W. Bracecamp, G. H. Carnson, R. Wilkins Rees, W. C. J. Neville, J. S. Hughes, J. F. Matthews, H. Minnie() and J. F. Milne, Alderman Bennett, Captain G. R. Crosfield, and Mr. C. E. Parker. SPEECH BY THE RECTOR.

The RECTOR, in opening the proceedings said there were three points on which he would like to speak. First, the value of meetings like this; secondly,the duty of England on the present occasion; and thirtily, the great importance of wisdom. With regard to the first point, perhaps a single meeting might not do very much good, but meetings held generally throughout the country, would, he thought, bear upon the Government at this very important crisis. (Hear. hear.) Therefore the more meetings they could have, the better it would be. Such meetings as that gaye an opportunity for the expression of a righteous indignation, and they did feel that in England they were free to speak and that on occasions like the present it was a good thing that the people should speak from their very hearts so as to show their condemnation of what is going on at. the present time in Macedonia. (Hear, hear.) Secondly, with regard to the duty of England in the present crisis, he would say that from what he had read he thought our honour was at stake. Our word was practic- ally pledged twenty-five years ago that we would try to do our very utmost for the in- habitants of Macedonia who were under Turkish rule. He felt that humanity demanded intervention. (Hear, hear.) In case of a fire we should feel it our bounden duty at all costs to ourselves to try and rescue those who were in danger. So in the same way if a man was in danger of drowning we should feel it right to risk our life regardless of the dangers; or if there was a terrible epidemic of disease, the doctors and clergy would not think for a moment about risking their lives. Humanity demanded that we should intervene at the present moment regardless of all consequences in what lwe believed to be right. (Hear, hear.) Referring to the necessity of exercising wisdom, the Rector thought they would all remember Lord Bea,constield's view 25 , years ago and also Mr. Gladstone's view—both very different ones. Lord Beaconsfield's view was that the Turks should remain where they are. !Mr. Gladstone's view was a very different one—that the Turks should be packed off "bag and baggage" from the country. He (the Rector) must honestly say that he did not quite agree with either view. It seemed to him that we should have a greater , chance of reforming the Turks in their own country than we should if we drove them into another part of the world. He honestly thought that the Russians were as bad as the Turks. The Russians professed to be Christianised, yet their conduct in some of their Eastern provinces was revolting, and in one instance a quarter of the population of a town was driven into the river. Iffp did not trust Russian conduct or the Russian characterr'for truth. If we drove the Turks out, Russia would take Turkey,and if the Russians came through the Black Sea into the Meditertanean they would be able to prevent our access through the Suez Canal to India. It was not simply from the Imperial point of view that he regarded the importance ,of India,but he felt that India was a trust which God had given us and it was our duty to try and Christianise that enormous population of three hundred millions. Russia would not hesitate. She wanted to get into the Pacific and also into the Indian oceans. He could not help thinking she would be delighted to get hold of India. In India there were 50 millions of Moham- medans, who acknowledged the rule of the Sultan. If i they bore these things n mind they would see how necessary it was that England should be very careful how she acted. It was right to do our very utmost at the present crisis to strengthen the hands of the Government and bring pressure to bear on the Govern- ment to act in concert with the great Europoan powers to stop this horrible state of things which was going on in Macedonia. (Hear, hear). Mr. H. J. Toast, a deputation from the Balkan Corn- mitts", endorsed the remarks of the Chairman as to the value of meetings of that kind. There was no doubt what- ever that the Government was very greatly helped in the present crisis by the voice of public opinion, and Lord Lansdowne, theOad every reason to believe, was exert- ing a very considerable amount of pressure upon the European powers to induce them to accept the policy of the Western powers in regard to the crisis in Macedonia. Lout Lansdowne's influence in the counsels of Europe was undoubtedly affected by the knowledge these powers had that British public opinion was behind him. One of the features of the move- ment was its international character for it was only ten days since that there was an immense meeting at Paris. The speaker expressed satisfaction that Warrington was going to inaugurate a relief fund for the afflicted • Macedonians' remarking:that unless we sent money many would perish during the winter. Mr. Torr in giving the reasons which had led the Macedonians to rebel laid stress upon threepoints, i.e., the iniquitous land system, the unjust judicial system and the fundamental differ- ence of opinion between the Turk's conception of the province and function /of woman andlthe Western or Christian idea. He pointed out that if an Englishman

saw his father or mother or sister treated in the way in which many Macedonians had been treated he would not go out with ;11. squirt of rosewater, but would stake all and give up his life gladly in rebellion. For the continuance of the iniquitous system which prevailed in Macedonia we were very largely responsible owing to the adoption of Lord Beaconsfield's policy at the time when the treaty of San Stefano was made by Russia. Proceeding he said he was very anxious that there should be no disagreement between powers which would give the Turk an opportunity of slipping out of the net. He believed that the presence of the British, French and Italian fleets at Smyana. wonld bring the Sultan to his knees in a moment. For England's sake in the long run in the great reckoning of history,it was for the people of this country to support the cause of oppressed Macedonia with all the enthusiasm at their command and do everything in their power to strengthen His Majesty's goverpment. (Applause.) The RECTOR then moved the following resolution "That this meeting of the inhabitants of Warrington views with horror and indignation thepresent condition of Macedonia. and recognising the great responsibility resting upon the signatories of the Treaty of Berlin it desires by all means in its power to further the en- deavours of his Majesty's Government, in concert with those of tke other great Powers, to press upon the Government of Turkey such measures as may secure the immediate cessation of the present state of anarchy, and give adequate guarantees for the future good government of the unhappy districts affected. That copies of this resolution be sent to the Prime Minister, the Secretary of State for Foreign Affairs, the Member for the Borough and the Secretary of the Balkan Com- mittee in London."

The Rev. FATHER Wtr.soN, seconded. He observe that if the people who were undergoing such suffering were pagans and savages of the most degraded type,they would still claim our pity and intervention. (Hear, hear.) The very bond of our common humanity would demand that. But when it was remembered that these were persons of a high type, that they were fellow- Christians with ourselves, and that there was.a deliber- ate and persistent policy of extermination being carried out—and that principally for the reason that they were Christians—surely we must feel ourselves impelled to rise up and do what we could to put an end to their sufferings. We were also bound to these poor people by the more stringent bond of a definite responsibility. If it had not been for theaction of England at the congress of Berlin the Macedonians would ere this in all probabil- ity have worked out for themselves their political salvation and would have been at this time prosperous and happy. (Hear, hear.) By the action of England at that time—an actiondictated by no means by unseash motives and clamourously approved by the people— the load of responsibility unquestionalily rested with the people of England. (Hear, hear.) Until they made their voice heard loudly and deeply in indignant protest, continued the speaker, they would not be in a position to throw stones at the Government: In a matter of this kind especially the Government was almost bound to wait for the voice of the people and it was only from the electorate that the Government could acquire that weight and impetus which would , make intervention effectual. (Applause.) Mr. C. E. PARKER. supported, and remarked that whatever differences they might have—Liberal or Con- servative, Churchman or Nonconformist—the Christian feeling of England was such that when they saw anation down-trodden, robbed, and despoiled they were all happy and willing to might onthe sameplatform,and in the same!spirit to bind up those wounds and pour in the I oil and wine. They had met not for the purpose of ' blowing up the Government, but for the purpose of backing them up. They believed that from the Prime Minister downwards they were all earnestly anxious that this pestilential simoon of cruelty which had swept over Macedonia and was desolating and devastat- ing the whole country should be stopped, and that right speedily. It was very important that the Government in the actiOn they must take should be backed up by the moral influence of the people, so • that their action should be strong, vigorous and earnest. They wanted abovelall things that in this matter the Government should hold the honour of England high. (Hear, hear). In the treaty of Berlin distinct proposals were made to the Macedonians that their liberties, religions and civil, should be respected, and in thost respects the treaty was a good one. The only pity about it was, that those provisions which were specially put in to compel Turkey to govern properly had not been en- forced by the powers. (Hear, hear.) The condition of Macedonia since the treaty was signed had been that of perpetual, organized tyranny. Freedom had been in chains; justice had been a by-word ; the poor and needy had been oppressed, and neither life, property, nor

THE MAYORALTY.

Councillor William Pierpoint Refuses and Alderman Bolton Accepts.

The subject of the Mayoralty was under discussion at the caucus meeting of the Town Council held on Wednesday evening. A deputation consisting of Alder- man Smethurst, Councillors Tinnion, Dodds and Craik was appointed to wait upon Councillor William Pier- point and again ask him to accept the Mayoralty, and in case of refusal to wait upon Alderman Bolton with a similar request. Yesterday afternoon, the deputation waited upon Councillor William Pierpoint lin the Mayor's Parlour at the Town hall. They again pressed Mr. Pierpoint to accept the Mayoralty, especially after the unani- mous expression of opinion by the members of the Council on Wednesday night. Mr. PIERPOINT replied that he valued very highly the honour that the Council wished to confer upon him, especially as he was born in Warrington and as many of his ancestors .were closely identified with the town. Nothing would have given him greater pleasure than to have acceded to their wishes, but he felt that he had now to consider that he had only a few brief years in which he could devote himself to his important and growing business, and recent experience had taught him that it would require considerably more of his personal attention than had hitherto been the case, at any rate for the next few years and more particularly during the forthcoming year. He felt that he would be called upon to go abroad and be absent from the Borough oftener than he had been in the past, and therefore he regretted that he could not afford the time which was absolutely necessary for the perform- ance of the duties of Mayor. He added that if when he had got his bush Ass settled and probably had a little more leisure, nothing would give him greater pleasure if the, Council saw fit to ask him then to accept the position of Mayor. The deputation then waited upon Alderman Bolton and pointed out that the resolution passed by the Conn- cil, while applying to Councillor Pierpoint, equally applied to Alderman Bolton, now that the former had declined to accept the position. They had pledgedJ themselves—and felt that they could pledge the Council —to give Aldarman Bolton loyal and unanimous sup- port in the event of him accepting the -Mayoralty. In reply, Alderman BOLTON said that if the Council would accept him as Mayor with all his failures—of which he knew as well as anyone else—he would do all he possibly could to make his year of office as successful as possible. He would leave nothing undone that he could do to promote the best interests of the Council and the Borough. .He felt it was a very great responsibility that he was taking upon himself, but after the very free expression of opinion from the deputation and the promise of the utmost loyalty on the part of the Council, he felt he would be able to carry out the duties successfully. He therefore placed himself entirely in their hands and accepted the position. A meeting of the Town Council will be held on Monday at 12 o'clock, when Alderman Bolton will be appointed Mayor of the Borough.

WARRINGTON'S HIGH DEATH IRATE.

The Registrar General reports that last week War- rington had a higher death rate than any of the seventy- six great towns in England and Wales: The weekly report of the Medical Officer of liealth (Dr. J. Guest Gornall) states that the rate was 31.7 per 1,000 against 17.7 theaverage of the previous four weeks and 22.2 thecorresponding period of last year :—The death-rate from the seven chief zymotic diseases was equal to 12.3 per 1,000, against 6.1 the average rate of the previ- ous four weeks against .7 the corresponding period of 1902. The deaths included-13 from measles. The following cases of infectious diseases were reported daring the week :—Scarlet fever, 15 cases; diphtheria, 2: enteric fever, 1; erysipelas, 1. The total number of, patients in hospital during the week ending 31st October was 52.

"SUNNY SOUTH" BAZAAR.

A WORTHY EFFORT AT BANK QUAY.

A Satisfactory :esponse

To fancy oneself transported to Sunny Spain, whilst at the same time sojourning in the district of Bank Quay during a November fog, requires a wide stretch of the imagination, but at the present moment the friends of the Workingmen's Mission are making praiseworthy efforts by means of a pictorial bazaar to delight the eyes of their visitors by a pleasing repre- sentation of the beauties of Spain, and at the same time to induce them to lend assistance in raising funds for the erection of a new chapel. The work of decorat- ing the schoolroom in Thewlis-street, where the bazaar is being held, had been placed in capable hands and the prettiest effect Possible had been produced. Scenes at Gibraltar, Vittoria, Barcelona, Seville, and other interesting places in Spain were faithfully depicted. As one wandered round the old-tima buildings, radiant in the golden light of a warmer clime, the onlooker's fancy revelled, as was aptly stated in the handbook, "in thoughts of deep blue skies, sublime scenery, orange groves, shady palm trees, pomegranate blossoms, dark-eyed ladies with jet black tresses and lace mantillas, gay cavaliers, black- browed priests, and grand cathedrals. Spain is the land of romance. It is the country of Don Quixote, of the Cid, of Christopher Columbus, and of the Peninsular War. It is the land of song and story, of love and daring, of enterprise long since calmed down, of dominion passed away."

THE OBJECI'S OF' THE BAZAAR.

In an illustrated handbook,printed at the "Examiner" Office, the objects of the bazaar were clearly set forth on behalf of the committee by Mr. Thomas Ashley (hon. treasurer), Mr. Alfred Tilling jun.(hon secretary), and Mr. George Hough (assistant hon secretary). "It will be generally known," said these officials, "that the ekist- ing Mission Cha,p3l which was built in 1871 was the property of the late Mr, John Crosfield (founder of the Mission) and that, being on the works ground, at a later period it became absorbed into the firm ofiXessrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sons, Limited. Through very great pressure of business, necessitating extension in several departments, the position occupied by the pre- sent church building is now required for works pur- poses. An intimation to this effect (evidently given with reluctance) was received from the Directors some months ago, and shortly afterwards (preliminary arrangements having been made for the erection of a new building) the Diractors gave a further intimation that the firm would subscribe £3OO to the fund. Mrs. Crosfield also promised £lOO and in additional £lOO to the proposed new organ. Tne men's class in connection Iwith the institution guaranteed £5O. and Mr. H. Roberts, J.P., promised .£lO in addition to defraying the expenses of the architect. Several small amounts have been realized from miscellaneous sources amount- ing to about £25. The total cost of the new chapel including organ will be about £1,200. Total amount realized and promised to date £585, so that we still require to raise approx £615. In our Bazaar effort we are hoping to realise £3OO. It is an undoubted fact that the present building, and the services that have I been held Sunday after Sunday within its walls, have proved an inestimable boon to the neighbourhood of Bank Quay, and it would be a sad loss indeed if such 1 services had to be discontinued. That there is a necessity for the new building to take the place of the present structure is evidenced by the large congre, gations—workingmen and women—which assemble in the chapel Sunday after Sunday, thus proving that the non-sectarian lines on which the Mission was:commenced, by the founder (Mr. Crosfield), and on which it has all along been run, have appealed to the sympathies of a gre at many people in the neighbourhood. The erection of the new building has been placed in the hands of Mr. George Wallington, who is working to plans pre- pared by Mr. R. M. Roberts, A.R.1.8.A._, and the build- ing operations are being superintended by Mr. John Hassan. A special feature of the new property will be its investiture in a trusteeship, appointed by the church members, by which the property will be held in the name of the Mission. We appeal with confidence to our many friends to assist us in this, our first bazaar effort. Many claims have been made on the public lately, but we trust our cause will not be less deserving of your help, and find a ready response."

THE OPENING CEREMONY.

SPEECHES BY MRS. CROSFIELD AND MR. A. H. CROSPIELD.

The duty of performing•the opening ceremony on Thursday, devolved upon Mrs. Crosfield, who, for many years, has taken a deep interest in the mission. The chair was occupied by Mr'. A. H. Crosfield, and he was accompanied onthe platform by Mrs. Crosfield. Miss T. Crosfield, Mrs. Roome, Mr. H. Roberts J.P., Councillor James Evans, Councillor Barton, Mr. N. THE ENTERTAINMENTS &c.

There were numerous attractions and sideshows. A excellent orchestral performed a choice selection o classical and popular music each evening. in addition, vocal items were contributed at intervals. On Thurs- day evening, a male voice quartette party sang glees, and Mr. A. White gavea conjuring entertainment. Last night the Primitive Methodist choir, under the leadership of Mr. A. Spruce, sang several selections. One of the most interesting sideshows was that devoted to curiosities. Thero was an excellent display of model engines, steamers, curios, and skins collected in foreign lands. Mr. Clatworthy showed two interesting mod 31s of engines made of cardboard and Mr. Dobbins displayed a model of a locomotive. Another interesting ex- hibit was the model of a tramline junction improvement by Mr. Ward, whereby the points are worked mechanically from the car. Other attractions include marionettes, a shooting range, a fishing bridge, throwing games, an electric battery in charge of Mr. Dobbins and a powerful microscope in charge of Mr. A. Ashley. A doll dressing competition was won by Miss Olive Dale and a knitting competition by Mrs. Benton.

The proceeds on Thursday realised. the satisfactory sum of .£ll5 Bs.

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

CRUELTY TO A CHILD AT EARLESTOWN—SMART PENALTY. At the Warrington Occasional Court on Thursday afternoon, before Captain Withington (presiding) and Mr. T. Halton, Charles English, Crow-lane West, Hades- town, was charged on a warrant with illtreating and neglecting his child Henrietta, aged6l years—Mr. J, IV. Knowles prosecuted on behalf of the N.S.P.C.C.—Evi- dence was given to the effect that on September 26th, prisoner flogged the girl with a strap. The child was examined by Dr. Gauge who said considerable violence had been used. The man was of drunken habits and spent money in gambling—Prisoner was further charged with assaulting his step-daughter aged 18—It appeared that when prisoner was flogging the other child his step-daughter interfered, whereupon he struck her on the face with a strap, knocked her down and kicked her, and she was obliged to leave the house—ln the first case prisoner was fined £5 and costs with the option of six weeks imprisonment and he was ordered to pay the costs in the second case. ~t,

Wright, and Geddes. , A CLERK ASSAULTED.—Patrick Malone, last from Winwick, was charged with having been drunk and dis- orderly in Sankey-street at 11.30 on Saturday night and with assaulting john BeMeld, a clerk residing at 23, Mill-lane.—The evidence showed that BeHeld went into Mr. Greenwood's chip potato shop in Sankey-street ' along with a companion named Hawthorne. Prisoner came in, and after disputing with Hawthorne, struck at him. The blow missed Hawthorne and BeMeld was struck in the mouth. Prisoner afterwards followed BeWield to Dixon-street, and knocked him down.—P.C. Hughes came upon the scene, and prisoner was taken into cnstody.—A fine of 10s. and costs was imposed. MAINTENANCE ARREARS.—Stephen Collins, 22. Ship yard, and James Deveraux, Boardman's yard, were both summoned for arrears of maintenance in respect of their children who had been committed to Industrial schools. The amount in Collins case was £1 18s 6d., and in DeVeraux's 15s.—The Bench ordered the money to be paid immediately, or defendants to go to goal for one month each. DRUNK ON LICENSED PREMISES.—John Ryan, 3, Heath-lane, was charged with being drunk and incapable on the licensed prenises of Peter Chrimes, of the Roebuck Inn, Bridge-street. on Tuesday. He pleaded. guilty.—Sergeant Barnett stated that at 9.2.5 on Tues- day morning he visited the Roebuck Inn, in company with Police-constable Whalley. They found the prisoner very drunk and leaning on the counter with a glass of whisky and tea before him. He drank some of it in their presence. They had a conversation with the landlady, and took the prisoner, who could not walk without assistance, to the Police Station.—The Chief Constable said that as the case would probably form the subject of a further prosecution he would not go into further particulars.—Prisoner was fined 7s. and costs, -or seven days' hard labour. PROTECTION AND THE LAND

QUESTION.

BY J. FLETCHER MOULTON, K.C., M.P.

Nothing is more worthy of note in Mr. Chamber- lain's" raging, tearing propaganda" than his silence as to the bearing of his scheme on the Land question. Take his prop lsed tax on corn, modestly fixed at 2s. per quarter to begin with, but which it is easy to prophesy would soon be raised to a much higher figure. This is the essential feature of his original scheme. On it depends his power to give a substantial preference to Canada, the colony from which he hopes the most. The other features of his later and mare complete scheme have varied from time to time, but this tax on corn has remained con- stant and prominent throughout. Hence we may fairly conclude that he has weighed well its signifi- cance and consequences. It is not unjust to him to take it as something more than a casual suggestion. Now we all know how and by wham such a tax is actually borne and paid. The payment at the Custom House does not show how the tax falls; it is merely an advance repaid to the merchant on his re-sale of the corn. The pries of the corn is raised by the amount of the tax throughout all the subsequent transactions until it comes to the actual consumer. He pays a price wfiich includes the tax, and no one repays him. On his shoulders the burden of the tax actually rests. If the tax is at such a rate that it amounts to a farthing on so much corn as goes to make a quartern loaf, th price of each quartern loaf that it bought is raised at least one farthing. That the Corn Tax will raise the price of corn in this wa: cannot be denied. We get such a large amount from foreign sources that there is no ground for thinking that the foreigner will lower the net price at which he sells it to us because we choose to tax it. The tax must be paid by the consumer in the shape of a rise in price. And, indeedAthis is calculated upon by Mr. Chamberlain in claiming that his Corn Tax will benefit the colonies. If it did not raise the price the gain to them would vanish. So that both sides accept that the price of corn will be raised by the tax. To render our ideas more precise, let as put it into figures. Roughly, the amounts of corn closumed in England and the origin of same are as follows:

Millions of cwts

Foreign Countries Colonies 35 Home 155 Mr. Chambevlain wishes to give a preference of,about, 6d. a cwt. to Coloniel-grown corn, and he therefore puts a tax to this amount on all imported corn which comes from foreign countries. He rightly concludes that this will raise the price of corn by the amount of the tax, so that the Colonist will get to this extent an increase in the price of his corn. _ _ _ _ .

But the tax is paid and borne when the loaf is bought by the man who buys it, and it is no matter where the corn came from out of which the loaf is made. So the tax raises the selling price of all corm— the home-grown corn equally with the Colonial and foreign. Hence the whole rise in cost of corn to the consumer,which is due to the tax is as follows : Millions of cwts. Amount of tax. Foreign Corn 132 ... £3,300,000 Colonial Home

These are the extra sums which the consumer pays. Who gets them? The amount of the tax on the foreign corn goes into the Treasury. That on Colonial corn goes into the pockets of the Colonists who occupy themselves in growing corn. But what becomes of the biggest of the three—amounting to well-nigh half of the total burden—that which cor- responds to the home-grown corn? Is there any doubt to whom it goes ? ,None whatever. The plan is not put forward as a temporary expedient, but as a permanent policy. We must therefore consider the effect of a permanent addition to the price of corn, due to no increase in the cost of growing it, but purely to the imposition of a tax on imported and competing corn. This increase must eventually go wholly to the landlords, in the form of increased rents. In other words, the tax of 2s. per quart2r on corn means ultimately an increase in the rents of agricultural land at home of at least 1:3,875,000—a figure which must be increased should the tax stimulate the growth of Icorn .at home. _ _ _

I have said that we are entitled to assume that Mr. Chamberlain has thought out the consequences of his tax, so that he must be taken to have realized this fact. But we are not left to conjecture whether he does or does not know that a tax on corn means a rise in rents. We- have abundant evidence from his former speeches that he knows it well. I need only quote two of his speeches made in the old days: 1. What is it that they (the Tories) have to offer us besides a vigorous foreign policy, which might perchance find places for some of their younger sons ; or a tax on the fool of the people, which, would undoubtedly raise their rents ? And 2. The owners of property, those who are inter- ested in the existing state of things, the men who have privileges `..0 maintain, would be glad to en- trap from the right path by raising the cry of Fair Trade, under which they cover their demand for Protection,and in connection- with which they would tax the food of the people in order to raise the rents of the landlord. Mr. Chamberlain has thrown overboard most of his past utterances on Free Trade,by alleging that times have changed, and that the existence of high tariffs in foreign countries justifies him in announcing all his former principles. But this is not a matter which depends in any way on foreign tariffs. It is simply a question of the economic results of a tax on the import of corn into our own country; and what was true of it in 1885 must be equally true now, and he must knew that it is so. To do him justice, I do ,not think that he has ever denied it. But he has maintained an absolute silence on the point. Why? He has taken the responsibility of urging his fellow-countrymen thus to tax themselves, and yet he says not one word to warn them that almost one- half of this self-imposed burden will go into the pockets of English landlords. He dwells much on the amount of benefit that our colonies will reap, whereas for every shilling that goes into their pockets 4s. 6d. goes to English landlords, as to which he says nothing. Yet in olden days he was the foremost to give this note of warning. Why do we no longer hear it from him? There is one and only one answer. Then he was on the side of the peoble and nlw he is on the 'side of .the lani lords. No man can serve two masters, and he now serves the landlord party, by whose help he hopes to carry his scheme. So he must keep from his hearers the unpleasant fact that the burdens he is laying on the poor will go to pay his rich supporters. Two reflections force themselves on us. What are we to think of the rich landlords themselves who ap- pear on Mr. Chamberlain's platform, support his pro- paganda, and form part of the Government which is de facto headed by him? Their cry is that as patriots each Englishman should cheerfully accept the sacri- fices which they say we must make to preserve our Empire. By joining Mr. Chamberlain they shew that they are ready to accept on their own behalf this community of sacrifice. And yet they know, but do not say, that the burden they ask their fellow-citizens to take on their shoulders is not only one that they will not touch with one of their fingers,but one out of which they calculate to make a huge revenue for them- selves. And lastly as to Mr. Chamberlain himself. .No man could have put himself into a position in which he was more absolutely bound in honour to make an honest and full disclosure of all the consequences of his scheme of which he has knowledge. The audi- ences he addresses are largely composed of men who have too hard a struggle in life to'have time for the study of economics, and they do not know of this secret profit to his supporters. And he is silent.

J. FT,ETCH/ER MOULTON.
