The Examiner, Saturday, November 7, 1903 MR. R. H PEARSON'S CONT. LIBERAL MEETING AT Me. Ella Russell and Concert-Party STOCKTON HEATH. at Parr Hall. SPEECHES BY MR. KING, M. A. H. CROSFIELD AND MR, C.E. PARKER.

Remembering that Madame Ella Russell was the only member of the concert-party at the Parr Hall on Tues- day evening who was known to Warrington audiences it was a very agreeable surprise to discover that all- round excellence in regard to all concerned was the predominating feature of the concert. The audience was not large but it was always unusually demonstrative and often enthusiastic in its discriminations of what was more or less meritorious in the performances of the various artistes. The solo instrumentalists were particularly good. The Dvonik Sonatine served to shew how entirely en rapport were Miss Muriel Elliot and Mr. Louis Pecskai in their associated capacities as pianist and violinist, and, though it opened the pro- gramme and might therefore have had in the ordinary course of things an indifferent hearing, this item quickly awakened interest and was listened to with the closest attention. Later Miss Elliot, as a piano execu- taut, evinced rare ability coupled with keen artistic insight in the Beethovian "Polonaise" (op. 29) and the Marche Militaire (Schnbert-Tausig). As a violin soloist too, Mr. L. Pecskai's pre-eminence was clearly something of a surprise to many in the audience. Rarely has Warrington heard a more finished player. The rounds of applause and the vociferous encores that greeted his rendering of Schubert's " Am Meere,"Bach's Prelude in E, and Hubay's " Hulldmz6 Balaton," were but a well-deserved tribute to genius, for, in point of purity of intonation (in involved doubled-stopped passages especially), in crispness and delicacy of execu- tion and in an all-prevailing depth of feeling, a des- cription of Mr. Pecskai's performances must cer- tainly be punctuated with many superlatives. Of the songs by Mr. Mans Hale it may be truthfully said that all attested that gentle- man's high capabilities as a vocalist. We cannot, however, confess to a feeling of deep interest in "Myself when young "(L. Lehmann) howeveradequate the interpretation of so inconsequential an item may have been. "The Yeomen of England" (Merrie England) was sometning worthier of effort, and here Mr. Hale's splendid voice and style were unmistakably evidenced. Mi. John Coates's singing charms more than anything else by reason of its downright earnest- ness. Though exception might be taken to the invita- tion to Maud to come into the garden" as now a little time-worn, it was impossible not to be moved by the contagious verve thrown into its presentment. Nor need there be any esitation in expressing admira- tion of Mr. Coates's fine tenor voice and manly deportment. I His encore item " I'll sing thee songs of Araby " and Coleridge-Taylor's fine song " Eleanore " were both tastefully interpre- ted. Miss Rosa Olitzka essayed the trying excerpt "Mon cceur &c.," from Saint-Saeas's • Samson et Dania " with rare dramatic force, albeit it is a thankless task to invest with adequate intensity of feeling so stirring an incident as that embodied in the text when thus severed from its context and sur- roundings. In the subdued" Sea cradle song " (Zardo) one had opportunity, later, of learning how sweetly and sympathetically Miss Olitzka could interpret a com- position which while so unostentatious in character may yet be so intrusically meritorious. Coming lastly, but the reverse of disrepectfully, to the performances of Madame Ella Russell herself, it is due to say that she charmed alike by her fine presence as by her artistic vocalization of the various items set down to her name:in the programme. The dramatic scena "Adonas "written for her by Mr. Landon Ronald gives ample scope for the display of Madame Russell's strofig points, though the composition must be said to fall short of its obvious strivings after pure emotionalism. In response to a loud recall Madame Russell sang IGounod's "Ave Maria" (the obbligato being played by Mr. Pecskai) with deep andappropriate devotional feeling. Everything that Madame Russell did elicited the highest encomiums and served to sus- tain the high repute in which she is deservedly held here as elsewhere. The spirited quartet "Un di, se ben" (Rigoletti), in which all the vocalists joined, concluded a most enjoyable concert, in course of which it was discerned that Mr. Hamilton Harty was a thoroughly I capable accompanist.

A well attended meeting was held in the Providence Schoolroom, Chapel-lane, Stockton Heath on Tuesday evening, under the auspices of the Knutsford Division Liberal Association. The chair was occupied by Mr. A. H. Crosfield,:and he was accompanied on the platform by Mr. A. J. King (the prospective Liberal candidate for the division), Mr. C. E. Parker, Mr. Joseph Moun- field, Mr. R. Lloyd Jones, Mr. A. H. Dunn, Mr. Joseph Monks, jun., Mr. E. England, Rev. J. S. Hughes, Mr. W. J. Brown,and Mr. Joseph Smith (secretary). SPEECH BY MR. A. H. CROSFIELD.

The CHAIRMAN (Mr. A. H. Crosfleld) who was cordi- ally received said: I thank you for your kind reception and I feel it a privilege to take the chair here to-night for my friend and your friend and candidate Mr. King. I admire as lam sure yoa admire his pluck in fighting what is or rather I will say, what was regarded as an uphill battle. Time was when this constituency was looked upon as a forlorn hope; but times are changed and if you get the main facts of the great issues of the day well "understanded of the people" in this constitu- ency I think you ought to win hands down. (Applause.) Because I doubt if within the recollection of anyone here the Liberal party ever went to the country with a stronger and wiser programe than the programme which Sir Henry Campbell-Banuerman in his powerful speech at Bolton, and Mc Asquith in speech; after speech of great ability and other prominent leaders of our party have put before the country. And who is better qualified to expound that programme and drive true facts home than our candidate and friend Mr. King? (Applause.) He is no mere party or social figurehead but a man thoroughly master of the political subjects of the day, and with a business training which at the present juncture is of especial service to him. (Hear, hear.) Well, lam not myself going through this Party programme. I have heard a Chairman's duty defined in various ways, and the last definition, if not very elegant is pithy and to the point. A Chairman lam told ought to "Stand up, speak up and shut up." (Laughter.) Still before completing that definition perhaps you will allow me to say a word or two on the senipiternal question which at present fills men's minds sleeping or waking. I presume that it is not necessary to give it a name. People say we Liberals are not ready to listen to argu- ment on the Fiscal question, or to treat the subject scientifically. I admit neither the one nor the other. Free Traders we are, because as the President of our Warrington Association, whom we are very glad to have on this platform to-night, put it the other day, the "Free Trade system is founded upon a rock."

matter if the figures are wrong is worth noting. But we are not to be caught by chaff of that kind. The figures are to the argument what the foundation is to the house. (Hear, hear.) If the figures and foundation are unsound, argument and house collapse'. Is there not a good deal of down right audacity in the attempt to pull down the whole fabric of our commerce as it at present exists by arguments based on figures that are utterly mis- leading, and statements that are utterly inaccurate. (Hear,hear.)

Many years ago a quaker came from Liverpool to call on a quaker relation of mine in Warrington, once well-know in Sankey-street, and the visitor said, "Friend I will write thee epitaph," lam not going to quote you the whole of that epitaph, but I will give you two lines of it. They run as follows: "Of impudenee he had the most abundant share His faults were many and his virtues rare." (Laughter.) I don't think the visitor made his exit by the window, but I am told he did retire rather abruptly by the door. Well I. have been reminded of that couplet a good deal lately, and I recommend you to give Mr. Chamberlain's policy a reply that shall be just as speedy just or emphatic as ray quaker relative gave to his friend.

lam afraid I have failed rather badly to fulfil the definition which I began by quoting to you, but I have been tempted to give Mr. King what I feel he deserves from every one in this constituency, as strong and a hearty backing as one is able, and it has been a pleasure to me to try and help him a little in Stockton Heath, more especially because for so many years my father was closely associated with and took a deep interest in the place. (Applause.) I have now great pleasure in calling upon Mr. King to address you.

ADDRESS BY MR. KING. A VOTE OF NO CONFIDENCE IN THE GOVERNMENT. THE PROGRESS OF WARRINGTON.

Mr. KING, at the outset, thanked Mr. Crosfield for the very kind and hearty manner in which he was always so willing to come to the assistance of the Liberal cause in that part of the division. That was the third time within twelve months on which he (Mr. King) had had the assistance of Mr. Crosfield at his meetings. He (Mr. King) was not the Liberal candidate for the division but his positionhad always been that they were determined to fight this constituency at the next elec- tion and if the Liberals could find someone more cap- able than himself he (Mr. King) was prepared to stand down, but failing that he had pledged himself to fill the -gap. (Applause.) He moved the following resolution —"That this meeting condemns the past and present policy of his Majesty's Government, and declares that the revelations of the War Commission, the Educatioa Act, and, above all, the Fiscal policy forshadowed by the Premier and ex-Colonial Secretary have destroyed any claim they might have had to the confidence of the country." In referring to Warrington, Mr. King said it would always hold a very warm place in his heart from the fact that it was the birth place of his mother and the town of which he had many of,his earliest recollections. (Hear, hear.) He could not help con- trasting the Warrington as he knew it 30 or 35 years ago with the Warrington as he saw it to-day. During the interval the Cheshire Lines railway station and the Bank Quay station had been built. He remembered the old Arpley station which he saw still existed, , and which he expected to see turned into a museum of antiquities. (Laughter.) A wooden shed on the high level line at Bank Quay served as the station for that line when he knew Warrington in its early days. Since then two stations had been built. Railway companies did not conduct their business on principles of philanthropy and it occurred to him that there must be evidence of growing commerce, industry and population in the old town of Warrington to induce these companies to make that large expenditure of capital. (Hear, hear.) Look- ing back also to those early years he recollected the narrow paved streets, but now he found expensive boulevards with trams passing to and fro, and the widening of streets was still going on. On all sides he saw in Warrington no signs of,that descrepitude and decay that were so graphically described by Mr. Chamberlain at Liverpool. He saw on the other hand features of prosperity. The statement of Mr. Chamber- lain was an insult to the intelligence of the Borough. He thought they would prefer to rely, as did their fathers, upon their own industry, intelligenceand enter- prise—(applause)—and required no Government protec- tion to enable them to compete successfully in their own markets and in the markets of the world. (Ap- plause.) Mr. Chamberlain had said, if he remembered rightly, that the wire-drawing industry of Warrington was in a. very decayed and decrepit condition ; that they were being beaten out of the market by German com- petition, and as a remedy for that they must have pro- tective duties. He (Mr. King) did not know very much about the wire trade but he understood that some of the wireworks had increased very largely in area. He thought that the first stage of the raw material was what was termed "steel billets" and the second was "rolled bars." But if they protected wire they would have a claim for the protection of the steel billets, and if they protected those there would then be a claim for the:i protection of the rolled bars, and he under- stood that a very large portion of the wire drawn in Warrington was made from German roll& bars, the reason being that they were more suitable, more ductile and cheaper than our own. If they started by protecting and putting on duties against what they called" manufactured imports "they would find that they were interfering with some of their own tradeswhich used those imports as raw material,as • in the case of the German bars. Mr. Chamberlain said he would,not do anything of that kind until he had had a conference of representatives of all trades represen- tatives of workmen, and representatives of, the colonies The:representatives would all meet. in London and give their points, and then someone—he ' did not envy the minister—was going to sift out from that babble some kind of a fiscal policy which would be suitable to them all. (Laughter). Again, Mr. Chamberlain said that in protective coun- tries, when trade was bad, they did not find the works being closed or the workmen dismissed, but the work proceeded as usual and then they dumped down their surplus stock into this country. In reply to that he might say he had just'read thatiin a:German iron works "280 employes had beeedismissed through bad trade,and there was every probability of thousands of others being disanissed." Therefore, it was a monstrous iniquity to tell the people of the country that with Protection they would have constant employment in good or bad trade. (Hear, hear.) Warrington, he understood, was interested in the glass trade, and it might be well to refer to Mr. Chamberlain's remarks about it. The glass trade, he understood, depended very largely on the supply of cheap alkali. Mr. Chamberlain pretended that he was going to improve the glass trade by putting duties on foreign glass, and yet, in a speech at Newcastle, he referred to the bad condition of the alkali trade and spoke of putting big duties on foreign alkali. How could they improve the glass trade if they put duties on alkali on which, as he had said, theyilargely depended? The price of alkali would be increased as the result of , the duty, and it would then be worse for the glass ' trade instead of better. Alkali was used in other trades and Mr. Crosfield knew something about it as it waF. used in the trade in which he was concerned. English soap was known all over the world, and even if they went to the pyramids in Egypt they saw Pear's soap advertised. (A Voice: "And Crosfield's too" laughter). That was because we had the privilege,which no other nation had, of buying our raw materials where we could buy them best, and as long as we retained that privilege and used our best intelligence and enterprise we need fear no competition in the world. (Applause.) One of the great difficulties which our manufacturers had to contend with was the question of carriage and railway rates, and it was 'certainly a hardship that the foreigner should be able to export stuff from the middle of Germany and get it delivered into this country at a less rate than they cotild deliver it from Warrington to Manchester or Birmingham. (Cries of "Shame.") Referring to that magnificent waterway the Ship Canal and to the benefits which Manchester had derived from it, he said it had been a cause of wonder to him that Warrington had not made more use of it than they had. It he might make a suggestion he would say that if they would convert the Atpley meadows into huge dock basins and invite the merchant fleets of the world there —(hear,hear)—they would do more for the prosperity of Warrington than by supporting the will-of-the-wisp suggestions of Mr. Chamberlain. (Applause.) Some years ago the Americans put on a very high tariff against woollen goods, and since that time the exports had fallen very considerably. Against this they had the very strange and remarkable fact that the consumption of wool of all kinds in this country had increased from 392 million pounds in 1887 to 578 millions in 1902. Therefore it was not fair to imagine that the prosperity of the country depended upon our export trade only. Our own workmen were engaged in manufacturing for our own"countrymen and not for our friends across the sea. (Applause.) There was one other remarkable point relative to Free Trade and Protection. Before the Australian Parliament was federated the State of Victoria remained Protectionist and that of New South Wales adhered to Free Trade. The result was that the Free Trade colony outstripped the Protectionist colony in the value and fq.uantity of its imports and exports and the population increased at a greater ratio. Mr. Chamberlain said at Liverpool that he would give them his personal guarantee and he asked thim to take his statements on that. When they were asked to stake their commercial prosperity'on the personal guarantee of I a statesman like Mr. Chamberlain they were entitled to ask something as to his past history and as to what his policies were worth. (Applause.) They remembered the old ago pension promise in 1895 which was said to be so simple that any child could under- stand it. At Newcastle the other day Mr. Chamberlain said "You need not trouble about this fiscal policy. My scheme is so simple that any child could understand it." Mr. Chamberlain in 1870 , was in favour of a system of national education when the Government brought in their recent Education Bill, ' although Nit.. Chamberlain did not take an active part in promoting the Bill—he said it was not in his depart- ment and did not concern him now that he had been on the veldt—he was at any rate a passive resister in regard to it. (Laughter.) If they looked into his past history he did not think Hr. Chamberlain was the man in whom they would have confidence to take a statement merely on the strength of has personal guarantee. Mr. Chamberlain was

very fond of quoting scripture. They all recollected that old saying of his about the House of Lords "They toil not neither do they spin " and yet at Glasgow his platform was crowded with landlords. He quoted scripture again the other day when he said "Man does not live by breadalone." That was quite true, but the inference was not that they should tax bread and bring up their rising generation on cheap tea and cigarettes. (Laughter.) That would not increase the physical stamina of the race. Did they know to whom that re- mark was made? It was made to the Devil. He (Mr. King) was looking it up the other day and he found a few verses lower down that this same gentleman pointing out the kingdoms of the earth and the glory there of said" All these things shall be thine if thou wilt fall down and worship me." Was net that Mr. Chamberlain to-day? (Laughter.) Did not history repeat itself? (Laughter and applause.) They could only hope that history would continue to repeat itself and that the temptation would be resisted as in the days of old. (Applause.) The members of the Liberal party at the present time were in a very anamolous and unusual position. They were the true conservatives of the:couutry. They had got their backs to the wall, try- ing to prevent all those revolutionary proposals of the so-called Unionist party, but they were not going to be content with that. They were going to fight to the last for their Free Trade, for a national system of education, and to prevent Mr. Balfour next session from giving the brewer a permanent value inhis an nual licence. (Applause.) In any fiscal changes which they made, they would turn their attention to such questions as the taxation .of land values. Aga in alluding to Manchester's gain by the Ship Canal, the speaker said there were other people who had gained more in proportion and risked nothing. They were the large land-owners outside Manchester who contributed ' nothing whatever, and sold land, which previously was of mere agricultural value, at enormously enhanced prices. It seemed to the Liberal party that when a community had spent money on gas, water, and tram- ways to the suburbs, and when the industry and enter- prise of the community increased the value of land, some of that increased value should go back into the public coffers. They also required a truly nation! system of education which would eliminate for ever the infernal wringling of the so-called religious sects. It was by such reforms as he had mentioned that the prosperity of the country would be advanced. (Applause.) SPEECH BY MR. C. E. PARKER.

Mr. PARKER, in seconding the resolution, expressed the pleasure it gave him to support a gentleman like Mr. King, who evidently had a firm grip of th economic truths of the fiscal controversy. After hear- ing the speeches by Mr. Crosfield and Mr. King, he thought they would all agree with Sir William Harcourt when he said that the fiscal policy as advocated by Mr. Chamberlain was propagated by appeals to class prejudices, was founded on fallacious arguments supported by baseless alarms, inaccurate figures and perverted facts. (Applause). Still, it was curious how it had filled the minds of the people and taken such a grip upon the countrv.ln thecourse of a dis cussion on the question he heara the question asked: "If our imports are not paid for by our exports how do we get paid for them?" That was a question Mr. Chamberlain himself could not answer unless he gave the correct answer, and that was that our imports were paid for by our exports, and to put up bars to shut out our imports would inevitably re-act upon the exports. They would have to settle the fiscal question before they could make progress with anything else that con- cerned the welfare of the people. There was one aspect of the question that he wished to deal with and that was what might be called the hi-product of the tariff proposals. He desired to refer to the inevitable demoralisation of politics through the introduction of the fiscal issue. (Hear, hhar.) His hearers undoubtedly represented many interests, political and otherwise. Perhaps some of them were specially interested in the cause of temperance. and seeing the terrible demoralis- ing:influence which intemperance had upon the health, well-being and prosperity of our countrymen they naturally desired that the control of the liquor traffic should be in their own hands. (Hear, hear.) Some of them realized that the, housing of the people was a very important matter. At the present time people were huddled together and their children had no place to play in but thc gutter. In regard to the Land Lsws which cramped people in towns and rendered it impossible for cheap dwellings to be built outside, men were enabled by possessing land to levy a sort of blackmail upon the energy and progress of the town. The community created the increased value of the land and the Owner ' " nobbled " the increase. Then there was the important question of education. Many of them were intensely interested in it and did not see why they should not have absolute control of the education of their children inasmuch as they paid for that education. (Hea,r,hear). All these questions had been ruthlessly thrust in the background because Mr. Chamberlain had dumped down this fiscal question upon the country. If Mr. Chamberlain and his party were successful in rein- troducing Protection into this country it would become a permanent factor in our politics, and that was a very serious aspect of the question. If retaliation were introduced it was probable that in Warrington the iron trade in its various branches would be the first to go in for it. To secure retaliation they would have to return a man to Parliament who would vote for it. The iron trade would become their politics. It would be so with all the various trades in the country and instead of the good of the country arid the elevation of the people being their object the fiscal question would obtrude itself and become the leading factor in our politics. (Applause.) This had already been exemplified in a town down south, where the great question at an election was whether or not they should have protection for the.cement trade. Mr. Parker pro- ceeded to quote from a letter written by a gentleman in France, in the course of which it was stated :—" We have had the cotton manufacturers of Normandy at war with those of Vosges. There are lcontinual struggles between the north and south. France is cut into sec- tions by the Protectionist spirit." A writer in the United States said "An enormous number of people come to look upon the legislature simply as a machine whieh worked one way will put money into their pockets, and worked another would take it out. Their sole interest in politics is therefore narrowed down to influencing congress in the way that will bring them in the greatest personal pro,fit. They subscribe accord- ingly colossal sums—a big Corporation will even give as much as £so,ooo—to the funds of that party which is pledged to uphold, and if possible increase, the tariff schedules in their favour." There was no doubt that if they allowed trades to bring their wealth, power, and influence to effect legislation it would have a very in- jurious influence on politics. The people who were the consumers would have to pay for all these increased prices. The danger was—and it. was a weak- ness, he thought, in the Free Trade position— that the consumer did not feel it nearly so directly as the produL.er. Every man and woman in the country should be thoroughlyawakened and get to know all the determining points and factors 'that] affected the ques- tion. He trusted that at the coming election they would thoroughly kill this old resurrected corpse of IProtection so that it would not show its head again during the present century. (Applause.) The resolution was put to the meeting and carried.

MR. LLOYD JONES AND THE INSTITUTE TRUSTEES.

Mr. LLOYD JONES moved " That this meeting pledges itself to use every legitimate means in its power to return at the head of the poll a good sound Liberal, at the next election, and urges all Liberals to unite in an active campaign against the re-actionary schemes of the present G_lvernment." He said he was proud that they had such a large gathering that night but he fully believed that they would have had a still larger meeting had they not been prevented from holding it in the Church Room. ("Shame.") As secretary of the association he visited the Secretary of the Trustees of the Institute and requested that they should have the use of the room for their meeting. They were prepared to pay the highest fee and he was promised a reply in a few days. He did not receive that reply and had to go to the secretary's house. He was then toid that they could not have the room. (Cries of "Shame " and" The Tories have it when they want it.") However, the door of the room in which they were assembled was open to them. ( Applause.) Referring to the political situation he said the present crisis in which they were placed called upon every man —he did not merely say every Liberal—who had the welfare of the country at heart, to rally round the Liberal banner, to fight the fiscal policy, and so uphold the standard of Free Trade, which was still the safe- guard of old England. (Applause.) In Mr. King they had a good, sound, prospective candidate for that division, and he ventured to say that their present inem- ber could not —with all his experience in the House of Commons—make such a speech as they had heard from Mr. King that night. (Hear, hear.) Their present member, he contended,misrepresented the constituency. That was a working men's constituency, and their member went arm in arm in the House of Commons with those who voted against the interests of the work- ing men. Their present member voted for Lord Penrhyn in the House of Commons—(" Shame ")—and would up- hold the interests of the landlords at every oppor- tunity. He called upon them to organize that part of the constituency, so that when the time came they would be ready to do their utmost in support of Mr. King, who, he was sure, would be their candidate. He might venture to say that they would not seek a better man than Mr. King, and if they did they would not find one. They had a large majority to wipe out, and he hoped they would do it. (Applause.) Mr. W. J. 13nowN seconded. The salvation of the workingman, he said, did not lie in Protection but in

Free Trade, the taxation of land values, and the obeli" tion of mining royalties. The resolution was carried with two dissentients. THANKS. The Rev. J. S. HUGHES proposed a vote of than to the Chairman and speaker, and said' Mr. Crosfleld had rendered valuable service to the Liberals and in the cause of freedom, both in that division and at Warring- ton. Those present would go away enlightened on the political irestions of the day, and, he trusted, be deter- mined to carry out the resolution which they had just shown themselves in favour of. They ought all to ir true to their convictions, and if they were true to their convictions they would be true to the nation and to God. He hoped they would have the joy of a centast as some of them were longing for a political fight in that district. A regular downright, stand-up fight for principles wduld do them all good. (Applause) The resolution was unanimously carried. MI rn . (Laughter.) They must. however, look at the„ fiscal question and every other question from a national standpoint, knowing that what was for the good of the nation at large would ultimately be for the good of the individual. (Applause).

RUNCORN AND AND WIDNES.

NEGLECT ICASE_AT WIDNES.

At Widnes on Thurschiy, William and Mary Houghton of Ditton, were given two months wherein to improve the condition of their children at home. From the evidence it transpired that they had been for a long time under the Naticmal Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Cnildren's inspection. The male defendant was a pigeon fancier, and neglected his home in the pursuit of this hobby. The five children were ill-clothed and dirty, but fairly well nourished. The home wa% far from being comfortable, and in consequence of non- pliance with previous warnings, the case was brought to court. -

• Sr. J. 4 RUNNER'S S E A.T.-TORY OPPOSITION

At a meeting of members of the Northwich Consti- tutional Club,on Wedaesday,it was stated that,although the Unionist party had no candidate at present, it was the fixed determination of the party to have a contest at the next general election. Sir John Brunner, Bart, has represented the division in the Liberal interest con- tinuously for fifteen years.

ST. HELENS.

HOOLIGANISM AT ST. HELENS.

At St. Helens •en Monday, two young men named Charles Melia, 83, Lyon-street, and Martin Morrison, 2, Back Liverpool-road, were charged with breach of the peace. Police-constable Shaw said that on Saturday night the prisoners and another man seriously assaulted another at the bottom end of Church-street. They walked through the crowd, striking and kicking people, and the man who was assaulted was very badly cut. A crowd of 500 or 600 people assembled, and the whole of the traffic was stopped.—Police-constable Chorlton corroborated.—Both men were ordered to find sureties to be of good behaviour for six months.

SMART CAPTURE AT ST. HELENS.

James Shutfleworth, of 9, Back Churton's-row, was charged at St. Helens OR Monday with stealing a ham value 7s. 6d. from the shop of Gerald Byrne, Peasley Cross-lane. Mr. Blundell, the manager, spoke to miss- ing the ham at a quarter past ten. He gave information to Sergeant Jackson, and they had the ham and prisoner in less than a quarter of an hour.—Sergeant Jackson said he went to Ada-street to a house occupied by prisoner's sister. Prisoner was alone in the house with the ham on the table.—The man had been convicted eight times and was now fined E 1 or fourteen days. •

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

/ BETTING RAID AT HAYDOCK. The county police, on Saturday, at Haydock, raided a crowd of betting men standing near the Haydock tramway terminus, who were surrounded by Sergeant Walker and a number of constables. About forty names of supposed bookmakers and people who were betting were taken, and proceedings will follow. A COSTLY CAKE.

Bernard Cahill, Earlestown, was charged at the War- rington County Petty Sessions with stealing a 30. cake the property of Edward Broadhurst, Parr Mount, St. Helens, at the Earlestown Market. on October 30. He pleaded not guilty. The evidence was that the prisoner bought a penny cake at the stall of Mr. Broadhurst,and after eating it he took the cake in question while Mary Keighley, who had charge of the stall, had her back turned.—Prisoner alleged that he had paid for the cake.l —A fine of 10s. and costs was imposed. PUBLIC LIBRARY QUESTION AT EARLESTOWN.

On Monday night a meeting of ratepayers of Newton- in-Makerfield was held at the Earleyotown Town Hall, in reference to the question of adopting the Public Free Libraries Act, and accepting the offer of £4,000 by Mr. Carnegie towards the ailding. There was a large attendance, Mr. W. Collingwood, chairman of the District Council, presiding. A resolution that the offer be respectfully declined was first submitted, but an amendment was proposed that it be accepted; whilst a rider was submitted that another meeting be held. The proposal to accept the gift and recommend the District Council to adopt the Free Libraries' Act was carried by a two-thirds majority. During the discussion it was lamented that there were not more public institutions, and the mover of the resolution not to accept the gift contended that public baths were more necessary. The discussion was of a spirited character.

ENTIRE BRIDGE MOVED SIMULTANEOUSLY. Almost half Paris turned out recently to witness the I extraordinary proceeding of moving bodily one of the bridges of the Seine, the so-called Passerelle de Passy. The entire bridge, weighing 360,000 tons, ,was lifted off 'the pillars and slowly rolled up the river 90 feet away. The remarkable part of this feat was that after only six weeks of preparation, for laying the rollers and setting the winches, 21 men sufficed to move the entire struc- ture.

A WILD SCHEME EOR PROTECTION.

But there are conceivable circumstances under which special measures might have to be taken to meet an organised attack either by foreign trusts or a Foreign Government intended to crush out an industry natural to this country, with a view,of course,to raising prices , afterwards,andthat I, for one,have recognised ina letter i to the Warrington press and in many discussions on this ; matter. (Hear, hear.) But I am not going into 1 theoretic considerations of that kind, beeause we have !no scientific investigation on these lines to deal with, . but I will deliberately call a wild scheme for Protection 'all along the line. (Hear, hear.) So long as that is the 1 real issue we cannot and must not budge an inch. (Hear, I hear.) Never be persuaded to take a stei3 on the slippery ! slope of Protection, which is intended to land you at the bottom of it. They call Mr. Chamberlain the emissary of Empire; on the contrary nothing could be more mischievous and more dangerous than the reckless way in which he is trying to tempt the Colonies to play up to him in the proposals he has made to introduce 1 sordid questions of conflicting interests, bickering and jealousy into the Imperial household. (Hear. hear.) It would be much nearer the mark to describe Mr. Cham- berlain as an incendiary going about the country with the torch of Protection with which he threatens to burn down the fiscal and commercial system built up during the last sixty years. Well, it is your duty and mine to see that our Free Trade fire engines are always ready to put out at a moment's notice this Protectionist conflagra- tion. (Hear, hear.)

THE INSTINCT OF CONSERVATISM.

You as an agricultural constituency are in- clined, if you have any faults at all, to conservatism. Well, conservatism has its advantages. It is an instinct that teaches us not to discard any- thing of proved value, or to abandon the "Bird in the hand," off which you can at least make your dinner,— (laughter)—in favour of half a dozen birds of paradise in the bush; and unquestionably that instinct of con- servatism should stand you in good stead now. I hope and believe that it will. I do not know whether you read the very piquant and spicy account of a large meeting of our Conservative friends in Sheffield the other day. Mr. Chaplin, needless to say was in his element, but even he for a time looked serious and that was when that excellent Conservative Major Rasch was speaking. He is a member for an agricultural con- stituency pretty much like this, and was returned by a large majority. But what did he tell his friends at. Sheffielda He said if I remember right, that what happened in the days of Protection was by no means forgotten in his constituency, and that if he went down and told an agricultural labourer that they were going back to the times when the loaf cost sevenpence, and when wages were about seven shillings a week to pay' for it, that labourer would show him the door,—(hear, hear)—or if the window happened to be handy —(laughter)—he might make a more speedy exit that way. (Renewed laughter). I don't advise you to make use of the window but I do recommend you to show any one the door who comes advising you to support proposals by which you yourselves will inevitably be the greatest losers. (Hear, hear.) Because, make no ' mistake about it, not only will your food be dearer but every article of clothing, every little luxury will go up in price, and as I saidat the Liberal Club in Warrington the other night, you will be able to purchase less with a sovereign than you can purchase to-day with 17s. 6d. Therefore the whole consumption of the home market— which is our greatest market—will be diminished, and trade with it. Less demand for labour follows, and are you going to believe that with such reduced demand for labour wages will rise? A short time since Mr. Chamberlain with his usual recklessness was promising this right and left. What are his promises worth ? How can we getarantee more employment when the total volume of trade is reduced? (Hear, hear). MR. CHA.MBaRLAIN'S PROPHEMS.

It is true he talks of only a small duty on corn, but as we know very well these duties are nearly always small to begin with. (Hear, hear). They have a knack of growing. There is a homely Lancashire saying that " Eating is like fighting; it only wants beginning." Remember that when someone wants you to let them nibble at Protection. (Applause). Before I sit downl am going to give you a few figures from an advance copy of the Liberal Magazine "which has been sent to me from London. I hope some of you find time to read that most excellent little journal. These figures are in reply to Mr. Chamberlain's statement in Liver- pool as regards the stagnancy of British trade. Mr. Chamberlain said in Liverpool : My Case is that the trade of this country as measured —and I think it ought to be mainly measured—by the exports of this country to foreign countries and to British possessions,has during the last twenty or thirty years been* practically stationary; that our export trade to all those foreign countries which have arranged tariffs against us has enormously diminished,and at the same time their exports to us have enormously increased. It would take me too long to attempt to answer such a statement, but I hope you will not be too mach alarmed by Mr. Chamberlain's prophecies and the lurid pictures of our present and future state which he paints. They remind one a bit of some lines of Burns :

- . MR. CHAMBERLAIN'S CASE.

"DIMINISHED TRADE." "PROTECTION AND PROSPERITY."

===== 0002601_19031107_art0146.txt | included=true | included =====
MAKING THE FOREIGNER PAY.

The Present Scheme

AT Liverpool on October 27th and 28th Mr. Chamberlain explained his "case" at length. The "case " was entirely different from what it was last May. Then we Were to sacrifice our trade to save our Empire. Now the Empire is held up as the best business asset of the nation. Then a tax on food would increase the cost of living. Now we are told, with pathetic pleadings, not to mistrust him, but to take his pledge and believe in his sincerity when he says that his proposals will not add one farthing to the cost of living. Then, a tax on food was going to produce a very large revenue, three-fourths of which was to be paid by the working classes, and to be devoted to Old-Age Pensions. Now, the foreigner will pay the tax, and nobody will get the pensions. Then, the whole scheme was arranged to bind the Empire together' without which it would fall into disjointed atoms. Now, no evil results will follow from a refusal of his proposals.

===== 0002601_19031107_art0147.txt | included=true | included =====
Exports Enormously Diminished.

Now let us consider Mr. Chamberlain's "proposals as they stand" to-day—or, rather, as they stood at Liverpool. His first point was that "our export trade to foreign countries has enormously diminished." Mr. Chamber- lain always talks in the superlative mood (men are either patriots or traitors), so that one hardly knows what "enormously" means. Be quotes no figures and mentions no But as he says Protectionist countries have increased in prosperity more rapidly than we have, let us compare the total exports of manufactures to all parts of the world for the last five years: Year. U. Kingdom. Germany. America. 1897 £199,000,000 £115,000,000 £57,000,000 1898 198,000,000 119,000,000 60,000,000 1899 213,000,000 135,000,000 70,000,000 1900 224,000,000 149,000,000 90,000,000 1901 221,000,000 144,000,000 85,000,000 If we consider our exports to protected countries only we find that, in spite of Protec- tion, our exports to them have increased. From 1875 to 1879 we sent them an average of £88,000,000. From 1899 tp 1902 we sent an average of £104,000,000. So much for our "enormously diminished exports." To get at the truth when Mr. Chamberlain speaks it seems necessary to reverse the meaning of the English language.

Protection and Prosperity. Mg. CHAMBERLAIN tells us that protected countries have grown prosperous more rapidly than we have. Of course Mr. Chamberlain has his own ideas of "prosperity." When he is told that the nation, and particularly our workers, are saving more and more money per head each year, he replies that these figures do not affect his case. So far as it is possible to sift any logical line of thought out of the whirling chaff of Mr. Chamberlain's .speeches, it seems that he measures a nation's prosperity by the smallness of its imports as compared with the largeness of its exports. Thus, according to Mr. Chamberlain, the United States of America cannot be what he would call "prosperous." Look at these figures. Since 1898 the imports into America have increased by 410,000,000d01. while the exports have only increased by 18'9,000,000d01. So, according to Mr. Chamberlain, the United btates, in spite or rigorous Protection, has lost 221,000,000d0l worth of trade since 1898. Surely the Americans must be nearly all bankrupt in business, or is it Mr. Chamber- lain who is bankrupt in argument ? German imports have increased by 1;70,000,000 in nine years, while Russia, the, most heavily pro- tected country in the world, has increased its imports from :379,000,000 roubles in 1891 to (320,000,000 roubles in 1900. Probably Mr. Chamberlain will tell us these figures prove nothing; they certainly do not prove his case. But what we can prove is that the British workman is better paid, frets better value for his money, works shorter hours and under safer conditions than any workmen on the Continent.

When the Foreigner Pays. M. CHAMBERLAIN is doing his utmost to make us believe the foreigner will pay any tax we may put upon his goods. A Unionist candidate went so far the other 'day as to say it was simply ":a question whether we should levy the revenue for the maintenance of the Empire from the foreigner at our ports, or collect it from our people at home." • Mr. Chamberlain does not go quite to the length of that absurdity, but he argues that, as he said in his second speech at Liverpool:

in no single case has the extra price to the con- sumer been in any real proportion to the amount of the taxes ;

while at Newcastle he said the amount of a tax that is paid by the consumer varies according to a number of circumstances, but that hardly ever is the whole of it paid by the consumer.

Mr. Chamberlain will not say in so many words "the foreigner would pay the food tax," but that is what he wants people to believe. Well, here are the prices of wheat in Europe, and the amount of duties imposed, in July last, according to the Board of Trade:

Price Country. Price. Duty. less Duty. s. d. s. d. s. d. France Germany United Kingdom 27 6 1 0 26 6

If the duty does not increase the price of wheat, will Mr. Chamberlain kindly explain what does P Of course we all know there are other things which affect the price of corn besides a duty. You can have a high duty and. cheap corn, according as the world's supply exceeds the demand. But the duty prevents corn from falling as low as it other- wise would. On the other hand, you can abolish the duty altogether, and have to pay a high price for corn, according as the world's harvest is equal to or below the demand. A duty ' would, then, make the corn so much dearer. There are other influences which affect the price of corn, but whether all of them, or none of them, come into play, you cannot put a duty upon corn without doing one of two things: • (1) Increasing the price above its natural level. (2) Preventing the price falling to its natural level.

But these are simple elementary economic facts which Mr. Chamberlain seems to think do not exist. Finally, let us ask Mr. Chamber- lain two questions: (1) If the foreigner pays the duty, why does he propose to exempt maize and bacon from the food taxes?

(2) If the foreigner pays, why does he propose to reduce the duty on tea, sugar, coffee, cocoas &c., to make up for the duty on other foods? We pause for a reply.

The Examiner, Saturday November 7, 1903.

===== 0002601_19031107_art0149.txt | included=true | included =====
rHE FREE CHURCHES.

THE FREE CHURCH ELECTION FUND.

The response made to the appeal of the National Free Church Council for a fund for fighting the next General Election in the interests of Education has been so far satis- factory, although, of course, the fund is only in its infancy. It is the intention of the National Council to raise at least £f20,000, which is to be spent in the following, among other ways : (1) Millions of leaflets and other forms of literature to be supplied; (2) Hundreds of efficient speakers to be sent into the constitu- encies; (3) To make grants in some cases to local Councils and Federations for the purpose of effectively carrying on the campaign; and (4) To make such arrangements as will enable numbers of Free Churchmen to stand as candidates, who are not at present able to meet the entire cost of a contested election. A letter has been sent to every Free Church minister in the country, signed by Rev. James Travis (President), Dr. W. J. Townsend (Ex-Presi- dent), Rev. F. B. Meyer (President-Elect), Messrs. It. W. Perks, M.P., Geo. Cadbury, and Evan Spicer (Treasurers), Rev. J. Monro Gibson (Hon. Secretary), and the Rev. Thomas Law (General Secretary of the National Free Church Council), pointing out the objects of the fund and appealing for assistance. The wealthier members of the Churches have already, done much, one generous friend having pro- mised £:5000 conditionally on a minimum sum of £20,000 being raised. But of course it is the rank and file who must be relied on for the bulk of the fund, and the promoters feel con- fident that Free .Churchmen of all conditions will rally to their assistance. It is being realised more and more that it is not sufficient to hold meetings and to pass resolutions. The next election must be fought in an organised way, and this caiknot be done without money.

MUNICIPAL •ELECTIONS.

The Free Churches all over the country are feeling the importance of contesting the Muni- cipal Elections. The Borough and District Councils have had placed in their hands the working of the Education Act, and it is highly necessary that on these bodies there should be a sufficient representation of the interests of Nonconformity. The need for action of this sort is shown in a striking manner in a booklet which has just been issued by the National Free Church Council. it is entitled "The Education Act at Work," and gives the actual composition as regards denominational and religious con- nections of practically all the new local authorities dealing with the Education Act, besides a host of other information useful to all interested in the subject. On many of the Councils Nonconformists are in a very small minority, and it is to remedy this state of affairs that the Free Church Councils are nominating candidates pledged to work the Act justly and in the interests of educational efficiency.

A GROWING MOVEMENT.

Deep interest continues to be shown in the operations of the Free Church Girls' Guild. One of the feature ot this work is the establishment of homes for girls in connection with the Guilds. These homes are largely used for classes, lec- tures, and for the social intercourse of the girls. One of these new Guild houses has just been opened in Derby. The rooms are very tastefully furnished, there is accommodation for a girl needing temporary lodging, and there are to be all kind of attractions for girls. A pleasing feature in connection with the work is the way in which girls of different social stand- ing and employed in a variety of ways, meet happily together; a recent report showed that weavers, spinners, typewriters, telegraph girls, domestic servants and shop girls are all meet- ing together in the same Guild and are a mutual help to one another.

===== 0002601_19031107_art0150.txt | included=true | included =====
DENOMINATIONAL SECRETARIES.

The Autumnal Meetings of the Baptist and Congregational Unions last month 'are still arousing comment. The proposals of the Secretary of the Baptist Union for a change in the constitution of that body are variously criticised. Mr. Shakespeare, it is admitted, put his case in a very striking manner, though there is much to be said, for instance, against the dropping of the Spring Assembly. It is interesting to note the personality of Secretaries of these great denominational bodies. Rev. T. H. Shakespeare, the Secretary of the Baptist Union, is very popular amongst his own denomination, and Free Churchmen in general. His bright and genial presence is welcomed all over the country. One of the greatest hits he made was undoubtedly in connection with the Baptist Twentieth Century Fund, when he did a great deal to bring the matter to its triumphant finish. Professor J. A. Mitchell, the new Secre- tary of the Congregational Union. is a man of somewhat different stamp. He has had wide experience in the positions he has held. It is felt that, to a great extent, he possesses the qualities of statesmanship so necessary in a posi- tion such as he now holds, and everyone is wish- ing him much success in his new undertaking.

===== 0002601_19031107_art0151.txt | included=true | included =====
AN ENTERPRISING PUBLICATION.

Church Secretaries and ministers all over the country are now considering the question of a localised magazine for 1904. They could hardly wish a magazine more suitable for their purpose than the Free Churchman, advance copies of the January number of which have just been issued: It is certainly a gem of periodical literature. The Portrait Gallery of Free Church leaders, which is produced in tone by a new process, will doubtless be framed in thousands of homes. The high tone and excellent quality of the magazine throughout are to be commended. Apart from the January number, the programme for Cy-, coming year is a remarkably strong one. The con- ductors have been fortunate enough to induce Dr. Clifford to write practically an antobio- graphy under the title, "Pages from my Life Story." All the finest writers among the Free Churches have contributed in some way or other to this programme, and every side of Church and home life is touched upon. The conductors of the Free Churchman have set themselves a high standard, vie., that nothing but the best shall appear in their pages. The January num- ber shows that they have reached their ideal. The _Tree Churchman is issued by the Publica- tion Department of the National Free Church Council, Memorial Hall, London, E.C.

===== 0002601_19031107_art0152.txt | included=true | included =====
THE FREE CHURCHES AND SOCIAL QUESTIONS

It is highly desirable that the attention of Free Churchmen should not be monopolised by the Education Question, and it is, therefore, gratifying to see that the Fret' church Chronicle has devoted space this month to "The Free Churches and Social Questions." Mr. Herbert D. Williams has secured an interview with Mr. J. A. Steuart, the author of " Wine on the Lees," etc., who says some rather startling thins. "The Church must destroy the Drink Tfaffic, or the Drink Traffic will destroy the Church." That is the substance of his remarks, and he points out that the Drink Question stands in the way of all real reform. He maintains that it should be made a test question at every election, for the Temperance people must have political power if they are to win. "The Tory Party," says Mr. Steuart, " is bound hand and foot to the drink-seller. You may think I am making a strong statement, but I believe that scarcely a single Tory candidate would be elected if he went against the beer-selling, interests. The publican has immense political power, and is highly organised to use it. You must get political power to defeat him. So I hold that the Free Church Council, which is so splendidly organised, is the right body to take up the question, and get all their candidates pledged to the reform of the Liquor Traffic."

STOCKTON HEATH IMPROVEMENTS

In accordance with a resolution of the Stockton Heath Parochial Committee, Mr. G. Pilling gave notice that at the next meeting he would move that the Council make good Calder street.and Bedford street, Stockton Heath. Mr. Mounfield said it was understood that Birch street would be included in the improvements. The Clerk said powers bad been obtained for the making of the street which had been re-named. Mr. Pilling decided to include that street in his proposition. The Clerk said the Parochial Committee had asked the Council to make good Chapel-lane which was a District Council road. Houses had been erected on each side, and he understood the footpaths were not properly made. Mr. Swinton said he estimated that the cost of the work would be about £l3O. It was decided to refer the estimate to the Parochial Committee, Mr. Pilling remarking that he did not think he committee had any idea that the work would cost so much.

THE LIGHTING OF WESTON.

The sub-committee appointed to consider this matter reported that about 50 lamps would be required to light the streets and principal roads of the Parish.—The Parochial Committee recommended that the Clerk be instructed to obtain from the Runcorn Gas Company a tender for lighting the parish and also to ascertain the cost of electric lighting.

The minutes.we-re ap-proved. A WELCOME

The Chairman extended a hearty welcome to Mr. J. L. Jones, who has been returned to the Council unopposed to succeed the late Mr. Peter Gleave, as representative of Moore. Mr. Jones, he said, had previously served on the Board of Guardians and Sanitary Committee and he was the right man for the work. In returning thanks Mr. Jones expressed regret that his election had been brought about under such sad cir- cumStances.

:CORLEY WATER SUPPLY.

The Local Government Board wrote calling attention to a paragraph in the annual report of the Medical Officer of Health with regard to the water supply of Norley and inquired what steps the Council had taken or proposed to take to provide an improved supply. The Clerk said he referred the matter to the Inspector of nuisances who pointed out that a most extensive length of pipes would be required to supply the whole of the parish. The worst-servesi portion of the district appeared to be that near the Vyrnwy main. To supplrthat neighbourhood, about 900 yards of mains would be required, and if they were 4 inch mains the cost would be about £l5O. If that sum were borrowed for the usual thirty years, the principal and interest would be £8 per annum. The water to be used at the cottages would cost about £lO per annum, and the revenue would be about £lB or Rl9 so that there would be a loss to the Council. After some discuspion it was decided that the Clerk should inquire whether the Liverpool Corporation would be prepared to treat for a partial supply of water to Norley.

THE THELIVALL _NUISANCE

At a meeting of the Thelwall Parish Council on the 19th October, the following resolution was passed and ordered to be sent to the Council.—" The Thelwall Parish Council as representing the inhabitants of The!- wall think that, though they are not in a position to resist payment of the expenses apportioned to them in connection with the nuisance in Weaste-lane, yet in their opinion the interests of the ratepayers have cer- tainly not been considered and that they think that the' whole matter has ly3en grossly mismanaged especially aslregards Professor Delepine's report." Admiral Jones Parry said he had had a good many humiliating moments during the ten years he had been a member of the Council, but none equal to his appear- ance at the Parish Council meeting. 'lt would probably be his last appearance there and also at the Rural Council meetings, so he wished to make a sort of "wind-up." He was not able to tell the Parish Council who ordered Professor Delepine's report to be printed and distributed. • Could he ask that question now? The Chairman: You may ask the question. Whether anyone willanswet: you is another question. Admiral Parry: I suppose you mean to say lam not to have an answer.

The Chairman: I don't say anything. Admiral Parry: I will ask the Clerk at once. Who ordered the report to be printed? The Clerk: Am I to answer the question,lVlr. Chair- mane

The Chairman : I leave it to you entirely. The Clerk : I have answered this question three times already at these meetings. AdmiraliParry : I have never heard it. The Clerk: Three times I have replied to Admiral Parry.

Admiral Parry: I dispute that. Am I alloxved to see the minutes ?

The Chairman : Are yougoing to move a resolution? Admiral Parry: Yes. I did not think a resolution was necessary. Continuing he centended that accord- ing to his, instructions, Professor, Delepine had nothing to do with the drainage system. He however, ordered Dr. Adams to obtain photographs of the pipes. Of what use were those photographs? -

The Chairman: The phOtoiraphs have been accepted by the District Council as evidence. • I don't think you can question theaction of the in that matter. . . . . _ _

Admiral Parry contended that Professor befepTne's report was not laid before the Council, _ _

The Chairman said each member received a copy. Admiral Parry said he agreed with the resolution of the Parish Council that there had been a very great waste of money and that the matter had been very care- lessly gone through. As far as he was concerned the matter was at an end.

The Chairman : I think Admiral Parry's action this morning is very much out of order indeed. I asked him whether he was going to end his remarks with a resolution. He was entirely out of order unless he in- tended to move a resolution with reference to the re- port before the Council. I consider it to be both an unkind and a disorderly action on the part of Admiral Parry in this matter.

A DANGER TO CYCLISTS. Mr. F. Pendlebury wrote complaining that the main road at Appleton (just past London Bridge) was not lighted. _ _ _ _ _ _

14-11-Ir. Pilling said the road in its present state was very dangerous to cyclists at nigh.. Mr. Baner endorsed the. complaint. _ _ _

On the motion of Mr. Pilling, seconded by Mr.Moun- field, it was decided to erect four lamps between Lon- don Bridge and the top of the first incline.

ALLEGED DAMAGE TO ROADS.

The Clerk reported that he had written to Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sons, Ltd., complaining of damage alleged to have been done to the road at Norley by a traction engine and waggons used for the conveyance of lime. The firm had replied, suggesting, without prejudice, that the question of damage should be referred to a competent surveyor, who was independent of both parties. If the Council would agree to this and Mr. Longdin would act, the firm would be agree- able to his acting in the matter. was decided to submit the names of Messrs. Linnell and Hawkins (County Council surveyors) to the firm as arbitrators.

Mr. Diggle—Wnip-iained that the road. at Newton had been damaged in the same way, and it was agreed to submit this matter to the sUrveyors appointed to arbi- trate.

WESTON WATER SUPPLY. The Clerk said the Salt Union required the water main at Weston to be extended tto their works imme- diately. He had approached tlie Urban Council and they were willing to lay the main, provided that the District Council or the Salt Union would pay the cost. The Council agreed to give an undertaking to meet the expense incurred. BUILDING COMMITTEE'S REGULATIONS.

Mr. Linaker reported that the Building Committee had decided that in future owners of houses should be required to obtain the certificate of the surveyors that the houses were built in accordance with the plans and specifications before they were occupied, and also that the surveyors should insist on new streets being laid out before building operations were commenced.

The minutes were confirmed. ANOTHER WATER QUESTION.

Mr. Piling reported with reference to the proceed- ings of the sub-committee appointed to discuss the question of the.water supply to Dutton Preston-o'-th'- Hill, and a portion of Aston. He detailed the particu- lars as to cost placed before the committee and said it was deciaed that the Liverpool Corporation be approached and asked to give a supply from the Vyrnwy main ; and that it be a recommendation to the District Council that the length of mainnecessary for the supply to the new isolation hospital be paid for by the Dis- trict Council out of the loan to be taken up for the erection of the hospital. The minutes were approved.

Warrington I.L P. v. The Non-Trading

Association.

Sir,—Dariag the recent Orford Ward election the Non-Trading Association, through their official organ the "Times" made an attack upon Mr. Fred Stott because of his advocacy of "Municipal Socialism." TheWarriagtou I.L.P. now challenge the Non-Trading Association to publicly debate the matter, each side to choose :its own representative. A small committee tot- e formedto make suitable arrangements. ABRAHAM W.A.RBURTON, Secretary. D., Cairo-street.

Mr. Stott and Orford Ward.

Sir,—At the first meeting of the Town Council, vacancy will be declared in the Orford Ward, this -vacancy being caused by my compulsory retirement May I be allowed to make an appeal to both political parties and to the Non-Trading Association not to oppose the candidature of Mr. Stott? Everyone is agreed I believe arm the general principle that Labour hould be directily represented in the Council. Mr. Stott was unanimously selected by the Trades and Labour Council, and his candidature was endorsed by 4 almost the whole of the Trades Unions in Warrington. In the unanimous opinion of the working men he is-the best qualified to represent them. On Monday last, in spite of the fact that he and kis committee 'had no ex- perience of fighting elections, and no conveyances, he polled a most respectable vote, coming within 82 votes of the success ful candidate, who was a very strong one. In other tow us Labour representatives have done splen- did work Jan Town Councils. Warrington an essentially working aass and industrial town has no such repre- sentative, and in my opinion it would be an exceedingly graceful act on the part of the Conservative and Liberal Parties, anfi the Non-Trading Association if, seeing the good veto Mr. Stott obtained on Monday, they allowed him obe :returned unopposed at the forthcoming elec- tiont—Yours, &c., • 184, Orford-lane.

JOHN LIPSON.

The Macedonian Relief Fund. Sir,—Will you permit me to call general public atten- tion ut your columns to this fund, in aid of which the meeting in Parr Hall last night passed a resolution requesting the Mayor to open a local branch? It is a fund which is urgently called for mid completely justi- fied by the necessities of the, thousands of unhappy sufferers in that afflicted country, and by the national responsibility which rests upon this United Kingdom in respect of Macedonia. This was, lam confident, the unanimous feeling of last night's meeting. The facts ably set forth by Mr. H. J. Torr leave no doubt as to the claim upon our sympathy and help. The fund we are asked -to help is presided over by the Bish9p of London, with Earl Beauchamp, the Bishop of Stepney and Rev. Marshall Hartley as vice presidents sThe committee includes the Bishops of gochester and Worcester, Lady Henry Somerset, the Right Hon, James Bryce M. P. Rev. J. G. Rogers, D. D. Sir Arthur Hayter, M. P. R. Yerburgh Esq, M. P. and others. The treasurer is Sir T. Fowell Buxton, Bart. I mention

iheEe names as a gu. arantee of wise management and

control. The fuii'd already reaches considerably over £4,00. May I express the very earnest hope that our Townsmen will display their characteristic generosity in giving help to this urgent and unfortunate cry for pity 9nd succour? Warrington has done nobly on many occasions, and has shown that it has a heart of sympathy and a hand of helpfulness for the needs of suffering humanity. Donations can be paid to the Mayor's account at any of the local banks. Cannot Congregational collections be vouchsafed? In other places this is being done, and the response has been willing and generous. I (venture, by your leave, to append the following statement from the London Relief Committee. The Secretary of the Macedonian Relief Committee has received an account of the first relief work in the village of Monastir, from .which the following are extracts:

"Resna, October 18th. At about eight o'clock went to the depot. A solid mass of people were banked against the closed shops opposite, waiting for us. We hada tin measure for flour, holding about five pounds. For present need we gave one measure to two people. I think I never did so much good in seven hours in my life, nor did I ever refuse so many desperately needy people. Many, many were the blessings invoked by the recipients upon-the donors of these gifts and upon the distributors. A family of eight orphans came to get flour. They hadtaraly enough to cover their nakedness, and were simply Shaking with cold. The most import- ant plea ..was for.. bedding. It is also said that the

Ochrida district of Deburfsa is in the most terrible condition of any. All these people have got to be fed till next harvest if life is kept in their bodies. -"An old manta the depot, who recently owned a flock of 200 sheep and other property to match, shed tears of gratitude at receiving only five piastres for food. It makes me shiver as flook out of my window on the snow-covered mountains to think of the thinly-chid, barefooted refugee women and children. • .

"I judge that:we can do little more this winter than preserve life, leaving the rebuilding of houses until the spring. I wish the generous people of England and America would hurry up their contributions. Speedy relief means a great saving of suffering, sickness, and mortality in the coming months." Since the receipt of this information the Committee has received a telegram from its own agent, Mr. Brails- ford, urgently requesting the remittance of several thousand pounds.—Thanking you for your kindness, I am, faithfully yours,

JOHN YONGE.

19, Wilson Patten-street, November 6th, 1903.

National Service League.

Sir,—Since we are now agreed on the question of in- security and the necessity of having a Home Defence Army in addition to our navy for the protection of . these shores, it only remains to discuss the best means of raising this army. You will admit, I think, that in view of past ex- perience, we insist not take our regular army into our calculations. The arguments against increasing this army so as to make it sufficiently strong to guard these shores in addition to its present Imperial duties, are so evident that we need not waste time discussing them. We will therefore devote-our attention to discussing whether we are to rely on the Auxiliary forces as they exist at present but rendered more efficient and more numerous by means of "adequate encouragement," or on a truly National Militia raised and trained exactly similar to that excellent model, the Swiss Militia. In a word, we will discuss the voluntary, versus the com- pulsory system. Now I will ask you to concede that since we are going to rely on our army being able to resist a thoroughly trained enemy, it will be necessary to have a training for this force very different to what the volunteers for instance have to-day; that instead of a volunteer putting in 40 drills of one hour each at various intervals during his first year, and 10 drills on succes- sive years Sand being obliged to attend the camp once every two years, we shall require him to di two month the first year and a fortnight in each three succeeding years, in addition to drills as at present. I need not insist on the far greater value of continuous over dis- continuous training. The good results that can be obtained by a short training provided it is consecutive were well borne out by the 1902 Yeomanry. I will ask you further to concede that the numbers of the auxiliary forces and the standard of physique will both have to be raised. Let us for argument sa3 we require an effective force of 500,000 men. How do you propose to get these men on voluntary lines? You will, I suppose, make l it worth while by means of pay. Now this is where the difficulty comes in; what is worth while for one man is not for another. This is the difficulty inherent in the very nature of a purely voluntary force. A man is only obliged to do what he can reasonably afford to dos or what he is inclined to do. What is the result? You see the wretched, the ludicrous spectacle of a Captain of a company drilling 10, 20, or 30 of his men instead of his 100. Yousfind that it is a matter for congratulation if, on a battalion parade 20 per cent. of the men are present! How can you possibly have uniformity of training and therefore successful training, under such conditions? Do not think I blame the men. No. I am often surprised that they dc, as much as they do. Why should we expect more from these men who, as it is, do give up some time to the duty of national defence, while nine-tenths of their countrymen do nothing, and probably score in theit employment where volunteers are not encouraged. In view of the difficulties, experienced with short I training to-day, how will it be with 500,000 men who are to be made much more efficient. We shall have to clink many golden sovereigns in their ears before that number will go to camp for two months. And even if we give each man a ten pound note we shall have to be careful to make the .training pleasant (useful will be trite a secondary conideration)er he will chuck it. But let us suppose it is feasible to get the men, how are you to get the officers? The importance of good, officers is before everything. A regiment is good or bad according to its leaders. The Volunteers are as is well known, terribly under-ofhcered. For the proposed force we should require about 18,000. How are we to get them? Golden sovereigns again? I think not. It is true that if we paid men 200 pounds a year to become officers in this force we should have many men of al certain type longing to don a star, but. we should never I get what we require, the flower and pick of the nation. There are other objections that can be raisedtbut.surely the insoluble difficulty of the officer question is suffi- cient to impeach our voluntary system alone. Now let us examine the other solution of this subject that of creating a truly National Militia by the law of

the land. Ah ! the hateful word compulsion IA instantly objected. . "We love our freedom far too much ever to submit to being compelled to do ourduty ;we think that 'Freedom and Publicity ' is the one road to National salvation." Where may I ask will our freedom be if the enemy is in the land? "Do ye wait for the spattered shrapnel ere ye,learnhow a gun is laid?" "For the low red glare to southward when the raided

coast towns burn?" Now if we were discussing three two or even one year's service I could much better understand this outcry against interfering with the liberty of the individual, but when all that is asked is that a man should give up 1001 daysisluring the whole of his life to learn the sacred duty of defending all that he holds most dear, then indeed I can only attribute it to the extraordinary ignoronce which prevails on this subject, and feel all the more strongly the necessity which has called the National Service League into existence to educate the public on this point. Another objection which one hears constantly is that if every one is trained to arms, Militarism will become rife through the country; that we shall become aggres- sive and shall never seek occasions for using the know- ledge of warfare that we have obtained. Should this be the case I should admit that it would make us question the expediency of adopting universal service even at the risk of remaining insecure, but lam absoluteky convinced that these fears are without foundation. Is there any country where civil and religious liberty is greater than in Switzerland? Are the Swiss aggressive? Are the Swedes or Norwegians, who both have a similar training? No. When you make every man in the country feel that if his country goes to war he may have to fight too ; will have to leave his wife and chil- dren, perhaps never to return, you make him watch the action of his government far closer than if he feels that the worst that can happen will be a slight tax on tea and sugar. I say that universal service instead of in- creasing jingoism and aggressiveness or militarism will plit them down. The last objection that we need discuss is, that to have men going to camp for two months will interfere very seriously with the trade of this country. we can prove that the,* is no objection on,this score where the training is for two years, still less need we consider it in the present scheme of two months. Let us see then what German employers say. Ido not think lam overstating it when I say that they are practically unanimous in admitting that the enhanced value of a workman becauseof his improved physique, his greater alertness of mind and his better formedcharacter due to his military training far outweigh the inconvenience that may aitse when he is called away from his business. Certainly all German employers that I have spoken to on this subject are agreed on this point. We have dis- cussed then the objections and found them wanting; let us turn finally to the splendid advantages that we shall get by putting our home defence on a National basis. _ .

First and foremost we shall get enough officers of the right ttamo ; for the force being National the whole people will take an interest in it and it will, just as in Switzerland, become an honour and a privilege to be an officer. We shall obtain the requisite number of men without having to lower the standard to the wretched extent that is now necessary. We shall with an outlay of 4i millions annually have a thoroughly effective force of 500,000 men; to do this on voluntary lines would cost 3 times as much and in thecommercial struggle that is before us every penny must' be consiolered. National training is far more effective, juster, simpler, mid cheaper than a partial voluntary trainingand by this means only can we obtain thatsecurity which is the finest guarantee of peace. And when we think of all the indirect benefits that will accrue to the nation from it.the stimulant to education and :thereby the improvement in our methods of manu- facture, the physical developments that will follow,the round shoulders that will be made straight, the narrow chests that will be filled out ; when we think of what a clearing out of slum-dwellers this will mean, into the fresh air and into the green fields; and how, since your rich man having to serve side by side of his poorer brethren, will have learnt the good heart that beats under a rough exterior, it will promote greater sym- pathy between the rich and the poor and help to lessen the snobbishness which is now so often prevalent. When we think of all this surely we can say what need we any spur but our own cause to prick us to redress." I appeal then to all those who care at all for the well- fare_ of their race and who do not need to be actuated by party or sectarian motives before helping forward a cause that is just, to do their best to educate opinion on this matter by joining the National Service League, and thus to help forward the day when we shall follow the splendid example of little Switzerland.—l am Sir, your obedient servant, G. R. CROSFIELD, Captain Hon. Sec. Warrrington Branch, N.S.L.
