NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

Rugby Football.

By " Ovalist."

Warrington were distinctly unfortunate in not ob- taining at _least one point at • Hull on Saturday, where they succuoa.bed by a penalty goat to nil. The .gataa was brilliantly contested, in fact it was the general opinion that so fine a contest has not been seen on the Rovers ground for many years. The visitors were quite a match for their powerful foes,. and a fairer result would have been a draw. The Warrington men, however, did lot disgrace themselves, and if they lost they left a reputation behind them which will take smile effacing in Hull.

Right from the commencement play was open and fast. Contrary to expectation the • Warrington forwards beat their opponents in the scrummages, Davies and Bate getting no end of opportunities. The backs were well watched, however, and particularly Fish. Still,more aggressive tactics by the halves must have resulted in something tangible, but it is an aggres- sive half back that Warrington are unable to get hold of. From end to end the game travelled, and a couple of penalties fell to the Rovers, from one of which Gordon placed the goal which won the match. Fish was all over a scorer soon after, but Sinclair deliberately obstructed him, and the penalty which followed came to nought. The referee should have awarded a try, but it needs a bold man to. do such a thing on the Craven-street gtound.

In the second half there were numerous brilliant efflrts on both sides, and each line had narrow escapes. Starks seemed sure to score when the War- rington men obstructed him, and so equalised matters in point of fairness. Fortunately for them the referee failed to see the incident, or a second goal would probably have been put on. There was no choice between the sides, but in the concluding stages War-. rington made things so hot that a score seem•,d almost inevitable. The Rovers' defence, however, prevailed. and Warrington had to submit to their second defeat of the season.

Hallam again played a great game, in fact all the visitors did smart work, which was cordially recog- nised by the crowd. Dickenson again displayed selfishness, and until he can rid himself of this fault he will never be the centre we all hoped to see him make. His work in other respects is splendid, but he does not get his wing man going in the way he ought to do. Bull-dog crashes to the line are alright now and then, but in the main they are faulty tactics for a centre. I counsel Dickenson to guard against this failing of his, or he will certainly lose his place in the team.

The Rovers proved themselves a vastly improved side since last season, and the work of Barry, Gordon, and West in the rear spells danger to opponents. They are ably supplementing the efforts of a grand set of forwards, who though beaten for possession by Warrington were brilliant in the loose. Starks was always on tile ball and twice came very near getting a try.

I-lad Warrington won on Saturday they would have tied with Oldham for top place, for Salford were wain defeated, and by Halifax. The Rovers still lead, and are followed by Oldham, Salford, and Warrington, all teams with a very even chanc3 for honours.

allowed compulsory service where were they to stop? They did not have full-blown conscription at once on the Continent, if we began with two' or three months' training Parliament could increase the time. It had already been advocated by those who were urging militarism in Englandthat opportunity should be given I not only to compel men to serve but to send them I abroad. If they once allowed that the State had the right to compel men to be soldiers, whether their con- sciences approved of it' or not, she thought they would have given away that liberty and freedom which had been valued so much in this country. (Ap- plause.) We netienot be afraid of attacks on the country unawares because there were always preliminary negotiations. These negotiations should not be left to angry, hot-headed men who had got in a rage with each other, but to people who would impartially judge the matter. Many wise men in Europe were earnestly seek- ing that nations should agree to enter into arbitration and settle the questions before they came to that angry state when they were ready, to fly at each other's throats Compulsory military service did much to alienate a nation from Christianity. She had been astonished in France at the great numbers of thoughtful men and women who were free thinkers, and not Christians, and she believed conscription had a great deal to do with it. They could not be constantly training men with the idea of enmity towards others without alienating them from the central idea of Christianity—the thought of love and brotherhood. In conclusion she asked her hearers to view these matters from the highest stand- point and to do all they could to urge forward wainan's I suffrage. (Applause.)

R. A. .NAYLOR, POET! AMUSING "MORNING LEADER"

SKIT.

The "Morning Leader" recently published tin following skit on some of Mr. R. A. Navlor's verses, under the heading "Sub Rosa," by "It may be that Parliamentary candidates sometimesi echo Job's yearning desire, " Oh, that mine adversary had written a book." I know not whether such an aspiration has over occurred to Mr. Lloyd George, but it happens that the gentleman who is seeking to capture Mr. Lloyd George's constituency—Mr. Robert Ander- ton Naylor—has written a book. It is a book which I have read with interest. Whether it will delight millions of readers yet unborn, or will make its readers wish they had never been born, is a matter of opinion.

The author, Mr. Rob--9rt Anlareon Ntylor. F.R.S.L., F.R.Met. S., F.R. list. S., F.R.G.S., etc., has written several books—" Nugas Canorx," "Hymns for the People," "Prince Oscar's Marriage Ode," " Shall we know them again ?" Sweden, 1832," "Norway, 1831," " Chazelworth Waters," "Songs of Trath,"ani " Joe- and Joe." [think I ought to state at once that the Joe mentioned immediately after the "Songs of Truth" is not the right honourable gentleman of whom no doubt some of my readers are thinking. The contrast would be too sudden to be artistic..

Mr. Naylor is a poet, and Wales is the'land of bards. Mr. Lloyd. George will have to keep his eye on his gifted opponefit, for a, man who can write a poem on the St. Lrence Rapids containing these lines, and can then publish them, is a man of courage :

My aspirations will not soar To shoot thy rapids any more— They are too rough; And to those who may e'er invite _ I'd answer straight, but yet polite, Once is enough. The penultimate line may well be taken as a motto by Parliamentary candidates, and if Mr. Naylor is heckled by inquisitive Welshmen in the rarnarvon Boroughs I hope he will always "answer straight, but yet polite."

Like all true poets Mr. Naylor sings because he can- not help it. He lisps in numbers because the numbers come. Thus he had a short peep at Toronto, and in- stantly he writes: Toronto, I like thee; I admire thy Sabbaths ; I will sing thy praises. He keeps his promise, too, and there is more than a page like this: Toronto, fair city, on thee we just glance, But our little vi43w will greatly enhance Our thoughts and our liking for thee. There are thirty-three more lines • quite as good. I turn over another page and find that the tireless bard has" had a go "at Niagara, in this way: Majestic and supremely grand, The greatest wonder of the land. - A thoughtful and appropriate tribute. There !ray be some who will think that Mr. Naylor might fittingly claim the description in the second line, but lam not prepared to say which is the more wonderful, the poet or the waterfall. I decide in favour of a dead-heat.

There are some tantalising passages in this book. Take this, for instance: One gentleman whose acquaintance I formed could tell amusing stories by the dozen, and I saw him keep one lady in a state of interested excitement for two or three hours. It seems to me that the electors in the Carnarvon Borough&havea right to know a little more about this. A few of those tales might make Mr. Naylor's speeches even more interesting than T have no doubt they are. And here is another incident which cries aloud for further elucidation: I was once travelling in a distant land, when an English lady of title said to me one of the sweetest things woman can say to man, and which, perhaps, no lady will ever say to me again. Note the pathetic hesitation in that word "perhaps." Hope springs eternal in the Naylorian breast. Of course I do not suggest that Mr. Naylor should reveal the name of the titled lady. That would be dishonourable. But what did she say? I think that is a fair question, and here is an opportunity for Mr. Naylor to 'answer straight, but yet polite." At least, I hope the remark was of such a nature that it can be repeated .without impoliteness.

Mr. 'N'avlor is modest, for he says in another place: I might as well make the confession that lam not a lady's man. It may be that his tendency to self-dep”eciation causes him to do himself injustice. In suggesting this lam in accord with the remark I find reported here as hav- ing been made by a shrewd observer: One American merchant addressed me after this fashion: "Well, I'm surprised that an elegant man like yon has never found a nice young lady." Rut the American merchant perhaps did not know all. It may be that he had not heard about that sweetest thing whispered to the "elegant man" by the English lady of title. Such an experience might well cause a man to hold aloof from mere "nice young ladies." Perhaps she was a Duchess!

Here is the masterly fragment on Saratoga, dashed off just as the hurried bard is leaving: Beautiful streets and tine grand hotels, Famous for springs and health-giving wells, High-stepping horses with bright-ringing bells. Plenty of diamonds and lots of great swells. There is the sure, unerring touch of the master hand about that. And this, on the Hudson River is every bit as good.: But yet, fair Hudson, thou canst boast What ancient Rhinelanders had not; For icy castles grace thy coast— Not just a few, but quite a lot. 'qr. 'Naylor is, I believe, a timber merchrtntas well as a Poet. His lines are like sound, well-seasoned- wood. You can saw a piece out anywhere and find it solid. Indeedhis poetry may be called hard lines. As a speci- men of multum in parvo, or of severely condensed narrative verse, this could not basily be beaten: We've been to see Chicago's Fair, And crossed the great Atlantic, And when we now our notes compare, We all say "Grand !" " Gigantic ! "

It is the Pass with which these littl trifles are thrown off that fills one with admiring despair.

'JP - The poet ivalso a politician of a sort. He has his views on the labour problem and our commerical out- look. as this shows: We are in a curious position; trades which would otherwise be fairly good are being ruined by the unreasonable 4emands of labour. Of course, Mr. Naylor is entitled to his view, but if he expresses that view at 4 gathering of working men he must not be surprised if they answer straight, but not quite polite. But liMr. Naylor is not only a poet, a timber merchant and a politician, for he is also a philosopher, and any hostile reception might be met in a manner suggested in another of his verses : There is a language all the same In countries of whatever name That is a smile. Their language may not be your own, And by you never may be known, Yet you can smile. Whether his smile will bl tin sort that won'l, come off remains to be seen.

RENIARRABLE HUNTING ACCIDENT TN TNDTA..

Cantain James D. Graham, Indian Medical Service, 4th Bengal Lancers, quartered at Allahabad, has sent to the "Indian Medical Gazette" an account of a pig- sticking accident which is probably unique. A lieu- tenant who was out pig-sticking Calrie up with the beast which was on his off-side, and stuck it. It swerved across to the near side, and the spear, striking the pony's off shoulder, was wrenched out of the rider's hand and also shaken clear of the pig. It was flung butt to the ground, and the point, rising in the air, penetrated the pony's near shoulder, passed through its chest and ribs, and out at the flap of the saddle; thence it entered the fleshy muscles at the back of the rider's left thigh. nassing out about 61. in. higher up. The pony stopped still, and the lance was extracted after the shaft had been cut. The lieutenant recovered under Captain Graham's care, but the pony died of pneumonia a week later.

ANNUAL MEETING OF THE WARRINGTON ASSOCIATION.

The Compulsory Military Service Proposals.

Evidence of the vitality of the Varrington IVomen's Liberal Association was afforded on Wednesday even- ing by the large attendance at the annual tea and business meeting held at the Liberal Club, Bewsey-street. About eighty members and friends sat down to an excelleni.. oft, and afterwards an adjournment was made to the Reading Rlom, where an interesting and success- ful meeting was held. Hrs. Armitage, the esteemed President of the organization,occupied the chair, and an address was delivered by Miss Ellen Robinson, of Liverpool.

===== 0002601_19031107_art0176.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PRESIDENT'S REMARKS.

Mrs. ARMITIGE pointed out that it was twelve years since the Association was formed. She expressed gratification at the large attendance and said she thought it must be due to the fact that a bazaar was going to be held. The present Government, she con- tanned, had disappointed many of its old supporters, to say nothing of those outside its ranks and had given a stimulus to the energies of the Liberal party. That also was no doubt responsible, in some measure, for the large gathering. (Applause.) ADDRESS BY MISS ROBINSON.

THE QUESTION OF COMPULSORY SERVICE. MISS ROBINSON, in thecourse of an interesting address referred to the municipal elections. One of her neices was canvassing among the women voters and she came back extremely discouraged, because so many women I absolutely refused to vote. They seemed to think it was rather improper for women to vote even in the municipal elections. She did not suppose such a sensible gathering as she saw before her would be guilty of such extreme folly but she wanted them to do their best not only to exercise their own responsibilities in these matters but to etir up the other women voters with whom they might be acquainted. It seemed to be a very strange thing that women should not be interested in ques- tions which affected Corporations and the welfare of thernselvesand their children. The municipal councils had so much to do with education. Was the question of the education of their children nothing to them? Was it nothing to them that parks and gardens should be provided forl the enjoyment of the chil- dren and the health of the neighbourhood; that sanitary ' affairs should be attended to in the town ; that the town should be healthy and well-ordered? She could not imagine how women came to khink that they had .no interest in these matters ; because it was of primary importance to women that the streets and homes should be kept in a sanitary con- dition. She wanted to appeal to the members of that Association to see the extreme necessity of getting right menNinto Corporations. It made all the difference in the world whether they had self-seeking men or men who were perhaps connected with some trade interested in the Corporation. It seemed to her to be a sien'of great stupidity when women refused to vote. 'She was a great advocate for woman's suffrage, and thought they ought to have votes not only for municipal but for Parliamentary elections. It was immensely important that they should return representatives to Parliament who would put the welfare of their country first, and who would be wise statesmen and not mere politicians. The votes which had been exercised in New Zealand and Australia by the women had been very beneficial, and on the whole had had the excellent effect of getting the light people into Parliament. She was rather sorry that party politics had so much to do with municipal affairs. What the, wanted was to have the town properly cared for and well managed. Con- tinuing Miss Robinson said a very important step was taken on October 14th and she regretted that so little notice, comparatively, had been taken of it in the newspapers. She referred to the signing of an arbitra- tion treaty between Englandand France. This was a step forward in the right direction and showed that-the voice of reason and conscience was making itself felt in the world. The question of international arbitration, might seem far away from a woman's gathering in War- rington, and yet it affected every one, of them, more or less, in their relations to humanity,in their whole moral ideal. The treaty between England and France was for five years only—but it was an immense step in advance to get a treaty for even that period,— and it had been decieed that questions which arose between the countries should be referred to the Hague Court instead of being decided by war. Of course, a great dealcombined tolforward the movement. There was the inter-change of visits between the King and Presi- dent Loubet and the hearty welcome each received sliowed how much sympathy existed between the two countries. A large number of 'members of the inter- national arbitration committee in the French Parliament also came to England and were received with open arms, and now the London Commercial Committee had just been visiting Paris. Feelings of friendli- ness and brotherhood mould, it was hoped, be established between the nations. It was a wild folly to look upon the foreigner as an enemy. Such a supposition was the crudest thought of early and barbarous ages and it was madness for us,after all these centuries of civijization to think that a foreigner must be an enemy, She earnestly hoped that the friendship between England and France would not be allowed to evaporate. She was much impressed with the idea that the French people were much ahead of us in this question of arbitration. There was a large party of leading men in France who were very enthusiastic about the Hague Court and arbitration. There was an arbitration party in the French Parlia- ment and she wished there was such a party in the English Parliament. She hoped the members of the Association would influence voters with that end in i view. The majority of the French people were heartily in favour of peace and were delighted that the treaty had been signed, their only regret being that it did not go very much farther. She was very sorry to see from the Warrington pipers that there was almove- ment in favour of increasing the forces of this country, of introducing some modified form of conscription or compulsory military service. She assured them that they could not follow up a policy of peace, friendship and brotherhood with a nation and at the same time be urging on militarism and compulsory service of any kind. One of her strongest objections to Mr. Chamber- lain's—and perhaps they might say the Government's I fiscal policy was that it was not a policy of friendship with other nations. It was a policy of antagonism and looked upon the foreigner as an enemy whose welfare was derogatory to ours, and if he were successful we were to be sorry. It was a great mistake to suppose that the loss of one nation was the gain of another or that the gain of one nation was the loss of another. The more flourishing neighbouring nations were, the more they would be able to trade with us. It should be remembered that in all the European countries there was a large Free Trade party and it would be the great- est pity for us, instead of encouraging that party, to go , back to the Protection that theyhad been fighting such I arbrave battle against for manylyears. She hoped she would not live to see the day when England allowed , compulsion to come into the land in regard to military service. She thought it would be a very sad day for our land. Anyone who knew what conscription was abroad know how it was hated, what a bad feeling it created in the minds of a great many men who were compelled to undergo the training, and what a loss it was to the nation and to the individual. Miss CROSFEELD remarked that she thought that was perfectly true of France where three years was the period of service, but they would not find such a state of things in Germany where they looked forward to th e service as an honour. What had been proposed in Warrington was only two months' training during the year. The object of the proposals was that we might be able to defend our country if necessary, and that would be the surest guarantee of peace. Miss ROBINSON said she was sorry to have to differ, from Miss Crosfleld. Conti mine, she pointed out that in France three years' training Cad to be undergone, and this meant a very great loss to the nation, as young men were away from their work for that period. In. regard to the contention that compulsory military ser- vice gave good physical training, the men who went into Barracks were not as healthy and strong when they came out after two or three years as they were when they entered. It had had a very bad effect upon the physique of young men and also upon their morals. , Moreover, it was really veor great tyranny to compel a 1 man to take up arms to defend his country, if he was conscientiously convinced that it was wrong to kill his fellow-men. In France, many men refused to serve and they were imprisoned in military fortresses, very severely punished, degraded, insulted, and threat- ened in , order to try and compel them to give way. ' There were better ways of serv'tng the country than by having all the young men trai din military arts. In Germany there was a very strong feeling among the Socialists, who had become a very large party, of antag- onism to the militarism in which the country was in- -1 , volved. There was in Germany and in France the 1 opportunity for young men who passed their examination !to have only one year's service instead of three. This, lof course,induced young men to study hard and she I thought iGerman young men were very much better I educated than those in - England. This was

not, in her opinion, entirely ,due to conscription We could have our young men educated without forcing them to compulsory military service. The German militarism was something dreadful. The Germans looked down upon those who were not in the army and some of the young officers were brought up so foolishly that one young man actually committed suicide because he did not wipe out an insult in blood. Militarism had entered into the life of the Germans so that there was little of that freedom which we loved in England. There was a much better • system in Switzerlanxl. Although there was compulsion it was only for a very short time, and there was a law that the soldiers must not cross the frontiers of their own country. If there was such a law in England she did not think we need fear compulsory service so much. But there was no such law, and if they once

===== 0002601_19031107_art0177.txt | included=true | included =====
qrSS, CROSFIELD'S VIEWS.

Miss BESSIE DUTTON proposed a vote of thanks to Miss Robinson, 1 gnd emphasised the inportaace of women exercising their votubrr rights at the municipal elections. It was said that it was not womanly to vote, but it was considered quite womanly to pay rates. (Laughter.) It was something to be proud of that they could exercise their thought and discretion in such important matters. (Applause.) Miss CROSFIELD seconded, and said the address had been highly interesting. She was, however, absolutely convinced, as she was convinced of her own soul, that the proposals of the National Service League were the most necessary that had ever been put forward. She would remind them that compulsion and conscription were two entirely different things. She thought conscription had been a great evil, as Miss Robinson had said, but the National Service League proposed simply compulsory volunteer- ing. That was a very different thing from conscription as it simply meant that men should be trained to be able to defend the nation if ever a time came when it was necessary to do so. A child had to be trained in the way it should go, and had not the nation to have a little training, too? One of the great primary duties was to be able to defend our country and our homes in case of need. To know that England was in an adequate state of defence was the surest guarantee of safety. England was hated on the continent by almost every nation for various reasons and if they saw us lying open and defenceless and chose to come and take us, 50,000 men could have walked from one end of the country to the other while the army was away in South Africa. She did not think a little training in the great grand duty of patriotism could have a bad moral effect on the men. On the contrary it would, in her opinion, have a splen- did effect, because she thought heaps of them needed disciplining a great deal more than they were at pre- sent. She hoped some of those present would be inter- ested in the subject and read the arguments on the other side. She thought if Miss Robinson had heard the argumentsjand read the publications of the League she would have taken a very different view of it. Conscription sounded awful, but these proposals were entirely different. The motion was heartily carried, and in responding Miss Robinson said she was afraid that reading of books would not convince her that the proposals were right.

THE INQUIRY.

Mr, H. Roberts' Cheque Covered.

Mr. Henry Thornton has issued a poster containing 'a copy of a letter which he has addressed to Mr. John Fairhurst, Borough Treasurer. It reads as follows: The Inquiry. To the ratepayers of Warrington. The Laurels, Fearnhead, near Warrington, November sth. Ladies and gentlemen,—Mr. Henry Roberts before the elections on Monday last published a poster in which he suggested to you that £5OO would cover the cost of printing and reporting the inquiry proceedings and he off ered £250 if the Non-Traders would find £250. As a member of the Municipal Non-Trading Associa- tion,' I have sent a cheque for £250 to the Borough Treasurer towards the cost of printing and reporting the proceedings on condition that Mr. Henry Roberts enters into an undertaking with the Borough Treasurer to pay the remainder of the cost of printing and reporting the proceedings of the inquiry as demanded by the Council-nominated Com- missioners. As I wished to avoid the idea that my challenge might influence any ratepayer's vote I decided to wait until after the election before making this offer. I am your obedient servant,HENßY THORNTON." We understand that Councillor James Evans intends to move a resolution at an early meeting of the Council that the inquiry should be conducted in public and the proceedings thrown open to the press.

===== 0002601_19031107_art0182.txt | included=true | included =====
LETTER FROM MR. ROBERTS.- COUNCILLOR THORNTON'S OFFER.

[TO THE EDITOR.] Sir,—After carefully deliberating upon my offer for more than a week, during which the electors have shown their opinion of the discredited and defeated Non-Trading Party, Mr. Thornton has issued a reply. Mr. Thornton (with a delicacy which accords so admir- ably with the methods adopted by his colleagues on the Parr Hall platform and elsewhere) now tells us that for fear "his challenge might influence any ratepayer's vote, he decided to wait until after the elections before making his offer." Your readers will ho doubt accu- ratelyassess the value of Mr. Thornton's disinterested conduct. Mr. Thornton offers to find £250, to add to my £250, on condition that I will "pay the remainder of the cost of printing and reporting the whole of the proceedings of the inquiry.' The Non-Traders have assured us that, this cost will not be less than £B,OOO and may reach £lO,OOO and your readers may depend that, if I was foolish enough to give the unlimited guarantee which Mr. Thornton requests, the Non-trading Commissioners, by their unrestrained chatter, all of which would have to be reported and printed, might, and undoubtedly would force the cost up to the outrageous figure which has been named, seeing that T should have to pay for it. It is therefore highly characteristic of Mr. Thornton's generosity that he should offer to pay £250 on condition that I agree to be responsible for not less than £7,500 to 29,500. ' My original offer I again repeat. I claim to have been falsely accused and I not only desire a full, free and public inquiry, but as evidence of my good faith, I am still prepared to pay not less than £2.50 to procure it. If as the Non-Traders say the public of Warrington demand this inquiry, seeing that the accusations against myself and others have been made upon the platform and in the press, the ratepayers have a perfect right to be present personally and represented by the press, *hen the inquiry into these accusations takes place. What necessity is there for a secret inquiry ?If the inquiry is open to the public and the press, there would not be a charge of one penny incurred for print- ing and publishing the proceedings,and by such a course the interests of tha ratepayers, as well as the interests of the parties, who have been so grossly abused, during the last two years will be equally served. The delay in proceeding withthe inquiry has seriously injured the honour and the material interests of the town, and has become a public scandal, and for this scandal and in- jury, the Non-Traders are solely responsiLle.— Yours faithfully, H. ROBERTS: Kingsley, Hill Cliffe, Nov. 6th 1903.

===== 0002601_19031107_art0183.txt | included=true | included =====
COUNCILLOR DODDS AND THE ELECTIONS.

Position and Outlook .of tha Non-Traders. A WARNING TO LIBERALS.

[To THE EDITOR].

Sir,—.3is the futtire-of the Non-Trading movement is exercising the minds of very many people, will you allow me to briefly state my views of the position and outlook?

—Lest I forget, let me deal with Mr.. A. H. Crosfield who at the Liberal Club the other night demurred to a remark of mine at Parr Hall to the effect that he was opposed to our cause, and claimed to have always been neutral in regard to it. I have observed that in other fields this has often been a convenient attitude on his part, but letting that pass, I find in looking over our correspondence of thirteen months ago that his sole object in approaching me was to prevent opposition by the Non-Traders to the re-election of Liberal Councillors, one of whom has since proved to be about the most insidious of our opponents. I also find that in the course of the corres- pondence he referred in a disparaging way to the num- ber and influence of our supporters. This hardly indicated frendliness or indeed neutrality, and I think I may still fairly claim (at any rate under an old dictum) that "he that is not with us is against as " ; however neither Mr. Crosfield's attitude nor that of the other leaders of the Liberal party matters "one straw " and we are quite indifferent so far as our ultimate suc- cess is concerned as to which side of the fence they finally come down upon. And let me in this connection warn my fellow members 'of the Liberal Club that such rejoicings as some of them indulged in on the narrow retention of the Orford seat, may one of these fine days come home to roost, for not a single circumstance of that contest is creditable to the leaders of the Liberal party, the opposi- tion to a representative Labour Candidate, the now notorious and more than questionable methods employed to secure his defeat, are surely such as no true Liberal can look back upon without shame and remorse. lam not going to defend the Non-Trading Committee, who, thinking, no doubt, that some of Mr. Stott's views would militate against his defeating the Liberal trading apologist, supported another candidate against my urgent advice, and with disastrous results to the common cause. So far as the future is concerned, however, I hope and believe any similar action will never be repeated. What I have stated and reiterated for two years past at almost every meeting we have 'held, needs to ever clearly borne in mind.

===== 0002601_19031107_art0184.txt | included=true | included =====
OUR AIM AND OBJECT

are to abolish trading, to see that men of our views on this question are elected, whatever their views on other subjects may be, and to remove from the Council all those who have either traded themselves or allowed others to do so while they have silently acquiesced. We, of course, desire efficiency on the part of both members of the Council and officials; we insist that the people shall receive 20s. in value for every 203. of their money which is spent; this will not be the case so long as (1) members are themselves supplying material to their own Council, and (2) so long as such materials have to be "passed" by officials who depend upon the Council for their livelihood.

We desire a full inquiry into the last seven years transactions of the council, but hold the opinion that while the inquiry should be thorough, no expenditure should be incurred which can be avoided without detri- ment to its completeness. The ratepayers desire to be informed as to how, when, and through whom, the profit of their Council's trading has passed into the pockets of elected, and therefore interested and biassed persons, but the ratepayers are not greatly concerned with the preliminary research which is necessary in order to enable the Commissioners to decide as to what evidence and what witnesses they should call. . . . .

We have neither the intention nor the desire to create a new party in the Council, nor to show any pre- ference whatever to Conservative, Liberal, or Labour Candidates as such and it is not a matter of concern to the Non-Trading Association as to what may be the political. or other complexion of the Cauncil from year to year, nor as to who shall be Alderman or Mayor. None of these things move the Association; all nat they aim at is to eliminate the elements of rivate gain from all thci transactions of the Council, L! to to fay nir th 3 candidature of only such candidates in th 3 nseivas to oppose and to openly denounce and votl at; Oast all trading, direct or indirect, by mem- bers of the Council.

of this I ha7o lnd ample evidence since the applrent b Lckwash of Nflniay last. The retirement of the chief trading offender twelve months ago, and of his brothers the other day; the carrying of the inquiry resolutions; the running of Non-Trading candidates who, although of high character and ability were almost unknown; with practically no organisa- tions, the profession of all the opposition candidates but oneof non-trading views, supplemented by over con- fidence,led many of our friends not to vote at all, and others to vote (without due reference to thereat demand of our cause)for men they actually knew and whom a they considered to be Non-Traders._

The occasional drooping of our flg wa-4 anticipltel by me in Parr Hall a year ago; from the action of cross currents the full consistency of the breeze is not alway 3 such as to sustain it at full stretch, but tempor- ary lulls such as that of Monday will bring more than an equivalent antidote. For it need only be known that

MR. HENRY ROBERTS

has said that he has been so encouraged by Monday's occurrence that it his intention to immediately seek to re-enter the Council, and we should see the town rise as one man to prevent his doing so as a trader. That the traders will never cease to seek to get back to the Council I am convinced.On any apparent loss of ground on our part they will be encouraged to try again. Let the ratepayers of Warrington learn a lesson from the the success of Tammany and all that that means in New York this week. At the previous election two years ago the New York purity party had a magnificent vic- tory, but from over confidence this year, and the desperate efforts ofTammany the good cause has suffered defeat. As Gladstone wrote to his son "after all the great thing is to be right"; the people know they are right—that they will ever desert a righteous cause I will never believe.—Yours Bcc.,

LIBERAL AND LABOUR.

A. Plea for a Better Understanding.

The present controversy, as to the claims labour has on the sympathy of the electoral community, is one that demands the earnest consideration of those fair-minded individuals who stand for progress in politics. The rapid growth of intellect has made the masses decidedly hostile to any apparent unfairness in the distribution of this • world's wealth, and " riotous-living "—supported by their hard-earned wages—is in future to be checked by combined effort. There can be no doubt, r,think, that the existing flag- rant examples of one-sided justice are the cause of the present rebellion ; and until taxation is fairly ad- justed and laws rightly administered there will be just reason for co-operative complaint. The form the present re-action is to take now con- cerns us, and we Would do well to glean some infor- mation as to the main features of the Labour Move- ment, and the objects it has in view, as it appears to those of us who cannot be classified as official Labour-ers

INDEPENDENT LABOUR PARTY

There are apparently two orgAnizati ms, and I take the following facts from leaflets issued by either one or other of them. We have the .Independent Labour Party or Socialists, and the Trade Unionists. For a moment let us confine our attention to the Inde- pendent Labour Party which WAS established in 1893. We are told that only avowed Socialists are admitted to membership, and its object is_ to establish "an industrial commonwealth founded upon Socialisation of land and capital." Its desire is to see enterprise, both municipal and State, extended until everfr industry has been brought under public ownership and control.

The Socialists originally began by proposing militant organization of the working classes and general insurrection, but that proposal proved impracticable and it has been abandoned—not without some out- spoken regrets—by English Socialists. It has mem- bers in Parliament,on the Town and Comity Councils, Boards of Guardians, and other elective bodies—a record surely unbroken in an industrial effort of only ten years' standing.

TR,illE UNIONISTS

The feature of a Trade Union is to protect the interest of its members, to get them higher wages and better conditions. It is really to safeguard the men in al! matters appertaining to workers as against employers when any dispute arises as to wages, &c., and the funds of the society, collected from its mem- bers, are to be used in case of strikes or other emerg- ency. In future, however, they are taking up

"political work" because they see that it "is going' to be less expensive and far more effective than the strike." We are told that "the Trade Union candi- dates will not all be Socialists, but they will support. such Socialist proposals, as nationalization of the land, mines, and railways, old-age pensions, better housing of the people, and the like." Allied also with the Trade Unionists are " The Co-operative Societies." which have a large and wealthy membership. Their vote, too, is to be recorded in favour of Labour.

===== 0002601_19031107_art0187.txt | included=true | included =====
WHERE ARE WE?

Now then where are we? It appears to us from the foregoing that if a candidate for Parliamentary honours is willing to support 'such proposals as those mentioned, viz : nationalisation of thn land,. mines and railways, old-age pensions, better -.housing for the people and similar benefits, and is, in other ways, a suitable person to occupy that respected and exalted position, then he ought to receive the support of b )th the Labour-man and the Trade Unionist, irrespective of Party. Will the Conservative candidate for Warrington, whoever he may be,—and rumour has it that we are likely to see a change support such proposals ? I think not • because as 3 party, "the Conservative party has gra:dualiy drifted Into supporting interests that are antagonistic to such• proposals and cannot enter into such pressina ques- tions without discirganizing its own party mac'hinery by frightening away powerful supporters upon whont it relies for financial and other assistance."

IVIIAT MEN ? If the movement for the bettertwat of the working: classes is to succeed with anything like the true measure of success it deserves, there . will have to be a little "giving and taking." No arbitrary expulsion of party will help it. Tie public go xi will have to stand in the frout o its-'platform. Aqd. I contend that to adequately represent the masses', attention wil/ have to be paid to the interests of those working men. who are not membars of either of the said Societies. There are plenty of electors who are in deep sympathy with the objects in view, but have not allied themselves—for various reasons—with the Labour movement. What of the "middle- class man" so-called—the rain who, because he 'doesn't take off his coat to work, is told that he doesn't wJrk? He cannot, probably, accept the teachings altogether of the Socialists, nor can he become a member of a Trade Union, for obvious reasons. Is he to be coerced into joining one or other of the organisations ? Consideration, at least, must be shewn to others.

===== 0002601_19031107_art0188.txt | included=true | included =====
LIBERAL AND LABOUR

Liberals contend that their desire is to see all these things put right,and in Warrington,at any rate, Liberalism d 3es not stand for anything if not for the. welfare of the working classes.And,l should like to say' en parsant in reply to a sneer at a recent meeting, that another election would have been won for Liberalism in Warrington before now if the working men haa really recognised that fact and had not been misled by electioneering experts. The time is now at hand when the position of the Labour party must be. made known. The Liberals have had in Mr. A. H. Oros-. field a candidate who, though unsuccessful for the reason already named, has always shown himself to be in sympathy with the sobjects of the Labour move- ment. He has done his utmost to win the seat for them when times were bad. Surely, now that there is "a good time coming" and there is an excellent chance of success, he is not going to be thrown over A thousand times ne ! Let there be such a rallyinx together of Liberals and Labour for the cause they have so much at heart that victory cannot be denied them. United they will win, divided they will assuredly lose.

IV CONCLUSION.

In my " impression" of the recent Labour meet- ing held in the Parr Hall, Mr. J. Podmore took:excep— tion to a remark of mine made as to the " revolu- tionary character of the debates " in the local Trades Council. My reason for stating that is founded on, fact. Perhaps he will tell us why the Ironworkers Association withdrew their members from the Trades. Council meeting? Was it not from the very reason I mentioned?

I do not desire, for a moment, to misrepresent the, Trade's Council. I realise, from what I know of it, that, rightly conducted, it can be made a very useful- institution. My only contention is that if it is going tO. represent the people there must be no boycotting. The term " Labour " will have to be • more clearly defined. Its portals will have to be open to those with like desires, but who have, unfortunately, been born "with a silver spoon in their mouths." There must be no judging of a man because he wears a better coat than his neighbour. His position may demand it thlligh his wages be the same. Unless we give and take freely with our fellows in talk and correspondence and in our efforts to benefit mankind, our methods and learning are al4 togrow obsolete and a seltiih niture be the result. But in the giving and the taking, the hard soil is broken up and light and air prepare it for the seed which, rightly sown and at the proper time, will bring forth an abundant harvest. There will be tares, probably. The Liberal Unionists can find room for them. EDMUND OSBORNE. Nov. 5, 1903.

CAT SUFFOCATES AN INFANT.

At an inquest held at Hartlepool on Monday night concerning the death of a five-months-old child, twat daughter of Joseph Dowe, blacksmith, evidence showed that early on Sunday morning a cat was discovered lying on the baby's face, and when it was removed the child was dead. A verdict was returned that the child died of suffocation caused by the cat lying on its face.

article of food which may be known to some people, and is useful t:-., all. If a dozen eggs of various degrees of freshness are placed in a basin of water, it can at once be seen which are the newly laid, which stale, and • : which may be regarded as comparatively fresh. The • ! newly laid will lie flat in the basin, the stale will stand I on end, and the rest will indicate their age by the ex- tent to which they rise from the horizontal. This is • not one of the fallacious "hints to housewives "of • which so many are printed. MADGE. , . WARRINGTON RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL. The Longford Depot Nuisance. • The monthly meeting of this Council was held at • Bewsey Chambers on Wednesday afternoon, Mr. T. Halton presiding. There were also present :—Dr. Murray, Messrs. Brooks, Houghton, Forshaw, Ball, iLongton, Mather with Dr. Sephton (medical officer of health), Messrs. J. C. Sutton (clerk), S. Sutton I(assistant clerk). F. Kerfoot (inspector of nuisances), B. S. Newcombe (surveyor), and H. H. Burnage (assistant surveyor.) 4 LONGFORD DEPOT. ' The CLERK read a letter from Mr. Joseph Smith, clerk to the Sankey Parish Council,stating that at a meet jug of that authority attention was drawn to the pollu- • tion of Sankey Brook by the discharge from Longford Depot and it was resolved that the Clerk write to the Rural District Council again urging them to take immediate steps to obtain an abatement of the nuisance. He (Mr. Sutton) instructed the inspector to make in- quiries as to the cause of complaint when he visited the 1 depot. The INSPECTOR reported that no further alterations had been carried out with the exception that one of the I dryers had been connected up. He was given to under- stand that tenders were being invited for the experi- ments about to be carried out. With reference to the 1 effluent no crude excreta' was turned into Sankey Brook. The CHAIRMAN: No. That conclusion was formed from a remark made at the last Council meeting. The INSPECTOR : It is simply the waste effluent from the tanks—about 1,500 gallons a week., Mr. FORSHAW said it was time something was done to abate the nuisance. Could not the council apply for ian injunction? . I The CHAIRMAN said they had the power to do so but 'at the last meeting it was decided to leave the matter in abeyance te see if there was any improvement when the new machinery was in operation. ItMr. LONGTON : They have been telling us about new machinery for over ten years, and there has never been any improvement—in fact, it goes worse. Mr. BROOKS: They are making a move this time. The CHAIRMAN: I think we may give them another Naonth. If there is nothing done, we can move in the matter. This course was agreed to. HEALTH OF THE DISTRICT. Dr. SEPHTON reparted that during the month thera had been 20 births and 35 deaths. The death rate was equal to 37.8 per 1,000 against 33 the average for the past three months and 23 for the corresponding month last year. Twenty-nine deaths occurred in the County Asylum and exclusive of these the death rate proper for the district was 6.5 per 1,000. The epidemic of measles had extended from Warrington to Orford and had affected the attendance of children at Orford Schools. He recommended that the schools be closed for three weeks. The report was adopted. THE MOTOR CAR ACT. . The SURVEYOR said the Clerk had handed to him a letter from the Clerk to the County Council and state- ment forms to be filled with particulars of narrow or dingerous highways where the District Council might consider it desirable that the speed of motor cars should -, be'restricted. i The SURVEYOR was directed to obtain the particulars Irequired in consultation with the Councillor for each parish. THE COUNCIL'S BYELA WS. - A Committee consisting of Dr. Murray, Messrs. Halton, Brooks, S. Sutton, Newcombe and Kerfoot was appointed to consider the building byelaws with a view to revision. • IMPROVEMENTS. The Council approved recommendations of the com- mittee for improvements involving an expenditure of over £1,500. One of the most important schemes is the general improvement at Hollins-lane, Winwick at a cost of £290. This was all the business of public interest. ge. THE WRITER OF OUR SPECIAL. Mr. John Fletcher Moulton, K. C. M. P., who con- tributes to our columns a special article on the Land Question this week, comes of a family of scholars. His father and grandfather were Wesleyan ministers. His brother was president of the Wesleyan Conference and Headmaster of Lays School,' Cambridge, while another brother is Professor.of English Literature at Chicago University. Mr. Fletcher Moulton himself had a most brilliant , scholastic career. He was educated at 4 Kingswood School, and proceeded to St. John's College,Cambridge, where he carried everything before him. He was Senior Wrangler, Smith's Prizeman. Gold Medallist of London University, and in 1868 became a Fellow of Christ's College, Cambridge. In 1874 he was called to the Bar, and in 1885 he was elected to Parliament for Clapham. The same year he became Queen's Counsel, and for a time was Alderman of the London County Council. In 1894 he sat again in Parliament for South Hackney, and in 1898 he was elected for Launceston in Cornwall, which division he has since continued to represent. Mr. Moulton is also an officer of the French Legion of Honour, and his knowledge of science and mathe- matics gives him an unrivalled position in dealing with tthe legal aspects of patents.

PENKETH. • WESLEYAN MUTUAL IMPROVNAI ENT ASSOCIATION.-- The weekly meeting of the above society in the VieB; leyan Schoolroom, was a signally successful one. the large audience regretted not their attendance, 'Pit paper for the evrning was taken by the Rey. The Medical Officer of Health reported cases of scarlet fever at Hill Clifie, Acton Grange, Grappenhall and Stockton Heath, and cases of chicken-pox at Grappenhall and Thelwall. Admiral Parry asked several questions in regard to the fee of 2s. 6d. paid to medical men for the notifica- tion of cases of infectious disease. The Clerk informed him that doctors were allowed that fee by an Act of Parliament which did not specify that the sum should be paid to anyone else who notified cases "HOW TO TAKE OUT A PATENT." We have received an interesting pamphlet on patents and kindred forms of Protection. This form of mono- poly is the oldest in existence and whatever may be the opinions of our readers at the present fiscal crisis there can be no doubt that patents for inventions have done and are still doing very much to foster inventive in- dustr2; as much for the good of the state as for the in- ventor himself. This pamphlet has already been favour- ably reviewed by London and provincial papers and we have no hesitation in adding our testimony to the large amount of useful information that it contains in com- pressed form. The author, Mr. Charles Coventry, C.E., of Liverpool, is a well-known expert in such matters as is amply proved by the contents of the book, of which copies may be had free from him on application. Further particulars may be seen on our front page.
